Title: Per-Query Recall Guarantees for Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search in Dynamic Vector Databases

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07579v1

Published Time: Fri, 06 Nov 2026 02:15:56 GMT

Number of Pages: 66

Markdown Content:
# Per-Query Recall Guarantees for Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search in Dynamic Vector Databases 

Woong-Kee Loh Suan Lee Gachon University Semyung University Seongnam, Republic of Korea Jecheon, Republic of Korea 

wkloh2@gachon.ac.kr suanlee@semyung.ac.kr 

This is the extended version of the paper submitted to ICDE 2027. It contains the complete proofs, the experiments omitted from the conference version, and supplementary results. This work has been submitted to the IEEE for possible publication. Copyright may be transferred without notice, after which this version may no longer be accessible. 

# Abstract 

Vector search now runs as an operator inside database systems, where a missed neighbor makes the answer of a filter, join, or aggregate over its results wrong, yet approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) indexes are tuned only so that their 

average recall reaches a target, and the average says nothing about an individual query. In our measurements, indexes tuned to an average recall of 0.99 left 5–19% of in-distribution queries and, tuned without a query log, up to 99.6% of real out-of-distribution queries below this target, and the misses grew under insertions and deletions. Existing guarantees based on conformal prediction bound only a risk averaged over the query distribution. In the spirit of approximate query processing (AQP), which attaches to each answer an error bound that holds with a stated probability, we present PQR-Search (per-query recall search), a k-ANN search method that guarantees Pr[Recall( q) < τ ] ≤ δ for every given query. The probability is taken only over a random orthogonal transformation inside the index, drawn independently of data and queries, so no distributional assumption is needed, only that queries and updates do not depend on it (its seed is kept secret from whoever issues them), and for τ < 1 the same run also satisfies E[Recall( q)] ≥ τ under a mild condition. When updates are independent of the transformation, the guarantee holds after insertions and deletions without retuning and without splitting δ between old and new parts of the index. To provide the guarantee at low cost, PQR-Search stores 1-bit codes of rotated residuals with respect to IVF centroids and prunes with exact quantiles of their error distribution. We derive a formula that predicts the fraction of vectors passing the code test without data-fitted constants and without reading the transformation, and use it to choose index parameters and maintenance operations without affecting the guarantee. On 20 datasets of up to 100 million vectors, failure rates stayed at most δ in every audit, including all 33 search steps of the official NeurIPS’23 streaming runbook without retuning, whereas CTR, a distribution-level guarantee, left up to 16.4% of queries below the same target recall. The guarantee is affordable. Where HNSW tuned to an average recall of 0.99 met that target, PQR-Search had 2.5 × its single-query latency and 1.3 × lower batch throughput (geometric means), yet that HNSW still left 3–29% of queries below 0.99, and indexes tuned to an average recall of 0.99 changed the answer of a top-5-tag aggregate over the results for up to 27% of queries, versus at most 0.04% of trials for PQR-Search. PQR-Search was faster than FAISS exhaustive search on CPU, which also meets the guarantee, in every evaluated static setting (geometric mean 26.4 × in batch throughput; 69–154 × at 100M scale). In all 52 main settings it was also faster than IVF whose nprobe was chosen afterwards so that every test query met the target, because a fixed nprobe must serve the hardest query while the work of PQR-Search adapts to each query. It was 27–76 × faster at one thread than PUFFINN, which also gives per-query guarantees. 

# 1. Introduction 

With the advance of deep neural networks, it has become common to represent unstructured data such as text, images, and audio as high-dimensional embedding vectors [1], [2]. Accordingly, k-nearest neighbor ( k-NN) search, which finds the vectors close to a query vector in a vector database, has become a core operation of many applications such as information retrieval [3], recommendation [4], and retrieval-augmented generation [5]. However, exact k-NN search on large-scale high-dimensional data is very expensive, and even classical indexes such as pivot-based indexes and metric trees are known to face lower bounds stating that they must examine most of the data in high dimensions [6], [7]. For this reason, practice widely relies on approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) search, which gives up some accuracy in exchange for a large reduction in latency [2], [8]. Graph-based indexes [9], [10], partition-based indexes such as IVF [8], [11], and quantization-based methods [12], [13], [14] have achieved both high recall and low latency on real data. The search accuracy of these methods is usually set by tuning parameters such as nprobe or efSearch until the average recall reaches a target, and the chosen value is applied equally to every query [15], [16], [17]. A high average recall does not mean that the result of an individual query is accurate. Benchmark queries tend to be easier than real workloads [18], and since the average is determined by such queries, the target can be met even if some 1arXiv:2611.07579v1 [cs.DB] 5 Nov 2026 hard queries have low recall. In our measurements, IVF-Flat, HNSW, and IVF-RaBitQ indexes tuned to an average recall of 0.99 met the target average recall on in-distribution queries, yet at k = 100, 5.0–19.2% of the queries had recall below 0.99 (Fig. 1(a), Section 2.1). It has also been reported that 4.8% of the queries had recall 0 on DiskANN with an average Recall@10 of 0.9 [19]. When the distribution of queries differs from that of the data, the performance of ANN indexes drops sharply [1], [20], [21]. In the same measurements, when queries that search for images by text or real user questions were sent to indexes tuned with data vectors in the absence of a query log, the fraction of queries with recall below 0.99 was 51.4–99.6% at k = 100 (Fig. 1(a), Section 2.1). How much the recall of an individual query matters depends on the application. In applications where the returned results are interchangeable, such as retrieval-augmented generation and recommendation, missing some neighbors does not significantly degrade downstream quality [4], [22], [23], [24]. In other applications, however, a single missed neighbor is itself an error, and the average recall is not sufficient. For example, in image copy detection, a missed copy keeps circulating. Copy detection is performed by matching learned image descriptors [25], [26] with ANN search, and a large-scale service processing billions of images per day [27] explicitly aims to increase recall while maintaining precision [28]. The same holds for vector databases and vector extensions of relational DBMSs. These systems provide vector search as an operation of query processing [29], [30], [31], [32], and its result set becomes the input of filters, joins, and aggregates, so a missed neighbor leads to an error in the final query result. In our measurements, tag aggregates over the results of indexes tuned to an average recall of 0.99 differed from those over exact results for up to 27% of queries (Section 6.6). However, these systems only let users specify a target accuracy or search parameters and give no guarantee on the result of an individual query [29], [30], [31]. Moreover, in searches where the cost of a missed document is high, such as legal document review and systematic literature reviews, the goal is recall as close to 100% as possible [33]. Approximate query processing (AQP) has met the same need for aggregates by attaching to each answer an error bound that holds with a stated probability over sampling [34], [35], [36]. Vector operators lack such a contract: methods that give per-query guarantees in vector search are rare, and even these are limited to per-neighbor probabilities, that is, the expected recall, or to a single nearest neighbor (Section 2.3). The problem grows in environments where data are continuously updated. The data are not fixed in the applications mentioned above either. Copy detection systems continuously inspect newly uploaded images, and misinformation newly verified by fact-checking organizations as well as new variants found by the system again become subjects of matching and review [28]. In a DBMS, vector columns are inserted, modified, and deleted like any other column, and Oracle advises checking the accuracy and rebuilding the index because such updates can degrade the accuracy of IVF indexes over time [30]. In applications where documents are constantly added, modified, and deleted, such as Wikipedia search, the distributions of queries and updates also change over time, and such dynamic workloads increase the latency of the index and degrade recall [37]. Accordingly, methods that maintain index quality under updates have been studied, such as graph repair [38], splitting and merging of partitions [37], [39], and local reclustering according to reconstruction error [40]. However, as Quake [37] pointed out, once the index structure changes, the initially chosen parameters cannot maintain the same recall. In our measurements as well, when five steps of 10% insertions and deletions were applied to an index initially tuned to an average recall of 0.99 on yandex, the fraction of queries with recall below 0.99 rose from 13.5% to 22.3% for IVF-Flat, and from 7.9% to 17.5% even for Quake [37], which takes a recall target directly ( k = 100, Fig. 1(b), Section 2.1). Retuning the parameters is costly because it again requires calibration queries with ground truth [15], [16], [17]. Research on guaranteeing the accuracy of search results has also been conducted. RaBitQ [14] used a random trans-formation to provide probabilistic error bounds for each individual distance estimate, and this paper starts from this result. ADSampling [41] turns error bounds of random projections into a high-probability guarantee of returning the 

k nearest candidates, which becomes a guarantee of exact search when all vectors are candidates. Its upper bound, however, contains a constant of unknown value, and it targets neither recall below exactness nor updates. ConANN [42] and CTR [43] presented distribution-level guarantees on recall using conformal prediction. Their guarantees, however, concern a risk averaged over the query distribution, and, as the authors of ConANN noted, per-query conditional guar-antees cannot be obtained by conformal methods [42], [44]. In our measurements, in 27 settings in which CTR was run with its own loss target, up to 16.4% of the queries had recall below the target τ , which its averaged guarantee permits (Fig. 1(c), Section 6.3). Per-query probabilistic guarantees have been given by PUFFINN [45], which scans an LSH index and stops once each true neighbor has been found with probability at least 1 − δ; by Confirmation Sampling [46], which repeatedly queries LSH data structures to find the exact nearest neighbor of each query with probability 1 − δ;and by TPU-KNN [47], which computes all distances and then meets a target expected recall under an assumption on the placement of the neighbors. Among LSH methods from the database community, SRS [48], VHP [49], and R2LSH [50] also return the exact nearest neighbor of each query with any given probability over data-independent random projections. However, PUFFINN and TPU-KNN bound each true neighbor’s miss probability, that is, the per-query expected recall, from which Markov’s inequality gives tail bounds on recall. PUFFINN is limited to cosine and Jaccard similarity and does not support deletion, and its public implementation, in the setting covered by its proof, was 27–76 × slower than our method at one thread (Appendix C.3). Confirmation Sampling, SRS, VHP, and R2LSH address a single nearest neighbor ( k = 1), and TPU-KNN presupposes computing all distances. Moreover, to the best of our knowledge, none of these guarantees was analyzed under updates (SRS, VHP, and R2LSH support updates 2through tree indexes but state no guarantee under them), and the dynamic index studies mentioned above [37], [38], [39], [40] were evaluated by average recall (Section 2.3). This paper addresses the problem of k-ANN search that guarantees per-query recall in dynamic vector databases. Given a target recall τ ∈ (0 , 1] and a failure probability δ, the goal is to guarantee Pr[Recall( q) < τ ] ≤ δ for every given query without assumptions on the data or the query distribution, provided only that the queries and updates do not depend on the randomness inside the index, whose seed is kept secret from whoever issues them; we call this the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee . Exact search ( τ = 1) is a special case of it. We also guarantee E[Recall( q)] ≥ τ for every query, which we call the expected-recall guarantee . In both guarantees, the probability and the expectation are taken not over multiple queries but, for a given single query, over the randomness inside the index. Hence the expected-recall guarantee ensures for each individual query the goal of conventional tuning, which brought the average recall over many queries to at least τ . In applications where missing some neighbors is acceptable, such as retrieval-augmented generation and recommendation, it suffices that the recall of each query reaches the target on average, so the expected-recall guarantee meets that requirement at lower cost than the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee. These guarantees must hold without retuning while insertions and deletions continue. To give a per-query guarantee, the probability of missing true neighbors remaining in unvisited regions must be bounded, but methods that do not inspect unvisited regions, such as graph traversal, cannot directly count the neighbors remaining in those regions. Re-ranking an HNSW result with exact distances removes false neighbors but cannot reveal missed ones, since finding them requires examining the unvisited vectors. To solve this problem, we propose PQR-Search (per-query recall search), an index and search method that defines the probability of the guarantee not over the query distribution but over randomness inside the index. Its index applies a random orthogonal transformation to vectors and stores them as 1-bit binary codes. Given a query, it first computes the exact distances to some vectors and uses the k-th of these distances as the pruning radius, progressively tests with codes that the remaining vectors cannot lie within this radius and prunes them, and then re-ranks only the remaining vectors by exact distances. Whenever an exact distance is computed during the test, the radius is shrunk to prune more vectors. Under a random orthogonal transformation, the test error follows a data-independent distribution [14], so an upper bound on the probability of missing a true neighbor can be computed for the given data and query. The premise needed for this guarantee is that the queries, the updates, and the construction of the index are independent of the orthogonal transformation, so the same guarantee holds after updates without retuning. What is encoded here is not the vector itself but its residual with respect to the centroid of an IVF partition, and the vectors whose exact distances are computed first are also chosen from partitions whose centroids are close to the query. Since the error of a distance estimated from a 1-bit code is proportional to the magnitude of the residual [14], the smaller the residual, the more accurate the test, and residual norms give deterministic bounds via the triangle inequality [51], [52]. Why this design provides the same guarantee at lower cost than exhaustive search is intuitive. To determine the result, most vectors need not the value of their distance but only a test showing that they cannot lie within the pruning radius; for vectors far from the radius this test is settled by a binary code of tens of bytes, so the original vectors are read to compute distances only for the few vectors that pass the test (Section 3.3). This design also favors high-dimensional data. The cost of testing unvisited vectors, as with scanning compressed codes such as PQ [12], is determined not by the dimension of the data but by the number of bits of the binary code, so for data of higher dimension, such as embedding vectors [1], the gain over exhaustive search, which computes all distances, when queries are processed one at a time actually becomes larger. In our measurements, with the first-stage code length fixed, this speedup grew from 19.9–24.6 × when the same data were reduced to 128 dimensions to 40.4–62.6 × at the original dimensions (768–1024) (Section 6.6). The design also favors updates. As updates continue, vectors drift away from the centroids to which they are assigned and the search cost increases, so a dynamic index maintains an efficient index, that is, an index with low search cost, by moving centroids or splitting partitions. Since insertion, deletion, and maintenance all take place per partition, an update affects only part of the index, and the conditions under which the guarantee holds during updates are simple. The main contributions of this paper are as follows. • A per-query contract for vector operators (Sections 2.2, 4.3, 5). We show by measurement that average-recall tuning and distribution-level guarantees based on conformal prediction cannot protect individual queries, and, like an AQP error bound, we define as the problem a ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee that is stated before execution and holds for every fixed dataset and query. The guarantee covers true neighbors in unvisited regions as well, and since its only randomness is the random orthogonal transformation P inside the index, it holds for out-of-distribution queries too, and any workload chosen without looking at P expects at most a δ fraction of failing queries. The principle that keeps this guarantee cheap is to let the guarantee come from P alone, to make the decisions that affect the guarantee (the stage configuration, maintenance operations, and their timing) by predictions that do not read P , and to confine the choices that may depend on P (the test order and early exact computation) to what Theorem 5 permits. Decisions based on values that depend on P , such as measured pass counts, introduce selection bias; this principle is therefore what keeps the guarantee intact without retuning, even under updates. 3• Per-query recall guarantees with exact quantiles (Sections 3.1, 3.2, Appendices A.3, B). For search that tests unvisited vectors with prefix-readable binary codes of rotated residuals, we show that the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee holds for every given query without assumptions on the data or query distribution, for queries and updates that do not depend on the index’s internal randomness, and that for τ < 1 the same run also satisfies the expected-recall guarantee under a mild condition. The guarantee theorems themselves are short, because they build on the error identity of RaBitQ [14] and the union bound over the k true neighbors of ADSampling [41]. What is new is the condition under which a radius set from exact distances of visited vectors also applies to unvisited true neighbors; the exact error distributions of prefix codes and 2-bit codes, stated for any code that is a function of P −1o, which give error-bound parameters from exact Beta quantiles instead of an unspecified constant (with these quantiles alone, even a RaBitQ full scan becomes a guaranteed search, 12.1–20.5 × faster than FAISS exhaustive search in geometric mean; Section 6.3); the adaptive radius (Theorem 5), with which we make explicit that the guarantee survives a radius that shrinks during testing although it depends on P , also with per-thread radii and stale radii; and the soundness of the implementation including floating-point error (Section 4.2, Appendix A.3). ADSampling’s per-comparison argument uses only that the distance is within the threshold, so it already tolerates such a dependence, but it does not discuss it, and its verification avoided it with a fixed threshold [41]. Moreover, for analyses that use only per-neighbor probabilities, the Markov step of the bound is tight; it is attained exactly only by a placement of exact duplicates with k = m + 1 (Proposition 1). Separately from the Markov step, we give lower bounds on cost: in a probe model, a per-query guarantee must probe at least a 1 − δ1/(m+1) fraction of the hidden cells in expectation, and a deterministic rule all but m of them (Theorems 7 and 8), and under randomized SETH no data structure with polynomial preprocessing can give such guarantees in query time n1−ϵ on worst-case inputs with D = ω(log n) (Theorem 9 in Appendix B). • Guarantees under updates (Section 5). We extend the index and search algorithm of Section 4 to dynamic environments by adding only a delta segment that holds new vectors separately, a bitmap that marks deletions, and merges. An insertion consists of encoding one vector and rearranging the delta region of its partition, and a deletion of marking one bit; unlike graph indexes, no connections need to be repaired. We show that if the updates and the decisions for maintaining an efficient index are independent of the orthogonal transformation, the static guarantee holds for a query at any time without retuning and without splitting the failure probability 

δ among segments. We also show that if the same orthogonal transformation keeps being used, the failure of a query can persist to later times, and that a fresh orthogonal transformation at merge time breaks this persistence, and we analyze the risk of adaptive updates. The same frame covers filtered k-NN: marking the vectors that fail a predicate in the deletion bitmap keeps the guarantee against the true neighbors among the qualifying vectors for any selectivity (Corollary 2). As a result, the decisions for maintaining an efficient index do not affect the guarantee and change only the search cost, and we determine them with the formula of Section 3.3. • Residual cost law (Section 3.3). We derive a formula that predicts the pass rate of the first-stage test, which sets the cost of the same guarantee, without data-fitted constants and without reading the orthogonal transformation. The formula shows that the cost is determined by the residual scale and the test radius; it thus answers quantitatively the normalization (centroid) choice that RaBitQ [14] left as future work, and it grounds the residual codes and the index design. Following the principle above, the formula sets the parameters of the index (Section 4.3) and, in dynamic settings, the operations that keep the index efficient and their timing (Section 5) without retuning. • Index and search (Section 4). Following the theory, we design an IVF index and a query processing algorithm and implement them with AVX-512. Unlike standard IVF, vectors in unvisited partitions are also tested, so the guarantee holds regardless of nprobe , and queries are processed fast with SIMD tests and multithreading within what the theorems of Section 3 permit. The parameter selection rule based on the cost law changes only the query processing cost, so the guarantee is unchanged even if the distributions of calibration queries and actual queries differ. • Evaluation (Section 6). We validated each contribution on 20 datasets (up to 100M points) reflecting the applications above (copy detection, cross-modal search, document retrieval, and database operators) and on the official NeurIPS’23 streaming runbook [53]. Failure rates of the per-query guarantees stayed at most δ in every audit. In 168 static settings the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee failed 45 times out of 4,176,000 (at most 0.5% of δ in each configuration), and, unlike CTR, which gives a distribution-level guarantee at the same target, no query had an average recall over orthogonal transformations significantly below the target. The price of the guarantee was modest. Where HNSW32 tuned to an average recall of 0.99 met the target on calibration queries, PQR-Search had 2.5 × its 1-thread latency and HNSW32 had 1.3 × higher batch throughput (geometric means), yet HNSW32 still left 3.3–29.2% of test queries below recall 0.99. Tuned instead to the tail target (at most 1% of calibration queries below recall 0.99), HNSW32 reached it within its search grid in only 10 of 18 settings and was there 1.1 ×

(1-thread) and 1.9 × (batch) slower than PQR-Search, whereas at a target of 0.9 HNSW32 tuned post hoc on the test queries was 2.0 × faster at 1 thread. Widening the tail-tuned baselines on out-of-distribution queries to HNSW with M up to 64 and efSearch up to 16,384, DiskANN, RoarGraph, and multi-bit RaBitQ kept PQR-Search 2.9 × (1-thread) and 5.3 × (batch) faster than the fastest baseline reaching 0.99, whereas on two 100M 4SIFT GIST arxiv yandex laion hotpotqa Dataset 025 50 75 100 % of queries with recall < 0.99 (a) IVF-Flat HNSW32 IVF-RaBitQ 0 1 2 3 4 5Update step 020 40 60 % of queries with recall < 0.99

In tag aggregates over the search results, indexes tuned to an average recall of 0.99 answered differently from exact search for up to 27% of queries, and PQR-Search for at most 0.04% of trials. The pass-rate prediction of the cost law agreed well with the measurements across partitioning methods, scales, and updates without data-fitted constants (log correlation of at least 0.999 for static indexes). Our index and search were faster than FAISS exhaustive search, which gives the same guarantee, in all 52 main settings (geometric means of 22.9 × when queries are processed one at a time and 26.4 × when 1,000 queries are processed at once) and in every setting on the three 100M-scale datasets, and in all 52 main settings also faster than IVF whose nprobe was chosen afterwards. They were also 18.3 × faster at one thread than PDX-BOND [54], an exact scan that prunes with partial distances. The parameter selection rule was 1.18 × faster than the default configuration in geometric mean without slowing down any setting. The RaBitQ-equivalent configuration (all partitions tested with the full code), which gives the same guarantee, was also 12.1–20.5 × faster than FAISS exhaustive search in geometric mean per metric, and our method was faster than it by another 1.64 × (1-thread), 2.33 × (32-thread), and 1.24 × (batch), and 3.07 × faster than the official RaBitQ implementation run with the same partitions and error-bound parameter (1-thread). It was 27–76 ×

faster at one thread than the public implementation of PUFFINN, which also gives per-query guarantees, in the setting covered by its proof. On the streaming runbook, the failure rate stayed at most δ in all 33 search steps without retuning, whereas IVF-Flat tuned at the first time point exceeded its target failure rate by up to 4.2 ×,and residual-based maintenance made search 1.29–8.97 × faster than an index without maintenance. The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section 2 shows by measurement the per-query failures of indexes tuned by average recall, defines the problem, and reviews related work and the prior theory on which our method is based. Section 3 presents the theory of per-query guarantees and the pass-rate formula that determines the cost. Section 4 describes the index and search algorithm designed from these results, their implementation, and parameter selection based on the cost law. Section 5 addresses guarantees under updates and how to maintain an efficient index based on the formula of Section 3.3. Section 6 presents the experimental results, and Section 7 concludes the paper. Appendix A contains the proofs, Appendix B the lower bound for per-query guarantees, and Appendix C supplementary experiments. 

# 2. Background and Related Work 

In this section, we first show by measurement that indexes tuned by average recall miss neighbors on some queries, that such queries increase sharply when the query distribution differs from the distribution used for tuning, and that the initial tuning cannot maintain recall when the data are updated (Section 2.1). We then summarize the definitions of recall and of guarantees and formally define the problem addressed in this paper (Section 2.2), and classify related work by the form of its guarantee (Section 2.3). Finally, we summarize the prior theory that our method inherits (Section 2.4). 

# 2.1 Motivating Experiments 

Widely used ANN indexes set their parameters so that the average recall measured on calibration queries reaches a target [15], [16], [17]. To examine what this practice guarantees for individual queries, we repeated 20 times an 5experiment that splits the queries in half into calibration and test queries on three 1M-scale datasets (SIFT, GIST [12], arxiv [1]) (see Section 6.1 for the data and the measurement method). When the parameters of IVF-Flat, HNSW32, and IVF-RaBitQ were set to an average recall of 0.99 on the calibration queries, the average recall of the test queries met the target, but the fraction of queries with recall below 0.99 was 5.8–7.3% at k = 10 and 5.0–19.2% at k = 100 (Fig. 1(a)). IVF-RaBitQ at k = 10 did not reach an average of 0.99 even on the calibration queries on SIFT and GIST and was excluded from these ranges (see Section 6.1 for the implementation). This problem becomes much more severe when the distribution of real queries differs from the distribution used for tuning. In benchmarks, it is customary to split the data randomly and use the smaller part as queries [18], and without a query log, data points must be used for tuning as well. In our measurements, when real queries were sent to indexes tuned in this way to an average recall of 0.99, the fraction of queries with recall below 0.99 rose to 19.9–98.4% at k = 10 and 51.4–99.6% at k = 100, and 8.1–95.8% of queries fell even below recall 0.9 at k = 100 (yandex and laion, which search for images by text, and hotpotqa [1], which consists of real user questions; Fig. 1(a)). For IVF-Flat on laion, the average recall dropped to 0.37 at k = 10, and 60% of the queries had recall below 0.5. In contrast, when tuning used, instead of data vectors, a separate set of 1,000 real queries drawn from the same distribution as the test queries (corresponding to a query log), in the cases that reached an average recall of 0.99, the fraction of queries with recall below 0.99 at k = 100 was 4.5–18.7%, on the same level as the 5.0–19.2% for in-distribution queries. Hence the cause of this failure can be attributed to the distribution mismatch between the queries used for tuning and the real queries. Even when tuned on a query log, HNSW32 on laion ( k = 100) and IVF-RaBitQ on yandex and laion did not reach an average recall of 0.99 even at the largest search parameter, and the HNSW32 result agrees with prior reports that graph-based ANN struggles with out-of-distribution (OOD) queries [20], [21]. When the data are updated, the initially tuned parameters cannot maintain the same recall. We built an index on 50% of the yandex data and tuned it to an average recall of 0.99, and then, without retuning, applied five consecutive steps, each inserting 10% of the data arriving in cluster order and deleting the oldest 10%. The fraction of queries with recall below 0.99 at k = 100 rose from 13.5% to 22.3% for IVF-Flat, from 13.8% to 20.0% for FreshDiskANN, and from 11.1% to 16.7% for hnswlib, and also rose from 7.9% to 17.5% for Quake [37], which takes a recall target directly (Fig. 1(b)). The per-query shortfalls of CTR [43], which gives a distribution-level guarantee, are also shown in Fig. 1(c), and the settings are given in Section 6.3. 

# 2.2 Problem Definition 

Let D (|D| = n) be the set of data vectors or ∈ RD , and let qr be the vector of query q. Each data vector has a distinct id, and distances are Euclidean. The k vectors closest to the query form the true k-nearest neighbor set N ∗ 

> k

(q)(ties are broken by a fixed rule), and di denotes the distance from the query to the i-th closest vector ( d1 ≤ · · · ≤ dn). Letting Nk(q) be the set of k vectors returned by the search, we define the recall@ k of query q as Recall( q) = |N k(q) ∩ N ∗ 

> k

(q)|

k (1) (we omit k, which is determined by the context) [43]. As in SPANN [11], however, a vector at the same distance as the true k-th neighbor counts as a correct answer that takes the corresponding place in N ∗ 

> k

(q) (Appendix A.1). Asearch result is exact if Recall( q) = 1. For a target recall τ , letting m = ⌊(1 − τ )k⌋ be the number of allowed misses, Recall( q) < τ is equivalent to missing at least m + 1 neighbors. The term recall guarantee differs greatly in meaning depending on what the probability is defined over. Table 1 divides recall guarantees into five levels by placing, between empirical tuning without a guarantee (L0) and deterministic exhaustive search (L4), the marginal guarantee [55] and the high-probability guarantee over the calibration set [56], which the conformal prediction literature distinguishes, and the per-query guarantee over the randomness inside the algorithm [57]. The input-conditional guarantee of the conformal prediction literature is not included as a level, because it cannot be obtained without distributional assumptions [44]. Methods that tune by average recall, like the indexes of Section 2.1, give no guarantee for new queries (L0). Methods based on conformal prediction give an L1 guarantee [42], [55], which bounds the risk averaged over the calibration set and the query distribution, or an L2 guarantee that holds with high probability over the calibration set [56]. However, since the L1 guarantee is an average over calibration sets, the risk can exceed the target for a single drawn calibration set [56], and above all, such guarantees do not prevent failures of individual queries. This paper targets the L3 guarantee for each given query, provides it at lower cost than exhaustive search (L4) without assumptions on the query distribution, and maintains it under updates. Prior work that guarantees only per-neighbor probabilities or a single exact nearest neighbor per query is listed separately as L3 (part) (Section 2.3). 6Table 1. > L3 (part) Per-neighbor probability, or one exact nearest neighbor, for every given q

In this paper, the only randomness over which the probability of the guarantees is defined is the random orthogonal transformation P used by the index, where P is a rotation drawn uniformly (from the Haar measure) from O(D), the set of all D ×D orthogonal matrices; we generate it by QR decomposition of a D ×D matrix with independent standard normal entries, correcting the signs of the columns of Q by the signs of the diagonal of R. P is drawn without looking at the data or the queries when the index is built, and is replaced according to the schedule of Section 5.1. Since the error of the binary code test depends on P , it is random even for a given query, and its distribution is determined regardless of the data and the query (Section 3.1). The failure probability in this paper is the probability of missing a true neighbor because of this error. This framework, which fixes the query and defines the probability over the randomness inside the algorithm, is the same as that of LSH theory [57], and we apply it to a target on k-NN recall rather than an approximation ratio. All probabilities Pr and expectations E in this paper are over P , and the data, the query, and everything determined without looking at P (partition centroids and assignments, the partitions to visit, the initial radius (determined in Section 3.2), the error-bound parameters, the decisions of the parameter selection rule 

made from the prediction of Eq. (12), and so on) are regarded as fixed. Decisions to enable techniques that are made by measuring times that depend on the orthogonal transformation also do not change the upper bound on the failure probability (Section 4.3). Therefore, all guarantees in this paper hold for fixed data and for each fixed (given) query. Within this framework, the problem addressed in this paper is as follows. Given a target τ ∈ (0 , 1] and δ ∈ (0 , 1), the problem is to perform, as fast as possible, a k-ANN search that guarantees for every query Pr[Recall( q) < τ ] ≤ δ (2) Eq. (2) is the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee mentioned in Section 1, and for τ = 1 it is the exact-search guarantee (T1 in the experiment tables). The expected-recall guarantee, which guarantees E[Recall( q)] ≥ τ for every query, is written E in the experiment tables. In general, neither guarantee implies the other, but the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee of our method also satisfies the expected-recall guarantee for τ < 1 under a mild condition, and if only the expected-recall guarantee is needed, a lower-cost mode can be used (Section 3.2). With the same method, we also handle a form that bounds, per query, the loss E[max(0 , τ − Recall( q))] that CTR bounds over the query distribution (Ectr), and range queries, which bound the probability of missing even one vector within radius r (Section 3.2). We note two points about these guarantees. First, the queries and the updated points must be independent of P (in practice, the seed from which P is drawn is kept secret from whoever issues them), because an adversary who has seen the orthogonal transformation or the stored codes can make the failure probability 1 (Section 5.1). Second, since all queries use the same orthogonal transformation, the fraction of failing queries in any workload chosen without looking at P is at most δ in expectation, but the failures of multiple queries within the same index are not independent of one another, and a failing query may keep failing until the transformation is replaced (Section 5.1). Meanwhile, a ratio guarantee, which states that the distance of the returned k-th point is within 1 + ε times the true k-th distance, is logically independent of recall guarantees, and in high dimensions it becomes nearly unrelated to recall because of distance concentration [58]. For example, in our measurements on SSNPP [25], for 92% of the queries the top 100 all lie within 1.1 times the nearest-neighbor distance, so even a result that misses the nearest neighbor (recall@1 = 0) satisfies the ratio guarantee with ε = 0 .1. The guarantees of PAC-NN [59], LSH [57], and most LSH methods from the database community [60], [61], [62], [63], [64], [65] are per-query, but since they concern such ratio guarantees, we 7exclude them from our comparison. Per-query guarantees based on LSH or random projections that allow ratio 1 and return the exact nearest neighbor [46], [48], [49], [50] are discussed in Section 2.3. Table 3 summarizes the notation of this paper. Vectors are written in bold italic, and quantities that depend on the test stage ℓ are marked with the superscript ( ℓ). # 2.3 Related Work 

Table 5 classifies related work by the form of its guarantee. RaBitQ [14] gives probabilistic error bounds for distance estimation with a random orthogonal transformation and 1-bit binary codes, and inside IVF it discards vectors in the probed partitions whose lower bound on the distance is larger than the distance of the nearest neighbor found so far, arguing that the true nearest neighbor in the probed partitions is sent to re-ranking with high probability (with an unspecified constant). Vectors in unprobed partitions are not covered and no per-query recall target is stated, and the choice of centroids was left as future work. Concurrent work, RaBitQ-SSD [68], gives an unbiased estimate and a probabilistic error bound from a prefix of the code alone, and uses it to discard, within the probed lists, candidates whose lower bound is at least the current k-th distance, reducing SSD reads. Its bound is a concentration inequality with an unspecified constant and its recall is reported empirically, whereas we show that the error of a prefix code follows an exact distribution and is independent of all of P −1o (Lemma 1), which yields tests with exact quantiles and the pass-rate formula, and we guarantee per-query recall including unvisited partitions. ADSampling [41] compares distances while progressively sampling coordinates after a random orthogonal transformation, and gives a per-query guarantee by arguing that if all distance comparisons for the true k-NN among the candidates are correct, those k vectors are returned. This guarantee states that the k

closest among the candidates produced by the index are returned, which becomes a guarantee of exact search when all vectors are candidates (Corollary 4.2 of [41]). However, since the upper bound on the failure probability contains a constant of unknown value, parameters matching a target failure probability cannot be computed (the implementation uses empirically determined values), and its analysis of a threshold that depends on the random transformation, the running k-th distance, is implicit: the per-comparison argument uses only that the distance is within the threshold, and its verification fixes the threshold (see the discussion after Theorem 5). This paper states the guarantee for the recall of the query by testing unvisited vectors with binary codes of IVF residuals, determines the error-bound parameters by exact quantiles of the Beta distribution (Section 3.1), and shows by a formula how centroids affect the test cost (Section 3.3). Recent methods that push the time-accuracy trade-off of the RaBitQ family further on static data have weaknesses under updates (Table 7). SymphonyQG [69] computes, for each graph vertex, the RaBitQ codes of its neighbors with the vertex’s own vector as the centroid, stores these duplicated codes next to the vertex, and sets out-degrees to fit the 32-vector batches of FastScan. Inserting or deleting a vector therefore requires recomputing the neighbor codes and rebuilding the batches of every vertex whose edges change, and the authors leave handling deletions by rebuilding the edges of the vertices pointing to the deleted vertex as future work. MRQ [70] and SAQ [71] apply a PCA projection learned from the data and concentrate bits on the leading high-variance dimensions, choosing the number of leading dimensions (MRQ) or the dimension segmentation and bit allocation (SAQ) from the per-dimension variances of the data. The error bound that MRQ uses for the discarded tail dimensions is likewise a Chebyshev bound based on the per-dimension variances of the training data and on the assumption that the data are centered. Both methods were evaluated only on fixed data; when vectors from a different distribution accumulate, the learned projection and bit allocation drift from the data, and retraining requires re-encoding every vector. Multi-bit RaBitQ (Extended RaBitQ [72]) encodes each vector independently without training, but its encoding time grows as O(2 B D log D) in the number of bits B, which SAQ reduces (by more than 80 × at B = 9). In our index, the only data-dependent components are the IVF centroids, and the codes are 1-bit prefix codes and a 2-bit final stage, so an insertion only encodes the new vector with the current centroid and orthogonal transformation and changes no other code (Section 5.2). IVF partitions confine the effect of an update to one partition, so the FastScan layout is rebuilt only for that partition of the delta segment, and entirely at a merge. Moreover, since our guarantee requires testing unvisited vectors with their codes, IVF, which stores one code per vector contiguously by partition, suits this test. When the distribution shifts, the guarantee holds and only the cost changes (Sections 5.1 and 6.4). 10 Table 7. Methods of the RaBitQ family that lead in time-accuracy on static data, and their updates.                   

> Method Index Determined from data Extra work per insertion or deletion
> SymphonyQG [69] Graph Edges (neighbor codes duplicated with the vertex vector as centroid) Recompute neighbor codes and rebuild 32-vector batches of every vertex whose edges change MRQ [70] IVF PCA projection, number of leading dimensions, tail-dimension variances (used in the error bound) Under distribution shift, retrain and re-encode all vectors SAQ [71] IVF PCA projection, dimension segmentation and bit allocation Under distribution shift, retrain and re-encode all vectors Extended RaBitQ [72] IVF Centroids Encode only the new vector (time
> O(2 BDlog D))
> Ours IVF + delta segment Centroids Encode only the new vector (1-bit prefix, 2-bit), re-lay out only its partition

CTR [43] first certifies the search result of HNSW and rectifies only the queries that fail certification with a costlier exact search. The certification step applies conformal risk control [55] and Learn-then-Test [56] to scores obtained during the HNSW search. It defines the loss of a query as the amount by which recall falls short of τ (recall shortfall), max(0 , τ − Recall( q)), and shows that the expected loss of a new query drawn from the query distribution is at most 

α(1 − τ ) plus a certification error term of the rectification step. ConANN [42] calibrates a per-query nprobe of IVF by conformal prediction, and its guarantee concerns the average over the query distribution of the fraction of missed true neighbors (false negative rate). Both methods rely on the assumption that calibration queries and new queries come from the same distribution [55], and cannot give (conditional) guarantees for each individual input without distributional assumptions [44]. Indeed, in our measurements, some queries of CTR had recall below τ on SIFT and GIST, the evaluation datasets of the CTR paper, and also on arxiv (Fig. 1(c), Section 6.3). In contrast, the probability in our work is over the random orthogonal transformation of the index, so the guarantee holds for every given query, including out-of-distribution queries, and calibration queries are used only to determine the cost. CTR’s loss can also be bounded by α(1 − τ ) per query with the same method (Sections 3.2, 6.3). Methods that learn early termination or predict recall (LAET [15], DARTH [16], Ada-ef [17], Quake’s APS [37]) only predict recall and do not guarantee it. Pruning based on the triangle inequality or the law of cosines [51], [52], [73] accelerates search by skipping vectors that cannot be answers without computing their distances. CLIP [73] and TRIM [52] relax the bounds and allow a slight drop in recall, whereas Tribase [51] uses only pruning that does not change the result, so it becomes deterministic exact search when all partitions of IVF are searched. However, when only some partitions are searched, it yields the same recall as IVF with the same nprobe and does not guarantee per-query recall, and the speedup Tribase reported at recall 1 was also measured at the nprobe chosen so that the recall of the test queries became 1. We compare against Tribase searching all partitions as a baseline for the exact-search guarantee (Section 6.3). Dynamic ANN studies maintain recall under updates by graph repair (FreshDiskANN [38]), size-based splitting and merging (LIRE of SPFresh [39]), splitting and merging driven by a cost model together with an adaptive 

nprobe (Quake [37]), local reclustering according to reconstruction error (Ada-IVF [40]), and periodic recomputation of centroids (DeDrift [74]). However, among these, [37], [38], [39], [40] were all evaluated by average recall without guarantees, and the theory of deletions in graphs [75] also only shows that the random-walk statistics of the graph are preserved after deletions and does not guarantee the recall of query results. Our guarantee, on the other hand, holds without retuning if the updates and the decisions for maintaining an efficient index are independent of the orthogonal transformation (Theorem 6 in Section 5.1), so the maintenance policy determines only the cost (Section 5.3). Prior works that gave per-query probabilistic guarantees that also cover true neighbors outside the candidates are PUFFINN, Confirmation Sampling, and TPU-KNN, together with the LSH methods from the database community discussed in the next paragraph. PUFFINN [45] scans multiple LSH tries starting from the longest hash matches and stops after examining a number of tries computed from the collision probability of the current k-th candidate, thereby finding each of the true k-nearest neighbors with probability at least 1 − δ. This is a guarantee that the expected recall of every query is at least 1 − δ, of the same form as our expected-recall guarantee. Since the probability is per neighbor, the probability of finding all k neighbors is at least 1 − kδ by the union bound, and since the expected number M

of missed neighbors is at most kδ , Markov’s inequality gives the tail bound Pr[ M ≥ m + 1] ≤ kδ/ (m + 1). With a per-neighbor probability of δ(m + 1) /k this bound equals the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee; our bound uses the same Markov step, which cannot be improved given only per-neighbor probabilities (Proposition 1). However, the implementation supports only cosine and Jaccard similarity, filters candidates before exact distance computation with a sketch filter that the proof does not cover, and does not support deletion, and the original paper validated the guarantee only 11 by average recall. Our guarantees contain PUFFINN’s per-neighbor problem (Corollary 3 in Appendix C.3) and hold under Euclidean distance and under updates including deletions. In a comparison with the same public implementation, its default setting missed even the average-recall target because of a candidate filter that the proof does not cover, and with the filter disabled it was 27–76 × slower at one thread than our method configured for the expected-recall guarantee at the same level (Appendix C.3). Confirmation Sampling [46] queries, one after another, LSH data structures whose success probabilities are unknown, and returns the exact nearest neighbor of each query with probability 1 − δ without assumptions on the data. This work addresses the exact nearest neighbor for k = 1 (only outlining an extension to 

k-NN in its conclusion) and does not address a target recall τ < 1 or updates. TPU-KNN [47] computes all distances and then divides the data into several bins and collects only the closest point in each bin; under the assumption that the top k are placed in the bins independently and uniformly, it computes the expected recall and chooses the number of bins that meets the target. Our expected-recall guarantee holds over the random orthogonal transformation of the index without such placement assumptions, and does not compute all distances. The database community has developed many LSH variants with probabilistic guarantees. Like ours, their probability is over data-independent hash functions or random projections and therefore holds for every fixed dataset and query, but most of them bound the distance ratio of one returned point with constant probability. LSB-forest [60] returns a 4-approximate nearest neighbor, and C2LSH [61], QALSH [62], PM-LSH [63], and DB-LSH [65] a c2-approximate one (QALSH with probability at least 1 /2 − δ′ for its parameter δ′, PM-LSH and DB-LSH at least 1 /2 − 1/e , DB-LSH in sublinear time); LCCS-LSH [64] answers the ( R, c )-near-neighbor problem with probability at least 1 /4. Their k-NN variants also bound distance ratios, with guarantees absent, conditional on early termination (SRS [48]), or omitted (VHP [49]). SRS [48], VHP [49], and R2LSH [50] also allow ratio c = 1 and return the exact nearest neighbor with any given probability, a per-query exact-search guarantee for k = 1; for SRS, the worst-case expected cost in this case is linear in the data size. SRS, VHP, and R2LSH keep the projections fixed and support insertions and deletions through R-trees or B +-trees, so their guarantees might be read as surviving updates independent of the projections, but none of them states a guarantee under updates. We found no method in this line that bounds Pr[Recall( q) < τ ] for k > 1or analyzes its guarantee under updates. Approximate query processing (AQP) is close to our work in that it trades exactness for speed under a probabilistic error bound. Online aggregation [34] progressively reports a confidence interval that contains the final answer with a stated probability, computed from records retrieved in random order, and BlinkDB [35] and VerdictDB [36] answer from precomputed samples and attach confidence intervals (e.g., a 1% error at 95% confidence). Their probability is over sampling and ours is over a rotation inside the index, but both hold for fixed data and queries; and just as precomputed samples are shared by queries, our rotation is shared by all queries, so failures may be correlated across queries (Section 2.2). The difference lies in what is fixed in advance. Sampling-based AQP fixes the sample or the time and reports the error after execution, and cannot guarantee a pre-specified error bound for every query [67]; we fix the target ( τ, δ ) before execution and let the cost vary per query. Meanwhile, on adversarially constructed data, the search time of graph indexes is linear in the data size; for example, DiskANN does not find any of the top 5 neighbors before examining 10% or more of the data [76]. Theoretically as well, if SETH (strong exponential time hypothesis) [77], [78], [79], a hypothesis widely used as the basis of lower bounds in computational complexity theory, is true, then nearest neighbor search with a sufficiently small approximation error in high dimensions requires, even with polynomial-time preprocessing, time nearly linear in the data size per query [80], and the inner product similarity join cannot be solved essentially faster than quadratic time, even approximately [81]. By a reduction of the same kind, we show that this lower bound also applies to per-query recall guarantees. That is, if randomized SETH is true, then for D = ω(log n) no data structure with polynomial-time preprocessing that gives a per-query ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee or expected-recall guarantee can achieve query time n1−ε on worst-case inputs (Theorem 9 in Appendix B). Thus, if SETH is true, no index that gives per-query guarantees without assumptions on the data can avoid nearly linear cost in the worst case, so this paper targets lower cost than exhaustive search not in the worst case but on real data. 

# 2.4 Preliminaries 

This section summarizes the residual binary codes of RaBitQ [14] and their error, the triangle-inequality bounds of residual norms [51], [52], and the test argument of ADSampling [41], on which our index and search method are based. RaBitQ divides the data vectors into IVF partitions and normalizes each vector with respect to the centroid c of its partition. For a data vector or and a query qr , letting x = ∥or − c∥, Φ = ∥qr − c∥, o = ( or − c)/x , and q = ( qr − c)/Φ, we have 

∥or − qr ∥2 = x2 + Φ 2 − 2xΦ ⟨o, q⟩ (3) Since x and Φ need to be computed only once per vector or per partition, distance computation reduces to computing the inner product of unit vectors ρ = ⟨o, q⟩. Whether a vector lies within distance r of the query can also be decided 12 by the inner product alone. We define the pruning threshold for distance r as 

θ(r) = x2 + Φ 2 − r2

2xΦ (4) Then, by Eq. (3), for x, Φ > 0, ∥or −qr ∥ ≤ r and ρ ≥ θ(r) are equivalent. Moreover, residual norms give a deterministic bound through the triangle inequality |x − Φ| ≤ ∥ or − qr ∥ [51], [52], so only vectors with |x − Φ| ≤ r need to be tested. Hereafter we call this condition the triangle-inequality range .RaBitQ stores, as the binary code ¯ x = sign( P −1o)/√D, only the sign of each coordinate of P −1o, the unit vector o

transformed by the inverse of a random orthogonal matrix P , and estimates the inner product with the code vector ¯o = P ¯x as ˆ ρ = ⟨¯o, q⟩/⟨¯o, o⟩. The denominator does not depend on the query and is precomputed at indexing time, and the numerator ⟨¯x, P −1q⟩ can be computed fast with lookup tables (LUTs). Under a random orthogonal transformation, the error of this estimate follows a data-independent distribution. The error is proportional to √1 − ρ2

and to the deviation of the code vector from the direction of o, and its proportionality coefficient ζ is symmetric about 0, has variance 1, and is close to the standard normal distribution when D is large [82]. Since the squares of the coordinates of a point uniformly distributed on the sphere follow a Beta distribution, its cumulative distribution function GD (s) = Pr[ ζ < s ] is given, for s ≥ 0, by 

GD (−s) = 12 Pr 

[

Beta ( 12 , D−22

) > s2

D − 1

]

(5) This is the coordinate distribution given by RaBitQ [14], written as a Beta distribution. For s < 0, by symmetry, 

GD (s) = 1 − GD (−s). ADSampling [41] performs the distance comparison that decides whether a candidate vector lies within the k-th distance found so far by reading the randomly orthogonally transformed coordinates little by little from the front. If the distance estimated from some of the coordinates exceeds that distance even after allowing a slack, it discards the vector without reading the remaining coordinates, and it bounds the probability of wrongly discarding a vector within the distance by adding up, with a union bound, the failure probabilities at all points where reading can stop. Moreover, since the result is the same as the one computed with exact distances as long as the comparisons for the true k-nearest neighbors are correct, it restricts the union bound to the k true neighbors instead of all candidates [41]. This paper inherits these two ideas, namely the progressive test that reads the transformed coordinates from the front and the union over the 

k true neighbors. Section 3 determines the slack with the exact distribution of Eq. (5) and extends the argument to vectors of unvisited regions tested with residual codes and to a radius that depends on the rotation, stating it as a recall guarantee for the query. 

# 3. Per-Query Guarantees and Cost 

In this section, we present the theory of the proposed per-query guarantees and the law that determines their cost. The error identity and the union argument of Section 2.4 alone cannot provide per-query guarantees at low cost. For codes of which only a prefix is read, there is no formula that sets the failure probability of a single test to a target value, and there is no guarantee for τ < 1 or for a radius shrunk while unvisited vectors are being tested. Section 3.1 presents a pruning rule that reads codes from the front together with its failure probability, and Section 3.2 presents theorems that transfer it to the per-query ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee and the expected-recall guarantee. These theorems also determine which vectors may be excluded without testing (the triangle-inequality range) and the condition under which the radius may be shrunk during testing (Theorem 5). Section 3.3 derives a formula that predicts the fraction of vectors passing the test without data-fitted constants and without reading the rotation, and identifies the factors that reduce this fraction. Section 4 designs the index and the search algorithm according to these results. 

# 3.1 Prefix-Truncated Codes and the Pruning Rule 

We extend the code of Section 2.4 to a staged test that reads the code from the front. Let ¯ x(ℓ) be the vector that keeps only the first mℓ coordinates of the code ¯ x, and let the code vector be ¯ o(ℓ) = P ¯x(ℓ) (m1 < · · · < m L ≤ D). The test passes through stages ℓ = 1 , . . . , L in turn, and stage ℓ reads only the first mℓ coordinates and estimates the inner product ρ as follows. ˆρ(ℓ) = ⟨¯o(ℓ), q⟩⟨¯o(ℓ), o⟩ = ⟨¯x(ℓ), P −1q⟩⟨¯x(ℓ), P −1o⟩ (6) When mℓ = D, this estimator coincides with that of RaBitQ [14], and its denominator and numerator are computed in the same way. Since a prefix of the rotated coordinates acts as a random projection, a short prefix can discard most 13 vectors, and longer codes need to be read only for the remaining vectors. This is the same idea as the progressive dimension sampling of ADSampling [41]. Hereafter, we call such codes read from the front prefix-truncated codes . The magnitude of the estimation error is determined by 

κ(ℓ) =

√

mℓ/D − ⟨ ¯o(ℓ), o⟩2

⟨¯o(ℓ), o⟩√D − 1 (7) which is precomputed for each vector at indexing time. In the denominator of κ(ℓ), ⟨¯o(ℓ), o⟩ is the component of the code vector in the direction of o, and the numerator is the length of the remainder of the code vector after this component is subtracted. For 1-bit prefix codes, the squared norm of the code vector is mℓ/D , so this length is 

√

mℓ/D − ⟨ ¯o(ℓ), o⟩2.The estimation error arises from this remaining component. When mℓ = D, the code vector is a unit vector, so 

κ(ℓ) =

√

1 − ⟨ ¯o(ℓ), o⟩2 / (⟨¯o(ℓ), o⟩√D − 1), which equals the scale of the error bound of RaBitQ [14]. 

Lemma 1 (Error Identity): Let D ≥ 3 and |ρ| < 1, let the code be a function only of the vector’s own P −1o (and of random numbers independent of P ), and let ⟨¯o(ℓ), o⟩ > 0. If κ(ℓ) is taken as Eq. (7) with mℓ/D replaced by the squared norm of the code vector (Eq. (7) itself for 1-bit prefix codes), then the estimator of Eq. (6) satisfies 

ˆρ(ℓ) − ρ = √1 − ρ2 κ(ℓ) ζ(ℓ) (8) 

where ζ(ℓ) is independent of P −1o (and of those random numbers) and follows the distribution of Eq. (5) .

The proof of Lemma 1 is given in Appendix A.1. For 1-bit codes with mℓ = D, Eq. (8) is equivalent to the error identity and the error distribution of RaBitQ [14] combined. Lemma 1 extends this to arbitrary codes, including 1-bit codes of which only a prefix is read and 2-bit codes. Furthermore, RaBitQ [14] states that the error is independent of ⟨¯o, o⟩, and the proof in its technical report (arXiv:2405.12497, Appendix B.3) conditions on P −1o; Lemma 1 states this independence from all of P −1o for any code that is a function of P −1o, including prefix codes and 2-bit codes. Consequently, even for prefix-truncated codes and 2-bit codes, in which κ(ℓ) is a function of P −1o, the failure probability of the test (Theorem 1) and the pass probability (Section 3.3) can be computed independently of the data. The test prunes vectors that cannot lie within radius r of the query. As shown in Section 2.4, a vector lies within radius r if and only if ρ ≥ θ(r). However, ρ is unknown for a vector whose exact distance has not been computed, so the estimate ˆ ρ(ℓ) obtained from the code is used instead. To prevent a vector within the radius from being discarded because its estimate comes out smaller than the true value, we prune every vector that falls short of the threshold even after an error slack is added to its estimate, that is, every vector satisfying ˆρ(ℓ) + f κ (ℓ)ε0,ℓ < θ (r) (9) Vectors with zero residual and vectors with ⟨¯o(ℓ), o⟩ ≤ 0 pass without testing, and if Φ = 0, the form of Eq. (9) multiplied by 2 xΦ is used, so that a vector is pruned only when x > r . Here, f κ (ℓ)ε0,ℓ is the slack added to the estimate to guard against the estimation error. By Eq. (8), ρ = ˆ ρ(ℓ) − √1 − ρ2 κ(ℓ)ζ(ℓ); hence, when the error satisfies ζ(ℓ) ≥ − ε0,ℓ and 

f ≥ √1 − ρ2, and ε0,ℓ ≥ 0 so that ( f − √1 − ρ2) κ(ℓ)ε0,ℓ ≥ 0, a pruned vector satisfies ρ ≤ ˆρ(ℓ) + f κ (ℓ)ε0,ℓ < θ (r), so as long as the error stays within this range, no vector within the radius is pruned. Therefore, a vector within the radius is pruned only when ζ(ℓ) < −ε0,ℓ (Fig. 2(a)). The slack factor f is a value that replaces √1 − ρ2, which cannot be computed because ρ is unknown. Since pruning a vector outside the radius does not affect the result, f need only be an upper bound of this factor for vectors within the radius, and f = 1 always satisfies this. In addition, since ζ(ℓ)

follows the data-independent distribution GD (Lemma 1), the probability of this event is GD (−ε0,ℓ ). Therefore, to assign a failure probability δℓ to stage ℓ, it suffices to set the error-bound parameter ε0,ℓ by GD (−ε0,ℓ ) = δℓ. We take 

δℓ ≤ 1/2, so that ε0,ℓ ≥ 0, which the argument above uses; every setting in our experiments satisfies this. 

Theorem 1 (Failure Probability of Pruning): Let D ≥ 3, let the code satisfy the conditions of Lemma 1, let the stage configuration and the error-bound parameters ε0,ℓ ≥ 0 (i.e., GD (−ε0,ℓ ) ≤ 1/2) be chosen independently of P , and let the slack factor satisfy f ≥ √1 − max( θ(r), 0) 2 (the radius r may depend on P ). (a) If a vector within radius r is pruned at stage ℓ, then ζ(ℓ) < −ε0,ℓ . (b) The event in (a) has probability at most GD (−ε0,ℓ ). (c) Hence, a vector within the radius is pruned at some stage with probability at most ∑Lℓ=1 GD (−ε0,ℓ ).

The proof of Theorem 1 is given in Appendix A.1. ADSampling [41] also bounds the probability of the same event, but its bound contains a constant of unknown value, so its actual implementation uses an error-bound parameter determined empirically. In contrast, Theorem 1 sets the error-bound parameter from the exact distribution of Eq. (5), so the failure probability is bounded by the target value itself, with no empirically determined constant. In addition, the error bound of RaBitQ [14] sets the factor √1 − ρ2 of Eq. (8) to 1, but since vectors within the radius satisfy 

ρ ≥ θ(r), when θ(r) > 0 one can choose f smaller than 1 and make the test tighter (the specific value is given in Section 4.2). 14 θ(r)
