# 3.2 Per-Query Guarantees 

Transferring Theorem 1 to a guarantee for a query requires premises about the search. The per-query guarantees hold for any search that satisfies the following four conditions, and they do not depend on the structure of the index or the traversal order. C1. The reference point (the centroid c that normalizes codes), the stage configuration, and the error-bound parameters are chosen independently of the random orthogonal transformation P .C2. The test radius r is the k-th smallest of the exact distances of vectors chosen independently of P (hereafter, visited vectors) (infinity if there are fewer than k visited vectors). C3. Unvisited vectors outside the triangle-inequality range are excluded without testing, and the other vectors are tested at every stage by Eq. (9) with a slack factor f ≥ √1 − max( θ(r), 0) 2.C4. The exact distances of the vectors that pass all stages are computed, and the k nearest among the vectors whose exact distances have been computed are returned as the result. Each condition is needed for the guarantee for the following reasons. C1 allows the distribution of the test error to be computed as GD of Theorem 1. If the reference point or the error-bound parameters were chosen by looking at the same P , a selection bias that picks out favorable rotations would arise. By C2, the radius is at least the true 

k-th distance, so all true neighbors lie within the radius. C3 ensures that unvisited vectors are also checked without omission (the condition on the slack factor is for applying Theorem 1), and since the vectors excluded without testing are certainly outside the radius, true neighbors can be missed only in the test. By C4, the true neighbors whose exact distances have been computed are among the k results, so the only true neighbors missing from the result are those whose exact distances were not computed. In contrast, no condition is imposed on the data distribution, the quality of the partitions, or the number of visited vectors; these change only the cost (Section 3.3). Our IVF search takes the vectors of the partitions with the nearest centroids as the visited vectors, sets the initial radius by C2, and satisfies C1, C3, and C4; during the search, it shrinks this radius according to Theorem 5 below (Section 4.2). To transfer the failure probability of a single test to a guarantee for a single query, the probabilities of Theorem 1 must be summed over all vectors that must not be missed (union bound). To this end, we call the probability allowed for a single vector to be pruned at any stage the failure probability budget β, and divide it into per-stage shares, writing 

β = ∑ 

> ℓ

δℓ. By Theorem 1, if GD (−ε0,ℓ ) = δℓ, the probability that a single vector within the radius is pruned is at most 

β. Summing over all n vectors would shrink the budget to β = δ/n , which enlarges the error-bound parameters and lets far more vectors pass the test (our measurements are in Section 6.5). However, the recall of a query result drops only when true neighbors are missed, so, as in ADSampling [41], it suffices to sum over only the k true neighbors. The following lemma shows this. 

Lemma 2 : If the k vectors with the smallest distances in a candidate set C (|C| ≥ k) are returned, then Recall( q) ≥

1 − |N ∗ 

> k

(q) \ C|/k , even with ties. 

The proof of Lemma 2 is given in Appendix A.1. The reason is that true neighbors in the candidate set are also among the k nearest within the candidate set. That is, recall falls below 1 only when some true neighbors fail to enter 15 the candidate set, and it falls only by their number. Hereafter, writing this number, i.e., the number of missed true neighbors, as M = |N ∗ 

> k

(q) \ C|, we have Recall( q) ≥ 1 − M/k . From this we obtain the guarantee of exact search. 

Theorem 2 (Exact Search): Let D ≥ 3, let the code satisfy the conditions of Lemma 1, and in a search satisfying C1–C4, set GD (−ε0,ℓ ) = δℓ ≤ 1/2 and ∑ 

> ℓ

δℓ = β. If V denotes the set of unvisited true neighbors, then 

Pr[ result is not exact ] ≤ | V |

> L

∑

> ℓ=1

GD (−ε0,ℓ ) ≤ kβ (10) 

and therefore, if β = δ/k , the (τ, δ )-recall guarantee of Eq. (2) holds for τ = 1 .

The proof of Theorem 2 is given in Appendix A.1. Visited true neighbors enter the candidate set with their exact distances, and unvisited true neighbors also pass all stages and enter the candidate set unless they are pruned; hence, by Lemma 2, the result is not exact only if some vector of V is pruned. Applying Theorem 1 to each vector of V

for this event and summing yields Eq. (10). The argument of taking the union over only the k true neighbors is that of ADSampling [41]. Theorem 2 applies this argument with exact quantiles when the radius is set from the exact distances of visited vectors and the other vectors are tested with codes, and shows that the same upper bound holds in the following two cases. When some vectors are excluded without testing by the triangle-inequality range before the test, the excluded vectors are certainly outside the radius, so no true neighbor is lost at this step. Also, when several vectors are at the same distance as the true k-th neighbor, by Lemma 2 the recall is determined only by the number of true neighbors missing from the result, so the same upper bound is obtained. When τ < 1, up to m true neighbors may be missed, so for the same δ the failure probability budget β can be set larger. A larger β enlarges the per-stage shares δℓ, which makes the error-bound parameters ε0,ℓ smaller; the slack of the test shrinks and more vectors are pruned, so the cost decreases. Theorem 3 below shows how large it can be set. That is, since Recall( q) < τ requires M ≥ m + 1, β may be set to δ(m + 1) /k , which is m + 1 times that of exact search. 

Theorem 3 (( τ, δ )-Recall Guarantee): In the same setting as Theorem 2, when τ < 1, if β = δ(m + 1) /k , then 

Pr[Recall( q) < τ ] ≤ δ for every query. 

The proof of Theorem 3 is given in Appendix A.1. By the same calculation as Eq. (10), E[M ] ≤ kβ = δ(m + 1), and since Recall( q) < τ is equivalent to M ≥ m + 1, Markov’s inequality [82] gives Pr[ M ≥ m + 1] ≤ E[M ]/(m + 1) ≤ δ.This Markov step is tight only for analyses that use the per-neighbor pruning probabilities alone: given only these probabilities, it cannot be improved, because Markov’s inequality holds with equality when the number of missed true neighbors M takes only the two values 0 and m + 1. Such simultaneous failures are realized by placements with exact duplicate vectors. With a single test stage and a positive test threshold, m+1 identical vectors in one unvisited partition make the failure probability arbitrarily close to this bound when k = m+1 (which means τ ≤ 1/k ); with m+1 identical vectors in each of several unvisited partitions ( k a multiple of m + 1), the gap from the bound is only a small quantity of second order in β (first order in relative terms) (Proposition 1 in Appendix A.1). The bound is thus attained exactly only in that special case and otherwise within a second-order term. This tightness is a statement about this proof step on duplicate-heavy data, not about the algorithm on other data or about every per-query method; the lower bounds of Appendix B (Theorems 7–9) concern the cost of a per-query guarantee, not the Markov step. Therefore, as long as the same allowed probability is set for every neighbor independently of the data, β cannot be set larger than this, and Theorem 3 uses only as much slack as an analysis based on per-neighbor probabilities requires. An expected-recall guarantee, which states that the expectation of recall over P is at least τ for every query, can also be provided. This guarantee is obtained from the linearity of expectation alone, without any assumption on the relationship among the failures of neighbors, and a much smaller slack than that of the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee suffices. 

Theorem 4 (Expected-Recall Guarantee): Let D ≥ 3, let the code satisfy the conditions of Lemma 1, and in a search satisfying C1–C4 with every ε0,ℓ ≥ 0, let V be the set of unvisited true neighbors. If 

|V |

> L

∑

> ℓ=1

GD (−ε0,ℓ ) ≤ (1 − τ )k (11) 

holds (in particular, if GD (−ε0,ℓ ) = δℓ ≤ 1/2 and β = ∑ 

> ℓ

δℓ ≤ 1 − τ ), then E[Recall( q)] ≥ τ for every query. 

The proof of Theorem 4 is given in Appendix A.1. If the probability of missing each individual true neighbor is at most 1−τ , then the expectation of the missed fraction M/k is also at most 1 −τ , and hence the expectation of recall is at least 

τ . That is, a failure probability budget of β = 1 − τ suffices for the expected-recall guarantee. This value is much larger than the β = δ(m + 1) /k of the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee, so the error-bound parameters are small. For example, in our experimental setting ( τ = 0 .9, δ = 0 .01, k ∈ { 10 , 100 }, two test stages), the expected-recall guarantee needs a slack of only 1.65 times the standard deviation of the error, whereas the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee with the same τ needs 3.08–3.26 times. By the same reasoning, replacing the right-hand side of Eq. (11) with αk yields E[max(0 , τ − Recall( q))] ≤ α for 16 every query, and setting α = αCTR (1 − τ ) for the target αCTR of CTR [43] bounds, for every query, the loss that CTR bounds on average over the query distribution. Unlike TPU-KNN [47], Theorem 4 holds for every query, including unvisited vectors, without any assumption on the placement of neighbors. The same reasoning applies to range queries, i.e., searches that return all vectors within a radius r given independently of P . In a search satisfying C1, C3, and C4, setting ∑ 

> ℓ

GD (−ε0,ℓ ) ≤ δ/n makes the probability of missing even one vector within the radius at most δ, by Theorem 1 (c) and the union over the unvisited vectors within the radius (at most n of them). The ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee bounds Pr[ M ≥ m + 1] and the expected-recall guarantee bounds E[M ]; in general, neither implies the other. For example, with k = 10 and τ = 0 .9, if recall is 0.9 with probability 1 − δ and 0 with probability 

δ, the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee holds but the expectation is 0 .9(1 − δ) < τ ; if recall is 1.0 and 0.8 with probability 1/2 each, the expected-recall guarantee holds but Pr[Recall( q) < τ ] = 1 /2. However, Theorem 3 bounds E[M ] before applying Markov’s inequality, so the following corollary shows that the same execution also satisfies the expected-recall guarantee. 

Corollary 1 : In the (τ, δ )-recall guarantee mode of Theorem 3, if δ(m + 1) ≤ (1 − τ )k, then E[Recall( q)] ≥ τ also holds for every query. 

The proof of Corollary 1 is given in Appendix A.1. This condition is equivalent to the β = δ(m + 1) /k of Theorem 3 satisfying the condition β ≤ 1 − τ of Theorem 4, and if (1 − τ )k ≥ 1, it holds whenever δ ≤ 1/2. All settings with 

τ < 1 in our experiments fall into this case, so the single ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee mode yields both guarantees at once. In contrast, for τ = 1, E[Recall( q)] ≥ 1 means that the failure probability is zero, so no method with a positive failure probability can satisfy the expected-recall guarantee. If the visited vectors contain few true neighbors, the radius set from their distances can be much larger than the true 

k-th distance. In this case, it is more efficient to shrink the radius to the k-th distance as more vectors have their exact distances computed while unvisited vectors are being tested, and to test subsequent vectors with the shrunk radius (hereafter called the adaptive radius ). C2 fixes the radius at the value set from the visited vectors and thus does not cover this case, but all that the guarantee actually requires is that the radius be at least the true k-th distance. However, which vectors pass the test and have their exact distances computed depends on the test results, and the test results depend on the rotation P . Hence, a radius shrunk in this way also depends on P . Since Theorems 2–4 presuppose a radius determined independently of the test errors, it must be shown separately that the guarantee is preserved even when the radius and the test errors depend on the same P . The following theorem shows that the guarantee remains intact even when C2 is relaxed in this way. 

Theorem 5 (Adaptive Radius): Let D ≥ 3, let the search satisfy C1, C3, and C4, and let the test radius be set as follows instead of by C2. After an initial radius is set from visited vectors chosen independently of P , the radius may be changed each time an exact distance is computed while unvisited vectors are tested, provided that (i) the radius used for the triangle-inequality range and for each test is the maximum of the distances of some k distinct data vectors whose exact distances have been computed (infinity until k such vectors have been collected), and (ii) the threshold θ(r) and the slack factor f of each test are computed with the radius r used in that test, with f ≥ √1 − max( θ(r), 0) 2. The order of tests, the choice of vectors whose exact distances are computed first, and whether the same vector is tested again may depend on P . Then, with V defined with respect to the visited vectors that set the initial radius, Theorems 2–4 and Corollary 1 hold as they are. 

The proof of Theorem 5 is given in Appendix A.1. The guarantee is preserved even though the radius depends on P

for the following reasons. By (i), the radius is always the maximum of the distances to k actual vectors, so it is at least the true k-th distance, and the true neighbors lie within the radius at every point in time. Theorem 1 (a) holds for any radius, so whenever and with whatever radius a true neighbor was pruned, its error satisfies ζ(ℓ) < −ε0,ℓ . Since this condition does not involve the radius, the union bounds and the expectation calculations of Theorems 2–4 do not use the P -dependent radius or order at all. Testing the same vector again at the same stage yields the same error, so the failure probability does not increase. Moreover, the radius in (i) need not be the k-th of all distances computed so far. Therefore, the guarantee remains intact even when multiple threads share the processing of a single query and each thread sets the radius from its own collected k vectors, or when a test uses the value from before another thread shrank the radius (Section 4.2). RaBitQ [14] also selects re-ranking targets within IVF using the distance of the nearest neighbor found so far as the threshold, and explains that, by its error bound, the true nearest neighbor in the visited partitions is sent to re-ranking with high probability. The step that converts the pruning event into a condition on the error ζ alone, without the radius, also appears in ADSampling [41], whose per-comparison argument uses only that the distance is within the threshold; ADSampling regards earlier tests changing the threshold of later tests as a bias in its verification study and fixes the threshold there at the distance of the true k-th neighbor. Theorem 5 makes explicit that the recall guarantee for a query, including unvisited vectors, is preserved even when the threshold depends on the rotation, also with per-thread radii and stale radii. 17 3.3 Residual Cost Law 

The theorems of Section 3.2 only set an upper bound on the failure probability; they do not determine the cost of providing the same guarantee. In this section, we present the law that determines this cost; in this paper, the law is used in two ways. First, it shows that the cost is determined by the residual scale, the test radius, and the error scale, which grounds the residual codes and the index and search design of Section 4. Second, since it predicts the cost without reading the rotation, the parameters chosen by this prediction (Section 4.3) and the decisions for maintaining an efficient index in dynamic environments (Section 5.3) do not break the premises of Theorem 1 and Theorem 6 (Section 5.1). Therefore, the cost can be adjusted without retuning while the guarantee is kept. Moreover, since this law is independent of the guarantee, a wrong prediction changes only the cost. We first show that the fraction of unvisited vectors that pass the first-stage ( ℓ = 1) test, i.e., the first-stage pass rate, determines the cost; we then derive a formula that predicts this fraction without data-fitted constants, and finally summarize the implications of this formula for designs that reduce the cost. Unvisited vectors not pruned at the first stage are tested again with longer codes at later stages, and if they pass all stages, their exact distances are computed with the original vectors. Hence, the more vectors pass the first stage, the higher the cost of the later-stage tests and of the exact distance computations. In our measurements, the vectors whose exact distances were computed amounted to a median of only 0.17% of all vectors. Therefore, most of the time is spent on the first-stage tests and on processing the vectors that pass the first stage, and the main variable that determines the cost is the first-stage pass rate. The first-stage test of our implementation is Eq. (9) with an added deterministic slack σdet that covers quantization and floating-point errors (Section 4.2). That is, a vector or passes when ˆρ(1) ≥ θ(r) − f κ (1) ε0,1 − σdet . Substituting Eq. (8) into this condition and solving for ζ(1) , the pass condition becomes 

ζ(1) ≥ z(κ), where κ = κ(1) and z(κ) = ( θ(r) − ρ − f κ ε 0,1 − σdet )/(√1 − ρ2 κ). By Lemma 1, ζ(1) is independent of P −1o and κ is a function of P −1o, so given κ, the probability of passing is GD (−z(κ)) by the symmetry of GD .Therefore, the probability that a vector or in the triangle-inequality range passes the first stage is Pr[pass] = Eκ

[GD (−z(κ)) ] (12) (Fig. 2(a)). Here, the expectation is over the distribution of κ induced by P , and this distribution is determined by D

and m1 alone. The first-stage pass rate of a query is the average of this probability over the triangle-inequality range. The only inputs are x, Φ, ρ, r, σdet , ( D, k, τ, δ ), and the stage configuration, and there is no data-fitted constant. When the radius r and the deterministic slack σdet are fixed, Eq. (12) is an equality, not an upper bound (for vectors with x, Φ > 0 and |ρ| < 1). Since the slack of the implementation varies slightly with the rotation, for the measured pass rate the formula is an approximation that plugs in the average slack. Although ρ is unknown during search, it is known for calibration queries, whose exact distances can be computed; hence, this formula is used to predict the pass rate with the true k-th distance of the calibration queries as the radius, and the parameter selection rule of Section 4.3 uses a light approximation of it. In our measurements, this prediction agreed well with the measured pass rates without any data-fitted constant. Over 256 points with up to 10 partitioning methods at 1M scale, the log correlation was 0.9995, and over 38 points at 10M scale it was 0.999 (Fig. 15; validation under updates and at 100M scale is also in Section 6.5). The effect of the centroid on the cost appears in the term θ(r) − ρ of Eq. (12). Rearranging it using Eq. (4) and Eq. (3) gives 

g = θ(r) − ρ = ∥or − qr ∥2 − r2

2xΦ (13) This margin g indicates how far the ρ of a vector outside the radius lies below the test threshold. Since ∥or − qr ∥ in the numerator is the distance between the original vectors, it does not depend on the centroid, and the entire effect of the centroid on the margin lies in the residual scale xΦ in the denominator. With the other inputs of Eq. (12) ( f , σdet ,

ρ) fixed, the pass probability decreases in the margin; therefore, when the centroid lies farther from the vector and the query so that xΦ grows, the margin shrinks and more vectors outside the radius pass the first stage (Fig. 2(b)). Conversely, when the radius shrinks from r to r′, the margin grows by ( r2 −r′2)/(2 xΦ), so the adaptive radius (Theorem 5) reduces the cost without changing the guarantee. This is why the cost of residual coding depends on the centroid, and Section 5.3 uses this relation in the decisions for maintaining an efficient index in dynamic environments. The error scale of a stage that reads mℓ code coordinates is typically κ(ℓ) ≈ √πD/ (2 mℓ) − 1/√D − 1, and for mℓ = D

it is about 0 .755 /√D, which corresponds to the ⟨¯o, o⟩ ≈ 0.8 reported by RaBitQ [14]. If the first-stage code length m1

is fixed, κ(1) converges to √π/ (2 m1) as the dimension grows and is nearly independent of the dimension. For example, with m1 = 128, it is 0.067 for D = 128 and 0.106 for D = 1024, and the limit is 0.111. Therefore, the cost of the first stage is determined by the number of bits, and the error scale does not exceed √π/ (2 m1) even as the dimension grows (Section 6.6). Eq. (12) shows how to reduce the cost under the same guarantee. To enlarge the margin g, one must reduce the residual scale xΦ or shrink the radius r toward the true k-th distance (Theorem 5); to reduce the error scale κ(1) , one must read 18 longer codes at the first stage, which increases the first-stage cost accordingly; and the deterministic slack σdet becomes smaller as the quantization error of the implementation is reduced. IVF partitions contribute to both components of the margin. The centroid, as the reference point that normalizes the 1-bit codes, reduces the residual scale, and visiting the partitions near the query first brings the initial radius close to the true k-th distance through the true neighbors in those partitions (Section 6.5). Section 4 designs the index and the search algorithm according to these terms. Comparing this result with exhaustive search gives an intuitive understanding of why the proposed method provides the same guarantee at low cost. Exhaustive search computes the distances of all vectors and thus reads all D coordinates of the original of every vector. However, what most vectors need for the result to be determined is not the value of their distance but only a test showing that they cannot lie within the radius r, and for vectors far from the radius, even a coarse estimate makes it clear that they are outside the radius. The first stage makes this test with an m1-bit code per vector. For example, with m1 = 128, this takes 23 bytes including the coefficients, about 1/130 of a 768-dimensional fp32 original (3,072 bytes), and SIMD tests 64 vectors at once (Section 4.2). The risk of discarding a true neighbor because of a wrong coarse estimate is prevented by a slack on the error, whose distribution is fixed thanks to the random orthogonal transformation. Since this slack grows only roughly in proportion to √ln(1 /δ ) as the failure probability δ

is reduced, strengthening the guarantee increases the cost only slightly. Residual codes increase the number of vectors filtered out under the same guarantee. Since the error is proportional to the magnitude of the residual, coding with respect to a nearby centroid enlarges the margin so that more vectors are filtered out at the first stage (Eq. (13), Fig. 2(b)); vectors outside the triangle-inequality range are discarded without even reading their codes; and visiting the partitions near the query first starts the radius small. As a result, for most vectors it suffices to read a few tens of bytes at the first stage, and only the few vectors that pass all stages have their originals read for distance computation. For the same first-stage code length, the size ratio between the original and the code is proportional to the dimension, so the gain grows with the dimension. IVF without guarantees also reduces the number of vectors whose originals are read, but since it does not look at unvisited partitions, it can miss the true neighbors there, and to answer all test queries correctly it must scan many partitions with originals to accommodate the hardest query. The proposed method examines only a few partitions with originals and handles the rest with this cheap test, while still providing a guarantee for every query. 

# 4. Index and Search 

In this section, we describe the index and the search algorithm designed according to Section 3. Section 4.1 describes the structure and construction of the index and then explains how and why it differs from standard IVF; Section 4.2 describes how queries are processed so as to satisfy the conditions of Theorem 5 (Section 3.2), together with the implementation; and Section 4.3 describes the rule that chooses, by the cost law of Section 3.3, the options that change only the query processing cost. Whatever the rule chooses, the guarantee remains intact, and its effect is measured in Section 6.5. 

# 4.1 Index Structure and Construction 

The index divides the data vectors into IVF partitions, normalizes each vector as a residual with respect to its partition centroid (Section 2.4), and stores it as a prefix-truncated code (Section 3.1). The per-vector values needed for the test (Eq. (9)), namely the residual norm x and, for each stage, the inner product of the code and the unit vector 

⟨¯o(ℓ), o⟩ = ⟨¯x(ℓ), P −1o⟩, the inner product of the code and the centroid ⟨¯x(ℓ), P −1c⟩, and the error scale κ(ℓ) (Eq. (7)), are computed at indexing time. Since the test radius must be an exact distance to actual vectors (C2 and C4 in Section 3.2), the original vectors are also stored. Vectors with zero residual (vectors equal to the centroid) have no defined unit vector or code, so they are marked separately. Algorithm 1 is the construction procedure of the index. The number and sizes of the partitions and the assignment rule are determined without reading P , so they are irrelevant to the guarantee and change only the cost. A large number of partitions N increases the routing cost of computing the distances to all centroids for each query, and a small N

increases the number of vectors in the visited partitions and hence the exact distance computations. In contrast, the first-stage test of unvisited vectors is performed for all vectors, so its cost is nearly independent of N , and a larger 

N reduces the residual scale and thereby the first-stage pass rate (Section 3.3). The centroids are obtained by the k-means [83] of FAISS [8] (line 1). The assignment places an upper bound on the partition size, following the same idea as the balanced clustering of SPANN [11] (line 2). This is because every partition is tested for each query and batch queries are processed in parallel per partition, so the largest partition determines the load imbalance among threads. However, vectors assigned to a centroid other than the nearest one because of the cap have larger residuals. Accordingly, vectors are assigned in decreasing order of the difference between the squared distances to their two nearest centroids, i.e., the increase in the squared residual when assigned to the second-nearest centroid, and the destinations are limited 19 Algorithm 1: Index Construction

Lines 1–3 do not read P , and P is drawn afterward in line 4, once and independently of the data, uniformly from the set of all D-dimensional orthogonal matrices. Lines 5–6 apply the Encode function (lines 7–11) to every vector and the Layout function (lines 12–15) to every partition. The Encode function normalizes the residual of one vector to make its code (line 8), computes for each stage the inner products of the code and the error scale κ(ℓ) (Eq. (7)) (line 9), and, if the 2-bit final stage (Section 4.3) is used, also computes that code (line 10). The Layout function sorts the vectors of one partition in order of the residual norm x and stores them divided into the first-stage data, the later-stage codes and coefficients, and the original vectors (Fig. 3(a); see the storage layout paragraph below). The main costs of construction are k-means, applying the rotation (matrix multiplication, 

O(nD 2)), and computing the codes ( O(nD )); Section 6.3 compares index construction costs. The segment structure for updates is described in Section 5.1, and insertion, deletion, and merging in Section 5.2; these operations reuse the Encode and Layout functions as they are (Algorithm 3). The proposed index is used differently from standard IVF [8], [12]. Standard IVF visits only nprobe partitions and tolerates missing the true neighbors left in the rest, whereas the proposed index tests all vectors of the unvisited partitions as well and discards only the vectors that are certainly outside the radius. Accordingly, residuals and codes serve different purposes. IVF-PQ encodes residuals because smaller residuals can be quantized more accurately with the same code length [12], whereas in the proposed index a smaller residual scale enlarges the margin of the test so that more vectors are discarded (Section 3.3). Codes are read from the front, and the failure probability of the test at each stage is set by Theorem 1. Making the distribution of the test error data-independent by a random orthogonal transformation and keeping the original vectors for re-ranking by exact distances are the same as in the IVF index of RaBitQ [14]; in the proposed index, they are the premises of, respectively, the computation of the failure probability (Lemma 1, Theorem 1) and the test radius determined by exact distances (Section 3.2). In addition, in the proposed index, an insertion is a partition assignment, a deletion is a bitmap mark, and maintenance takes place per centroid, so the conditions of the guarantee remain simple under updates (Section 5). Since most of the search time is spent on the first-stage tests and on processing the vectors that pass the first stage (Section 3.3), the storage layout is designed to reduce the amount of data read in these two kinds of processing. Fig. 3(a) shows the storage layout of one partition, which is built by the Layout function of Algorithm 1 (lines 12–15). The vectors in a partition are sorted in order of the residual norm x. Then, as in CLIP [73], which skips parts of inverted lists, the triangle-inequality range becomes a single contiguous interval in each partition and can be found by binary search. The storage is divided into three parts according to which vectors they are read for. The first-stage data at the top are read for all unvisited vectors in the triangle-inequality range for every query, so they are kept much smaller than the originals. The first-stage data consist of the code prefix, three coefficients for computing Eq. (9) with a single comparison per vector, and x. The coefficients are determined by x, ⟨¯o(1) , o⟩, κ(1) , and the inner product of the code and the centroid ⟨¯x(1) , P −1c⟩ (the use of this inner product is described in Section 4.2), and they are stored quantized to 8 bits with the minimum and step of each partition. For example, with m1 = 128, the first-stage data take 23 bytes per vector. Below them, the later-stage codes and coefficients are read only for the vectors that pass the preceding 20 rq

stage, and the original vectors (integer data in their original 8-bit integers) are read only for the visited vectors and the vectors that pass all stages. 

# 4.2 Query Processing 

Algorithm 2 and Fig. 3(b) show the processing of a single query. Lines 1–3 visit nprobe partitions in order of the exact distance Φ to the centroid, compute the exact distances between the query and all vectors in those partitions, and set the k-th of these distances as the initial radius r. In the unvisited partitions, vectors outside the triangle-inequality range are excluded without testing (line 6), and vectors within the range are tested progressively with binary codes (line 8). The 1-bit test stages start at the first-stage code length m1 and end at D bits, and if the 2-bit final stage is used, one more stage is added after them (Section 4.3). However, vectors with zero residual have no code, so if they fall within the triangle-inequality range, their exact distances are computed without a code test (lines 8–9). The vectors that pass all stages are re-ranked by computing their exact distances; with these distances, the radius is shrunk to the k-th distance among the candidates, and subsequent tests use the shrunk radius (line 9, adaptive radius). Line 10 returns the k nearest candidates (Lemma 2), and vectors near the k-th distance are ranked after their distances are recomputed in double precision (fp64). Fig. 3(b) illustrates this process in two dimensions. The two partitions nearest to the query q are visited, and the radius r (dashed circle) is set from the vectors in them (lines 1–3). If the distance between the query and the centroid of an unvisited partition is Φ, vectors closer to the centroid than Φ − r have x < Φ − r and hence lie farther than r

from the query, so they are excluded without testing (hatched inner circle, line 6). The remaining vectors are tested with their codes; most of them are pruned ( ×), and only a few pass and have their exact distances computed ( ⃝, lines 8–9). Partitions all of whose vectors lie outside the triangle-inequality range, like the two partitions at the bottom of the figure, are excluded entirely. Algorithm 2 satisfies the conditions of Theorem 5. The centroids are determined before P (Algorithm 1), the stage configuration is determined without reading the rotation (Section 4.3), and the error-bound parameters ε0,ℓ are fixed in advance, independently of the rotation and the query, by GD (−ε0,ℓ ) = δℓ from the per-stage shares δℓ of the failure probability budget β (Theorems 2–4); hence C1 holds. The visited vectors are the vectors of the nprobe partitions chosen in order of Φ, which is independent of P , so the initial radius satisfies C2 (lines 1–3). Lines 6 and 8 satisfy C3 and lines 9–10 satisfy C4, and in addition, line 9 shrinks the radius only to distances of k distinct vectors whose exact distances have been computed. Only the test in line 8 reads the rotation P (the LUTs in line 4 are a preparation for this test); the radius shrunk in line 9 depends on P through the test results of line 8, and by Theorem 5 the guarantees of Theorems 2–4 hold in this case as well. Why the remaining lines do not discard true neighbors was shown in Section 3.2. In standard IVF, nprobe determines recall. In contrast, the proposed index tests all unvisited partitions as well, so nprobe 

is merely the number of candidates from which the initial radius is set, and the guarantee that the failure probability is at most δ (Theorems 2–5) holds regardless of the value of nprobe . Increasing nprobe brings the initial radius closer to the true k-th distance and reduces the number of vectors that pass the test, but increases the number of vectors whose exact distances are computed in the visited partitions. The implementation described below serves to speed up query processing, and the theorems of Section 3 permit it 21 Algorithm 2: Query Processing

Output: k vectors close to qr

while preserving the guarantee. Since most of the time of Algorithm 2 is spent in lines 8–9 (Section 3.3), we speed up the first-stage test by computing it for many vectors at once with SIMD instructions. Since Theorem 1 sets the failure probability of the test per stage, the guarantee remains intact even if the first stage tests with short codes and the later stages process only the few vectors that pass the first stage. Query processing is implemented in C++ with AVX-512. The LUTs are built once per query from the rotated coordinates P −1qr of the original query vector, and 

⟨¯x, P −1(qr − c)⟩, which is needed for the numerator ⟨¯x, P −1q⟩ of Eq. (6), is obtained from them by subtracting the inner product of the code and the centroid stored in the per-vector coefficients. As in fast-scan [84], the first stage quantizes a 16-entry LUT for every 4 code coordinates (4 bits) to 8 bits, keeps it in SIMD registers, and looks it up with shuffle instructions; it accumulates 64 vectors at a time, the number that fits in one 512-bit register, and tests them in the integer domain. Moreover, since Theorem 5 does not restrict the order of tests, a single query scans a front part of the unvisited partitions first and then the rest in stored order so that memory is read sequentially, and batch queries are sorted in order of their nearest centroids and processed in groups per partition so that the first-stage data are reused from the cache. Since Theorem 1 requires only f ≥ √1 − max( θ(r), 0) 2 of the slack factor, the first stage uses f = min(1 , r/ Φ), a single value per partition. This value is an upper bound of √1 − max( θ(r), 0) 2 over x, so it satisfies the condition of Theorem 1 for all vectors in the partition. Since it is a single value per partition, the test can be computed from per-vector coefficients and per-query, per-partition values alone, which suits the SIMD test above; and in unvisited partitions with 

r < Φ, the slack is smaller than when 1 is used as in RaBitQ [14], so more vectors are pruned. The later stages use the per-vector value √1 − max( θ(r), 0) 2. Testing with 8-bit quantized LUTs and coefficients keeps the guarantee intact. If the rounding errors of quantization and accumulation are replaced by deterministic bounds so that the left-hand side of the test is computed to be no smaller than its exact value and the right-hand side no larger than its exact value (with a bound that holds for every rotation, even if the slack depends on the rotation), then any vector pruned by the implementation has an exact left-hand side smaller than the exact right-hand side and is therefore also pruned by Eq. (9). Hence, the test of the implementation passes every vector that Eq. (9) passes, and Theorem 1 applies as is. The sum of these bounds is the deterministic slack σdet of Section 3.3 (the list of errors to which this argument applies is in Appendix A.3). Since the radius of Theorem 5 need only be the maximum of the distances of some k distinct vectors whose exact distances have been computed, synchronization among threads can be reduced when a single query is processed by multiple threads. Each thread keeps a local heap, the radius is shared through atomic minimum updates, and the vectors in a local heap are handed over to the shared heap whenever the lock can be acquired without waiting and when the thread finishes its processing. On the other hand, counting the same vector twice could make the radius smaller than the true k-th distance; hence, if a vector that has already entered the candidates through early exact computation (Section 4.3) passes the test again during the scan, it is filtered out by its id so that it is not inserted into the candidates twice. 

# 4.3 Parameter Selection by the Cost Law 

The proposed index has options that change only the query processing cost while preserving the guarantee: the configuration of the test stages (the first-stage code length m1 and the use of a final 2-bit test stage) and the use of 

optimization techniques that shrink the radius early or reduce the deterministic slack. Which choice is faster depends on which term of Eq. (12) is large, and thus differs by dataset and metric. In this section, we describe the parameter selection rule based on the cost law of Section 3.3. The rule chooses the stage configuration and global centering by the 22 prediction of Eq. (12), which does not read the rotation, and the use of the other two techniques by times measured on calibration queries; since all choices lie within the conditions of Theorem 1 and Theorem 5, the per-query guarantee remains intact whatever is chosen. Therefore, calibration queries affect only speed, and the guarantee is maintained even if the distributions of calibration queries and actual queries differ. This distinguishes our approach from standard IVF, which tunes nprobe on calibration queries to meet an average recall, and from CTR [43] and ConANN [42], whose guarantees hold under the assumption that calibration queries and actual queries follow the same distribution. The targets of the selection are the three terms of Eq. (12) that determine the first-stage pass rate: the margin g, the error scale κ(1) , and the deterministic slack σdet . Since the margin grows as the radius shrinks, the adaptive radius of Section 4.2 is always used, and two techniques that shrink the radius early are added for cases in which the initial radius is larger than the true k-th distance, such as out-of-distribution queries. Early exact computation computes, before line 5 of Algorithm 2, the exact distances of the vectors with small first-stage estimated distances in a few partitions at the front in Φ order; estimate-ordered processing, when a single query is processed by multiple threads, first processes to the end a few vectors with small estimated distances among those that pass the first stage in a partition, thereby shrinking the radius, and then processes the rest (lines 7–9). The error scale κ(1) is determined by the first-stage code length m1; longer codes reduce the pass rate, but the first-stage cost increases proportionally. Adding, after the last 1-bit stage, a stage that tests with a 2-bit code that quantizes each coordinate of P −1o to a 4-level uniform grid symmetric about 0 makes the error scale of that stage about half that of the 1-bit code, at the expense of more memory. The LUT quantization slack, which accounts for most of the deterministic slack, is proportional to the norm of the query vector, so for data whose mean is far from the origin, it is reduced by global centering , which builds the LUTs in coordinates relative to the mean c0 of the entire data. The benefits of these choices differ by dataset. For example, the configuration that tests the first stage with short codes is fast on in-distribution data, but on datasets with out-of-distribution queries, where many vectors pass the first stage, the configuration that reads the entire code at once is faster (Section 6.5). The operation and implementation of each technique are summarized in the appendix (Appendix C.7). The per-query guarantee is preserved no matter how the first-stage code length m1, the 2-bit final stage, global centering, early exact computation, and estimate-ordered processing are chosen or turned on and off. The stage configuration is determined without reading the rotation, so it lies within the assumptions of Theorem 1. The 2-bit code, like Extended RaBitQ [72], determines the code, including the scale of the grid, from each vector’s P −1o

alone, so it satisfies the conditions of Lemma 1, and its stage is also included when the failure probability budget β

is divided into per-stage shares. Global centering does not change the test value and only reduces the slack, since 

⟨¯x, P −1(qr − c0)⟩ − ⟨ ¯x, P −1(c − c0)⟩ = ⟨¯x, P −1(qr − c)⟩. The adaptive radius and the two techniques that assist it change only the radius, the order of tests, and the choice of vectors whose exact distances are computed first, so they lie within the conditions of Theorem 5. The parameter selection rule is run once before queries are processed. After the per-stage values and the storage layout are built for each candidate configuration with the Encode and Layout functions of Algorithm 1, the first-stage code length m1 and the use of the other techniques are determined for each dataset, query setting ( k and the guarantee level), and performance metric. This is because, even on the same data, the error-bound parameters differ with the guarantee level, and the fastest configuration can differ between processing queries one at a time and processing them together. The metrics are the latency when a single query is processed by a single thread, the latency when a single query is divided among multiple threads, and the throughput when many queries are processed together by multiple threads. The stage configuration and global centering are chosen as the configuration with the shortest predicted time on calibration queries (the simpler one if the difference is small). The predicted time is obtained by converting into time, with machine constants, a pass-rate prediction that uses the true k-th distance of the calibration queries as the radius and lightly approximates Eq. (12) (setting each vector’s ρ to 0, κ to a per-stage representative value, and the deterministic slack to an expression proportional to the query norm); the candidates are the configurations that combine the first-stage code length (the default or D), the use of the 2-bit final stage, and the use of global centering. The default is set so that a longer code is chosen when the first-stage pass rate is higher, measured with some data vectors as queries on an index built with a separate rotation independent of the index. The inputs of these rules are the true k-th distances of the calibration queries, the query–centroid distances, the residual norms, the error-bound parameters, per-stage representative values obtained from the universal distribution of κ, machine constants, and the pass rate measured with the separate rotation; they do not read the rotation or the codes of the index. Whether to turn on early exact computation (when a single query is processed by a single thread, and for batches) and estimate-ordered processing (when a single query is processed by multiple threads) is decided by directly measuring the times of the enabled and disabled paths on calibration queries, and a technique is turned on only when the enabled path is sufficiently faster (hereafter, the enabling rule). These times depend on the rotation of the index, so this amounts to choosing a path by looking at the rotation, yet the guarantee remains intact. The two paths share the visited partitions, the stage configuration, and the error-bound parameters, so on either path a true neighbor is missed only when the estimation error of an unvisited true neighbor exceeds the error-bound parameter ( ζ(ℓ) < −ε0,ℓ ) (Theorem 5). This condition is determined independently of the path, so whichever path is chosen, the failure probability does not exceed 23 the bounds of Theorems 2–4. In our measurements, the configurations chosen by the rule were 1.18 × faster in geometric mean than the configuration with the default first-stage code length and all techniques other than the adaptive radius turned off, over 96 settings on eight 1M datasets, with no setting slowed down; the gains were concentrated on laion, yandex, and SSNPP, where one term of Eq. (12) is large, reaching up to 3.8 × (Section 6.5). The values and procedure of the rule are summarized in the appendix (Appendix C.5). 

# 5. Guarantees under Updates 

In this section, we extend the index and search algorithm of Section 4 to a dynamic environment with ongoing insertions and deletions. The extension adds three components: a delta segment that holds new vectors, a bitmap that marks deletions, and merges that combine the two segments. Update operations reuse the encoding function and the layout function of Algorithm 1, search runs Algorithm 2 unchanged, and neither the implementation of Section 4.2 nor the parameter selection rule of Section 4.3 changes. Without updates, the dynamic index is identical to the index of Section 4. Section 5.1 defines the structure of the dynamic index, gives the conditions the dynamic index must satisfy, shows that the static guarantees are maintained unchanged under these conditions, and shows the risk when updates are chosen after observing search results. Section 5.2 explains what the update operations and search reuse from Section 4 and shows that they satisfy these conditions. Section 5.3 addresses how to maintain an efficient index without affecting the guarantee and explains why the overhead added by the dynamic extension is small. 

# 5.1 Guarantee under Updates 

Like the long-term index, temporary index, and delete list of FreshDiskANN [38], the dynamic index consists of a main segment , a delta segment , and a deletion bitmap (Fig. 4(a)). The main segment is the index built by Algorithm 1; new vectors are inserted into the delta segment, deletions are marked in the bitmap, and accumulated updates are folded into the main segment by merges. As updates continue, new vectors lie farther from their assigned centroids, so residuals grow, and partition sizes become uneven, which raises search cost (Section 3.3). To reverse this, at merges we maintain an efficient index by applying recentering , which moves a centroid to the mean of its live members; split , which divides a partition in two by 2-means on the original vectors; and dissolution , which removes a small partition and moves its members to the nearest other partitions. Hereafter we call these three operations maintenance operations .The procedure for applying the operations at a merge is described in Section 5.2, and the method for choosing them in Section 5.3. A vector not marked as deleted is called a live vector ; let Lt be the set of live vectors at time t, N ∗

> k,t

(q)the set of true k nearest neighbors in it, and Recall t(q) the recall with it as the ground truth. Probabilities are defined as in Section 2.2. We call the insertions and deletions that have occurred from the initial index build up to time t, listed in the order in which they occurred, the update sequence . The times of merges and of rotation changes are determined by values that do not use the rotation, such as vector counts and a predetermined schedule, and are regarded as fixed together with the update sequence. The probability at time t is then taken only over P ,with the data, the update sequence, and the query fixed. This extends the “given query” of the static guarantee to updates, and means that the update sequence and the query are given independently of the rotation. In this frame, for the dynamic index to keep the guarantee, it must satisfy the following three conditions in addition to the conditions of Section 3.2. C5. When centroids and assignments are changed to maintain an efficient index, which partitions are changed and how, and the resulting new centroids and assignments, are determined only from values computed without using the rotation, such as residual norms of original vectors, partition sizes, 2-means on original vectors, and predictions by Eq. (12). The number of vectors passing stage 1 as actually counted by testing under the current rotation (hereafter the observed pass count ) and search results are not used. C6. The two segments use the same rotation and the same centroids, and each stored code is a function only of its vector, the current centroid, and the current rotation. C7. Vectors marked as deleted are used neither as candidates nor for the radius (also when the radius is shrunk while unvisited vectors are tested), and at each time C1, C3, and C4 hold and the radius used for testing is set so as to satisfy C2 or the conditions of Theorem 5. Each condition is needed for the following reasons. If C5 is violated, centroids become functions of the rotation, and the distribution of the test error cannot be computed by Theorem 1. C6 ensures that, even after updates, the stored codes represent residuals with respect to the current centroids. If codes computed with old centroids remain after recentering, the residuals used by the pruning rule and the codes disagree, and the test error does not follow the distribution of Theorem 1. If C7 is violated, the distance of a vector marked as deleted can enter the radius, and the radius can become smaller than the true k-th distance among live vectors. 24 Theorem 6 (Per-Query Guarantee under Updates): In this frame, a dynamic index satisfying C5–C7 has the following properties. (a) For a query at any time t, the result of the dynamic index satisfies, unchanged, the guarantees obtained by applying Theorems 2–5 to a static index with Lt as its data. For example, in the (τ, δ )-recall guarantee mode, 

Pr [Recall t(q) < τ ] ≤ δ (14) 

and the failure probability δ need not be split between the main segment and the delta segment. (b) When the same rotation continues to be used, let Fs and Ft be the failure events of the same query asked at two times s < t ; then 

Pr[ Fs ∪ Ft] ≤ 2δ, and the two events are in general not independent. In particular, if a true neighbor pruned at time 

s remains a true neighbor at time t, its centroid and deterministic slack are unchanged, it is still unvisited and not a target of early exact computation, and the radius at the moment it is tested satisfies rt ≤ rs, then that neighbor is pruned at time t as well. (c) If the rotation is changed on a predetermined schedule at times when all live vectors are re-encoded with a new rotation (e.g., merges), failures at two times that use different rotations are mutually independent (given the live vectors, the centroids, the assignments, the query, and the decisions of the parameter selection rule). 

The proof of Theorem 6 is given in Appendix A.2. Eq. (14) has the same form as Eq. (2), except that recall is measured against N ∗

> k,t

(q), the set of true k nearest neighbors among the vectors live at time t. The proof starts from the following: since the update sequence is given and, by C5, the state at time t (live vectors, centroids, assignments) is independent of the rotation, the visited set is determined independently of P ; and by C7, the radius is set from exact distances of live vectors. Since an unvisited true neighbor is live, by C7 the triangle-inequality range and the test path of its own segment process that vector, and by C6 the premise of Lemma 1 holds. The terms of the union bound and Markov’s inequality are only the k vectors of N ∗

> k,t

(q), so each neighbor is counted only once, whichever segment it resides in. In a simple design that sets the radius of the delta segment to the k-th distance of the main segment’s result, two union bounds are needed, and the failure probability δ must be divided between the two segments. What Theorem 6 shows is that such a split is unnecessary if the radius and the error-bound parameters are set from the whole of Lt from the start. In addition, Theorem 6 identifies the conditions (C5–C7) under which the guarantee is maintained unchanged as updates continue, and distinguishes how failures at different times are related when the same rotation continues to be used and when it is changed. Adaptive updates, in which updates are chosen after observing the rotation or search results, fall outside this frame, and the guarantee can then be broken. An adaptive adversary [85], which chooses inputs after observing the rotation or test results, can insert a new nearest vector of query q into an unvisited partition, choosing it so that Eq. (9) surely prunes it; the failure probability of exact search then becomes 1 (for τ < 1, it suffices to insert m + 1 such vectors). In our synthetic experiment ( D = 32, δ = 0 .05), insertions chosen in this way by observing the rotation caused failures on 67 of 68 queries under that same rotation, but when the same vectors were evaluated under 24 independent rotations, the average failure rate was 0.0049, within δ. Selection bias also arises when decisions made to maintain an efficient index observe the rotation (that is, when C5 is violated). If the targets of recentering or split are chosen by observed pass counts, centroids become functions of the rotation, and if the configuration with the smallest observed pass count is chosen among several candidate configurations, the upper bound on the failure rate is multiplied by the number of candidates. Updates decided from exact-search results (e.g., inserting an upload when no matching copy is found), however, see the rotation only through failures. If the decision depends only on the distances of the results (e.g., whether the nearest distance is at most a threshold) and the deciding queries are exact searches ( τ = 1), a query at time t fails with probability at most (1 + j)δ, where j is the number of earlier queries whose results decided updates since the last rotation change. The update sequence that would arise if every result were exact (the ideal sequence) is a function of the data, the query sequence, and the decision rule alone, so it is independent of the rotation, and every exact result has the same distances and hence yields the same decision. The actual state can therefore depart from the ideal state only if one of the j earlier queries fails on the ideal state, which by Theorem 6 (a) has probability at most δ for each query. A failure at time t is either a failure on the ideal state or such a departure, so the union bound gives (1 + j)δ. If the rotation is replaced at predetermined times, the updates before a replacement form a fixed sequence independent of the new rotation, so only queries after the replacement are counted. Hence a pipeline that, like copy detection, decides insertions from results and makes at most jmax such decisions between merges that replace the rotation can meet a target failure probability by setting δ to the target divided by 1 + jmax . The cost depends on how small δ becomes. The error-bound parameters grow only like √ln(1 /δ ℓ), and a 10 × smaller δ took only 1.08–1.14 × the time (geometric means), but the first-stage pass rate grows faster than these parameters, so on six 1M datasets δ = 10 −4 and 10 −8

took 1.37 × and 2.25 × the 1-thread time of δ = 0 .01 in geometric mean (up to 5.7 ×), still less than that of FAISS IndexFlatL2 in every setting, and even at δ = 10 −8 the time of copy-detection range queries changed by at most 6.2% (Section 6.3). A filtered k-NN query returns the k nearest vectors among those that satisfy a predicate φq on metadata. Let Sq be the live vectors at time t that satisfy φq , and measure recall, Recall φ(q), against the true min( k, |Sq |) nearest neighbors in Sq . No new machinery is needed. 25 Corollary 2 (Filtered Search): Suppose that φq is determined independently of the rotation (by the query, the metadata, and the update sequence, not by the results of searches that use P ), and that the search marks the vectors in Lt \ Sq

in the deletion bitmap, in addition to the deleted vectors, and then runs the dynamic search unchanged. Then, for a dynamic index satisfying C5–C7, (a) every returned vector satisfies φq ; (b) if |Sq | ≤ k, the result is Sq itself, so it is exact; and (c) if |Sq | > k , the guarantees of Theorems 2–5 hold with Sq as the data, for example Pr[Recall φ(q) < τ ] ≤ δ,for any selectivity and without splitting δ among predicates or segments. 

To prove it, append to the update sequence the deletion of Lt \ Sq at time t without a merge. Since φq is independent of P , this sequence is still given independently of P ; deletions only set bits, so C5 and C6 continue to hold; and the filtered search is the search just after time t, whose live set is Sq . Theorem 6 (a) then gives (c), and C7 gives (a). For (b), C2 sets the radius to infinity while fewer than k vectors have been visited, and the search visits partitions until 

k live candidates are collected or the partitions are exhausted, so every vector of Sq is computed exactly. Predicates built from the results of other searches with the same rotation (e.g., a semi-join of two k-NN operators) fall outside the corollary, as do the result-decided updates above. The guarantee does not depend on selectivity, but the cost does: stage 1 still reads the codes of masked vectors, and the radius, the k-th distance within Sq , grows as selectivity falls. Section 6.6 measures this cost and a plan that switches to exact search over Sq when Sq is small. 

# 5.2 Update Operations and Search 

This section describes the update operations and search designed to satisfy C5–C7 of Section 5.1. Algorithm 3 gives the update operations and search of the dynamic index. The main segment is exactly the index of Section 4, and the delta segment is an index of the same format using the same partitions. Insertion (lines 1–4) assigns a new vector to the partition of the nearest current centroid, computes the code and coefficients of that single vector with the encoding function of Algorithm 1 (Section 4.1), inserts them into the delta segment, and then re-lays out only that partition of the delta segment with the layout function. Neither k-means (line 1 of Algorithm 1) nor rotation generation (line 4) is redone, and the codes of other vectors are not changed. When several vectors are inserted together, each vector is assigned and encoded, and then the layout function needs to run only once for each partition of the delta segment that received vectors. Deletion (lines 5–6) ends with marking the bitmap; neither the codes nor the 8-bit quantization ranges of the pruning-rule coefficients kept per partition (line 13 of Algorithm 1) are recomputed. These ranges are the reference for restoring the stored coefficients, and the coefficients of live vectors are quantized within these ranges; so if the ranges are kept, the restored values do not change, and the deterministic slack only becomes slightly larger, to the extent that the interval is wider than necessary. Hence, even without physically removing vectors marked as deleted, the bounds used by the test do not become wrong. The delta segment must be scanned separately in each unvisited partition, and vectors marked as deleted are still read as stage-1 data until they are removed; therefore, when accumulated updates grow, we perform a merge that folds the delta segment into the main segment (lines 7–13, Fig. 4(b)). A merge is performed when the number of vectors in the delta segment plus the number of vectors marked as deleted in the main segment reaches a fixed fraction (the merge threshold ) of the number of live vectors in the main segment. A merge removes vectors marked as deleted and empty partitions, folds the vectors of the delta segment into the partitions of the main segment, and rebuilds the layouts with the layout function (lines 11–12). Codes are not recomputed, since they were already computed with the current centroids at insertion. Since a merge is also the time when layouts are rebuilt, maintenance operations that reduce residuals grown by updates are applied at the same time (line 8). Which operations to apply and when is addressed in Section 5.3. After maintenance operations, only vectors whose assigned centroid changed, that is, vectors of partitions that underwent recentering or split and vectors moved to another partition by dissolution, need to be re-encoded (line 10), so this work is handled together with rebuilding the layouts. Hence a merge skips k-means, rotation generation, and most code computation in Algorithm 1, and its cost consists of the linear time of rebuilding the layouts and the encoding of the vectors changed by maintenance operations. Graph indexes lose search quality unless the connections around deleted nodes are repaired [38], whereas for our index, postponing merges only increases cost. A merge may also change the rotation (lines 9–10). If the same rotation continues to be used, by Theorem 6 (b) the failure of a query may persist after a merge. By Theorem 6 (c), if a new rotation is drawn at each merge on a predetermined schedule and all live vectors are re-encoded, failures in different merge intervals become mutually independent. Since the failure probability at each time is at most δ either way, this choice matters for applications in which the same query is repeated over time, such as continual copy checks, to keep failures from persisting. The price is the cost of re-encoding all live vectors at each merge (Section 6.4). Since the new rotation is drawn according to a predetermined schedule and random sequence, it does not depend on the update contents or search results, and since the choice of maintenance operations does not read the rotation, this choice does not violate C5. The seed of this random sequence must be kept secret from whoever issues queries and updates; anyone who knows it can compute the current and future rotations and choose queries or updates against them, which is the adaptive setting below. Search in the dynamic index (lines 14–15) runs Algorithm 2 unchanged and differs only in the following points (Fig. 5). 26 Algorithm 3: Updates and Search of the Dynamic Index

The visited vectors in line 2 and the test targets in line 7 of Algorithm 2 are restricted to live vectors, and lines 6–9 are performed separately on the main segment and the delta segment in each unvisited partition. If the visited partitions contain fewer than k live vectors, more partitions are visited in the order of Φ; these visits are also independent of the rotation and thus within C2. Since the two segments share the set of partitions, routing (line 1), the initial radius (line 3), and the LUTs (line 4) are computed once per query; the intervals of the two segments are tested with the same radius and the same error-bound parameters, and vectors whose exact distances are computed are put into the same candidate set C and shrink the radius together. Accordingly, the implementation of Section 4.2 (SIMD tests, the slack factor, multithreading, id deduplication) and the early exact computation, estimate-ordered processing, 2-bit final stage, and global centering of Section 4.3 are all used unchanged, and the deletion bitmap is applied once, with a bitwise operation, to the stage-1 SIMD test results. Since vectors marked as deleted are excluded at stage 1, the vectors chosen by early exact computation and the distances used to shrink the radius are all restricted to live vectors (C7). The parameter selection rule (Section 4.3) is applied once on the initial index. Algorithm 3 satisfies C5–C7. The choice of maintenance operations is made by the policy of Section 5.3 only from values computed without using the rotation, so C5 holds. Insertion computes codes with the current centroids and the current rotation (line 3), a merge re-encodes vectors whose assigned centroid changed (line 10), and when a new rotation is used, all live vectors are re-encoded (lines 9–10), so C6 holds. Search excludes vectors marked as deleted from the visited vectors and the test targets (line 15) and shrinks the radius only with live vectors whose exact distances are computed, and is otherwise identical to Algorithm 2; hence C7 holds, together with C1, C3, and C4 and the radius 27 vectors of one unvisited partition, sorted by x in each segment main segment triangle range delta segment triangle range both intervals judged with the same r and ε0

# 5.3 Residual-Based Maintenance 

Since Theorem 6 holds however the index is maintained, as long as C5 is respected, decisions made to maintain an efficient index do not affect the guarantee and change only search cost. This section addresses the principle and policy for choosing the maintenance operations that reduce search cost at the merges of Section 5.2. As Eq. (13) shows, the residual growth mentioned in Section 5.1 shrinks the margin and lets more vectors outside the radius pass stage 1, so the timing of maintenance operations can be set by balancing this cost increase against the cost of the operations. At a merge, for each partition p, one of the candidate maintenance operations α (recentering, split, dissolution, or doing nothing) is chosen. Let QH be the number of queries to be processed until the next merge, 

Cm(α, p ) the cost of applying operation α to partition p, and T (q) and T α,p (q) the search cost of query q before and after the operation, respectively; then the gain obtained by applying operation α to partition p is 

Bp(α) = QH Eq

[T (q) − T α,p (q)] − Cm(α, p ) (15) where Eq is the average over calibration queries and T (q) is the cost predicted by Eq. (12). That is, the gain is the total search cost saved until the next merge minus the cost of the operation. At a merge, the gains of the four candidate operations are computed for each partition, the operation α∗ 

> p

with the largest gain is applied (line 8 of Algorithm 3), and only vectors whose assigned centroid changed are re-encoded (line 10). Doing nothing has no cost and does not change search cost, so its gain is 0; hence, if the gains of the other three operations are all 0 or less, partition p is left as is. Since the inputs of Eq. (12) are independent of the rotation, T α,p can be predicted before the operation is applied, without reading the rotation. Computing the gain in Eq. (15) requires predicting the search cost T α,p after the operation before the operation is applied, and maintenance policies are distinguished by this prediction method. We call policy R the policy that regards the number of vectors passing stage 1 in a partition as proportional to a power of the partition’s mean residual, with the exponent measured per dataset, and that predicts T α,p by approximating the residual after a split with Zador’s law of quantization error [86]; we compare it in Section 6.4. Our default policy (policy K) predicts the cost of unvisited partitions with a light approximation of Eq. (12), so its pass-rate prediction has no data-fitted constants. LIRE [39], which splits partitions according to a size threshold and merges small partitions into their neighbors, uses partition size as the maintenance signal. Using the residual (reconstruction error) as the maintenance signal also appears in Ada-IVF [40]. Our policy takes as its signal the margin (Eq. (13)), which directly determines cost; because it predicts the pass rate by a formula with no data-fitted constants, it need not be refitted when the data change, and because it separates maintenance from the guarantee, a wrong prediction does not increase the failure probability. It did not, however, search faster than a size-based policy (Section 6.4). Policy K predicts T α,p by approximating Eq. (12) on sample vectors drawn from each partition. As the radius it uses the initial radii of calibration queries (C2); it approximates the distribution of κ, which is determined only by D

and m1 (Section 3.3), by a few representative values and GD by a normal distribution, and it omits the deterministic slack. This prediction uses neither the rotation, the codes, nor observed pass counts. Candidate operations are limited to partitions changed since the last merge and small partitions, and the cost of visited partitions is computed from partition sizes. For the constants of the maintenance cost Cm(α, p ) (the time to encode one vector and the time of 2-means), we use values measured once on the initial index with a separate rotation independent of the index, and the 1-bit and 2-bit encoding of vectors whose centroids were changed by maintenance operations is done all at once at the merge. A single insertion consists of finding the nearest centroid, encoding, and re-laying out the delta interval of that partition, and modifies neither the codes of other vectors nor the connections of the index. A deletion changes one bit. In search, 28 the only additions are scanning one more interval, that of the delta segment, in each unvisited partition and applying the deletion bitmap to the stage-1 test results with a bitwise operation, and the delta segment stays below the merge threshold. A merge skips most of Algorithm 1 (Section 5.2). Moreover, since the guarantee is maintained unchanged as updates continue (Theorem 6), there is no need to retune parameters or re-check recall with calibration queries after updates. An index tuned for average recall sees per-query failures increase as updates continue and must be retuned (Fig. 1(b)). On the official streaming runbook of Section 6.4, the time of the last merge was 0.12 times the time to rebuild from scratch, and the search speed of the maintained index was 0.81–1.16 times that of an index rebuilt from scratch at the same point. 

# 6. Experiments 

In this section, we answer the following questions. Is the guarantee of our method, PQR-Search, actually kept (Sec-tion 6.2)? How much faster is it than existing methods that give the same guarantee (Section 6.3)? What happens to the guarantee and cost under updates (Section 6.4)? Is the pass-rate prediction of the cost law accurate, how much do the centroids and each component contribute to cost, and does the parameter selection rule work on unseen data as well (Section 6.5)? Finally, we present results by application (copy detection and database operators), scale, and dimension (Section 6.6). 

# 6.1 Setup 

All experiments were run alone on a server with an AMD Threadripper PRO 9975WX (32 cores, 64 threads, 128 MB L3) and 256 GB of memory. Table 9 lists the datasets used and the application each represents. Reflecting the applications mentioned in Section 1, we centered the datasets on SSNPP [25], a copy detection benchmark; laion, yandex, and imagenet-align [1], for cross-modal search that finds images from text; and hotpotqa [1], which finds documents from real user questions. Cross-modal queries and real questions differ in distribution from the data vectors [1], so they are real cases in which an index tuned for average recall meets a distribution gap between queries and data. In addition, for comparison with prior work, we also used SIFT and GIST [12], the image retrieval dataset YFCC [53], and the text datasets arxiv, agnews, and msmarco [1], which use held-out documents as queries. imagenet-align was never used in designing or calibrating the parameter selection rule and served only to verify generalization; LAION1M was used for the comparison under the same conditions as the CTR paper [43], and msturing-10M, a web search embedding dataset, for the official NeurIPS’23 streaming runbook. We also added the widely used benchmarks DEEP1M [25] and GloVe [87], and for the verification at scale we used DEEP-100M, MSTuring-100M, and SIFT100M, which take the first 100M vectors each of DEEP, MSTuring, and BIGANN (SIFT) of the Big-ANN’21 benchmark [25]. All ground-truth distances were computed in fp64. For SIFT, arxiv, and msmarco, which have published ground truth, we recomputed fp64 distances for the ids of the published ground truth and re-sorted them (confirming that the k-th distance matches that of fp64 exhaustive search); for the others we used fp64 exhaustive search. For GIST and agnews, the published ground truth had queries that missed a closer point at nearly the same distance at the boundary (6 and 2 queries, respectively, at k = 100), so we used exhaustive search.
