The settings of our method are the same on all datasets. A query visits the two partitions with the smallest Φ (nprobe = 2); the candidates for the stage-1 code length m1 are 128 bits, 256 bits, and D bits, and the default is 256 bits if the stage-1 pass rate predicted by the formula of Section 4.3 exceeds 5%, and 128 bits otherwise. For example, the m1 candidates for laion ( D = 512) are 128, 256, and 512 bits. One partition is created per 1,000 vectors, with a size cap of 1.1 times the mean and the 8 nearest centroids as assignment candidates. At 100M scale, k-means training is reduced to 64 samples per centroid and 10 iterations, the 8 nearest centroids of each vector are found with HNSW [9] built over the centroids, and FAISS IndexIVFFlat also uses the same centroids and assignment. The merge threshold of the dynamic index is 10% of the number of live vectors in the main segment; when choosing maintenance operations, the number of queries until the next merge, QH , is assumed to be 10 queries per updated vector, and policy K uses 64 calibration queries and 32 samples per partition. These values are unrelated to the guarantee and change only cost (the remaining values are in Appendix C.5). Baselines with the same kind of guarantee are FAISS [8] IndexFlatL2, Tribase [51], and PDX-BOND [54] for exact search and CTR [43] and ConANN [42] for distribution-level guarantees, and we compare with them only at the same guarantee level. IndexFlatL2 was used with its default settings (pip wheel faiss-cpu 1.15.1), and a batch of 1,000 queries is processed through the matrix-multiplication path of the OpenBLAS bundled with the wheel. In addition, FAISS IndexIVFFlat serves as a reference for measuring the cost of the guarantee. The number of partitions is max(1000, n/1000), and nprobe was set in two ways. One is the minimum value that answers all test queries exactly (for the expected-recall guarantee, the minimum for which every test query has recall at least τ ), hereafter called the oracle .The oracle is chosen by looking at the test ground truth and thus cannot be obtained in actual operation; it represents the post-hoc optimal cost for IVF to get only the test queries right without a per-query guarantee. Using such a post-hoc optimal configuration as a reference rather than as a competitor is also done in related work [16], [37]. The other is a value set with separate calibration queries, which corresponds to actual operation but has no per-query guarantee. The latency of processing a single query with 32 threads was also measured with settings provided by FAISS. IndexFlatL2 automatically uses a path that splits the data across threads when the number of queries is smaller than the number of threads; bound by memory bandwidth, it was 2.0–2.5 times faster with 32 threads than with 1 thread. IndexIVFFlat by default parallelizes over queries and processes a single query on one thread, so we used the setting that parallelizes over the partitions to be scanned (parallel_mode = 1). At 100M scale, FAISS 1.15.1 IndexIVFFlat returned wrong results with its default setting at 32 threads, so batch was measured with the setting that parallelizes over queries only when there are multiple queries (parallel_mode = 3). For CTR [43], ConANN [42], and Tribase [51], we used the official code (ConANN built with AVX-512, as is our method), and the numbers of calibration queries and tuning queries of ConANN followed the official settings (5,000 and 1,000). PDX-BOND [54], the exact search of the PDX vertical layout, prunes a vector once its partial distance reaches the current k-th distance. It has been removed from the current official PDX repository, so we built the last official version that contains it (Python package pdxearch 0.3, commit 4a2e65e) with the official build procedure (Clang 18, -O3 -march=native) and used its official Python API, which processes one query at a time on a single thread. ANN methods without guarantees are not baselines with the same guarantee; they serve to 30 expose per-query failures and the resulting wrong operator answers (Sections 2.1, 6.4, and 6.6) and, timed in the same run, to price the guarantee (end of Section 6.3). For static indexes, we used the standard FAISS [8] implementations of three representative families: partition-based IVF-Flat, graph-based HNSW32 ( M = 32, efConstruction 200), and quantization-based IVF-RaBitQ (including a stage that re-ranks with exact distances). For the update experiments, we used only implementations that support insertion and deletion through a standard API. Since FAISS HNSW and IVF-RaBitQ with the re-ranking stage do not support deletion, for updates involving deletions we used hnswlib [9], which implements the same algorithm, instead of HNSW, excluded IVF-RaBitQ, and added FreshDiskANN [38], a graph index designed for updates. We also ran Quake [37], which is designed to meet a recall target under updates, with its public implementation. For Quake, we gave a recall target of 0.99 to its feature that determines the number of partitions to scan per query (APS) and invoked its maintenance at each step. However, with the default value (10%) of the fraction of partitions that APS initially takes as candidates, the average recall on out-of-distribution queries stayed at 0.92, so we set this fraction by the same rule as for the other methods (the smallest value for which the average recall of the calibration queries is at least 0.99), which gave 50%. Since average recall is dominated by easy queries [18], we do not use it as the main metric. Moreover, since the probability of the guarantee is over the rotation of the index, not over the query distribution, measuring the fraction of queries that meet the target in a single run [16], [42], [43] cannot verify a per-query guarantee. Therefore, to verify the guarantee, we use new rotations and test queries not used in the design, and report the failure fraction measured for each query over several independent rotations and the number of queries whose Clopper–Pearson lower bound [91] on the per-query failure rate exceeds δ (significant violations). Hereafter this procedure is called an audit . Whether a result is exact was judged strictly by whether the j-th of the sorted result distances is at most the true j-th distance (distances re-measured in fp64, with a relative tolerance of 10 −9 on squared distances). For contrast with methods without guarantees, we use the fraction of queries that fall short of the target and the recall of the worst query. The fraction of queries that fall short of the target is 1 minus Robustness-δ@K [19], which measures robustness as the fraction of queries whose recall is at least a threshold, and that threshold corresponds to our τ (the δ in this name is a recall threshold, different from our failure probability δ). For timing we used 1,000 distinct test queries (300 of them only for the 1-thread latency at 10M scale and above and on hotpotqa), because a batch that repeats the same queries shares partition visits and inflates throughput. Batch throughput is the median over 3 rounds of the median of measurements taken, after one warm-up run, at least 5 times and until 1 s accumulates, and the order of the compared methods was changed in each round. The batch time of our method includes the time to sort the queries in the order of their nearest centroids. Measured in the original unsorted order, the geometric mean of the main results was 26.7 ×, almost the same as the 26.4 × that includes the sorting time. Latency is the median time of processing the queries one at a time. Since FAISS’s per-query time is nearly constant, its latency was measured with 50 (1-thread) and 100 (32-thread) queries sampled evenly from the 1,000, and with 10 and 20 at 5M scale and above. At 10M scale and on hotpotqa, the IVF times were measured in only 1 round. Of the three metrics of Section 4.3, the two that use multiple threads are measured with 32 threads, and the three are hereafter called 1-thread, 32-thread, and batch, respectively. Most related work reports only 1-thread latency [14], [41], [43], but in interactive applications it is natural to share cores to reduce the response time of a single query, so we also measured 32-thread latency. The thread count for batch was set to 32 after measuring the throughput of our method and FAISS at 24–64 threads, as the count closest to peak throughput for both methods, and the FAISS batch was also measured in the same run with the same thread count (the selection procedure is in Appendix C.5). The Python binding of FAISS installs an interrupt callback at import, which makes the HNSW batch search process queries in chunks of about 10 8/(levels · d · efSearch), so at high dimension and large efSearch only some of the threads work. We therefore re-timed the HNSW32 batches of Tables 15, 13, and 17 in a separate run with the callback disabled, as in the C++ library; this raised their throughput by up to 4.9 × (msmarco, efSearch 4,096) and left the returned results bit-identical. The callback did not affect IndexIVFFlat, IVF-RaBitQ, or single-query latency. 

# 6.2 Verification of the Guarantees 

Table 11 summarizes the results of verifying the guarantees (in tables and figures, the exact-search guarantee is abbre-viated as T1 and the expected-recall guarantee with τ = 0 .9 as E). Audits were performed by running the rule-selected configuration used for measurement on the processing path of that metric (batch, 32-thread, 1-thread). The dynamic index was audited with all techniques of Section 4.3 enabled and timed with the configuration chosen by the enabling rule. The two configurations are paths of Theorem 5 that use the same error-bound parameters, so their guarantees are the same. The failure rates of the exact-search guarantee ( δ = 0 .01 , 0.1) were at most δ in all settings, and on static indexes the setting with the highest failure rate was at 16% of δ. Over the 168 settings in which the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee was verified on static indexes (120 from ten 1M datasets and 48 from the 10M-scale datasets and hotpotqa; 504 processing paths, among which 198 distinct configurations were chosen by the rule; 10 or 4 rotations × 1,000 queries), the total number of failures was 45 of 4,176,000, at most 0.5% of δ per configuration. For the expected-recall guarantee, not a single query had a rotation-averaged recall below τ . To check Corollary 1, we also tested the per-query 31 rotation-averaged recall in the same runs of the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee mode. In all of the 168 static settings and the 36 LAION1M settings, no query had an average below τ , and the per-query average was at least 0.025 above τ even in the lowest case (at least 0.06 on 1M data). On LAION1M, under the same conditions as the CTR paper, we tested up to significant violations with 30 rotations and 4,000 queries (0 violations). In the settings with 100M scale, with 

δ = 0 .001, and with varied nprobe and partition size, the failure rates were also all at most δ. For the dynamic index as well, the failure rate was at most δ at every search step and every time point (for the exact-search guarantee, the maximum at a single time point was 7 of 3,000, 23% of δ).

The failure rates observed in the verification were at most δ in all settings, and the gap from δ varied by dataset. On static indexes, the settings closest to δ were on SSNPP and laion, where the adaptive radius sits close to the true k-th distance (at most 16% of δ for the exact-search guarantee). When the radius is large, a true neighbor clears the pruning threshold by a wide margin and survives even if the error satisfies ζ(ℓ) < −ε0,ℓ , but when the radius is close to the true 

k-th distance, true neighbors lie just above the pruning threshold, so such an error leads directly to pruning. Hence the observation that measured failure rates are smaller than δ stems from radii larger than the true k-th distance, for which the bound is not attained, not from a miscalibrated error-bound parameter, and it must not be used as grounds for relaxing the nominal δ. On the other hand, since 10 rotations cannot test an upper bound on the failure rate of each individual query, on 1M data we compared the number of significant violations expected if every query’s true failure rate were exactly δ (256 for the exact-search guarantee, 3,071 for the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee) with the actual number (0). For the 10M-scale datasets and hotpotqa, measured with 4 rotations, and for δ = 0 .001, even a query that failed only once has a lower bound on its failure rate exceeding δ (0.0127 > 0.01 with 4 rotations, 0.0051 > 0.001 with 10), 32 0.01 0.1 0.5 Nominal ±

> (b) Exact ( ¿= 1) ¿= 0.9 ¿= 0.8 ¿= 0.5 Dynamic Figure 6. Verification of the guarantees. (a) Nominal δversus measured failure rate (the 0 band at the bottom marks settings with no failures). (b) Target τof the expected-recall guarantee versus the minimum per-query average recall.

so instead of this test we report only the failure rates. Per-query upper bounds were checked separately on laion and SSNPP, where failure rates are closest to δ (exact-search guarantee, k ∈ { 10 , 100 }, the rule’s 1-thread path). Auditing every one of the 1,000 test queries with 1,000 fresh rotations, 1,567 of 4,000,000 trials failed, and no query failed more than 5 times (rate 0.005). The one-sided 95% Clopper–Pearson upper bound was below δ for 3,998 of the 4,000 query– k pairs; the other two were queries that failed 5 times (upper bound 0.0105). There was no significant violation, where 47.9 per setting would be expected if every query failed with probability exactly δ, and no single rotation made more than 0.4% of the queries fail. The 100 hard queries selected in advance from the 10 rotations of the earlier audit failed only 1.07–1.40 × as often as all queries, and contained only 0–3 of the 10 queries that failed most often over the 1,000 rotations: a few rotations cannot identify hard queries in advance. Whether the audit can detect violations was checked with a positive control, a synthetic placement where the bound is attained. Running the placement of Proposition 1 ( D = 64 with the exact-search guarantee for k = 1 , 10 and the (0 .9, 0.01)-recall guarantee for k = 10; D = 256 with the exact-search guarantee for k = 100) through the same kernel and audit code over 100,000 rotations, the failure rate for δ = 0 .01 was 0.95–0.97%, just below the bound. When the error-bound parameters were deliberately reduced to the values for δ′ = 0 .02 and 0 .05, the failure rates rose to 1.87–1.98% and 4.69–4.86%, and the audit clearly exposed the violation of δ = 0 .01. Thus the failure rates far below δ

on real data come from radii larger than the true k-th distance, not from an insensitive audit. Fig. 6 shows the same audit results against the nominal δ. The failure rates measured in the audits of static indexes (one point per setting and processing path) and in the two runbooks of the dynamic index were all below the diagonal, that is, below the nominal δ, and for τ < 1 most settings had no failures (Fig. 6(a)). For the expected-recall guarantee as well, the minimum per-query average recall (the average over rotations) was at least the target τ in all settings (Fig. 6(b), settings with 10 or more rotations). Even when the queries were divided by difficulty, failures were not concentrated on hard queries. Difficulty was measured by the relative contrast RC k [92], the mean distance divided by the distance to the k-th neighbor; the smaller it is, the harder the query [1], [18]. When the queries of eight 1M datasets were divided into deciles of RC k, the per-decile failure rates of the exact-search guarantee ( δ = 0 .01) were all at most 0.088%, including the hardest decile (Clopper–Pearson upper bound at most 0.17%), and for the (0 .9, 0.01)-recall guarantee there were no failures in any decile. 

# 6.3 Cost under the Same Guarantee 

We first compared our method with FAISS for exact search (T1) and the expected-recall guarantee (E, τ = 0 .9). In 52 settings combining ten 1M datasets and three 10M datasets with k ∈ { 10 , 100 }, our method was faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 in every setting. The speedup was 3.3–88.8 × (1-thread), 13.8–303 × (32-thread), and 5.8–73.4 × (batch), with geometric means of 22.9 ×, 53.3 ×, and 26.4 ×, respectively. Among them, the 26 settings of the expected-recall guarantee were 12.7–88.8 × (1-thread), 22.7–303 × (32-thread), and 10.3–73.4 × (batch). Our method was also faster in all 52 settings of the main comparison than IndexIVFFlat with the oracle nprobe , the post-hoc optimal reference (1.2–64.4 × 1-thread, 2.6–98.1 × 32-thread, 13.5–486 × batch); k = 1 and the 100M scale, where this relation partly reverses, are discussed in Section 6.6. This is because a fixed nprobe must serve the hardest test query while the work of our method adapts to each query, and IVF batches scan lists per query without BLAS, so they were even slower than IndexFlatL2 batches. Table 13 gives the absolute latency and throughput behind these ratios. The batches of FAISS IndexFlatL2 use the OpenBLAS bundled with its pip wheel; an exhaustive search based on matrix multiplication that we optimized ourselves had 1.5–8.4 × its batch throughput, and our method was 1.03–37 × faster in batch than this baseline in every setting except laion ( k = 100, T1), where the baseline was 1.2 × faster (Appendix C.1). search on a GPU was faster in batch at 1M scale, where the data fit in GPU memory (Appendix C.1). Fig. 7 shows the 26 T1 settings (numbers in Appendix Table 33). Bars show the speedup over FAISS IndexFlatL2 and markers the speedup over IndexIVFFlat with the oracle nprobe ; every bar and marker exceeds 1. The smallest speedup was 3.3 ×

on SSNPP-1M ( k = 100, 1-thread), followed by 3.7 × on GloVe ( k = 100). SSNPP has a high intrinsic dimension, that is, the dimension of the space over which the data actually spread, so the distance gap between true neighbors and other vectors is small and it is hard to filter vectors with the 1-bit test. The median local intrinsic dimension [93], estimated from the distances to the 20 nearest neighbors of a query, was 96 on SSNPP versus 16–52 on the other seven 1M datasets measured the same way, and the fraction of vectors whose exact distances were computed was at most 21% on SSNPP, at most 7.6% on GloVe, and at most 1.3% on the other datasets (100% for FAISS IndexFlatL2). In our T1 results, 2–3 of 26,000 queries, depending on the processing path, were not exact (1 of the 21,800 queries in 1-thread latency), and FAISS IndexFlatL2 (fp32) also failed to answer 22 queries exactly when ties are judged strictly. An nprobe chosen with calibration queries gives no per-query guarantee. In 29 of the 52 settings (14 T1, 15 E), the calibrated nprobe differed from the oracle, and in 9 T1 and 15 E settings among them it answered test queries incorrectly (T1 numbers in Appendix Table 33; two T1 settings caused by ties in fp32 distances are excluded). For example, on arxiv ( k = 10), it failed to answer 17 of the 1,000 test queries exactly. Since nprobe is fixed, these queries fail every time, and nothing bounds the fraction of queries it fails in another workload. Our method can also fail the same query repeatedly while one transformation is in use, but under the exact-search guarantee (T1), in any workload chosen without looking at P the expected fraction of failing queries is at most δ, and under the expected-recall guarantee (E) every query has expected recall at least τ . Our method uses the same calibration queries only for parameter selection, and its guarantee holds separately for each query. IVF with the calibrated nprobe had shorter latency than our method in three settings on YFCC-10M (exact search with k = 10, expected-recall guarantee with k = 10 and 100; ours at 0.44–0.83 × at 1 thread, and 0.83 × at 32 threads only for the expected-recall guarantee with k = 10), but in all three the calibrated nprobe failed test queries (12, 7, and 3). Since the oracle may be unfavorable to IVF, we also measured the smallest nprobe for which at most δ (10) of the 1,000 test queries miss the target (the δ-oracle; for the expected-recall settings, a post-hoc ( τ = 0 .9, δ = 0 .01) criterion). The δ-oracle scanned up to 4 × (T1) and 12 × (E) fewer partitions than the oracle; our method was faster in batch in all 52 settings (5.7–248 ×, geometric means 43.6 × for T1 and 27.3 ×

for E) and in every metric, including 1- and 32-thread latency, in 49 settings. In the remaining three, the YFCC-10M settings above, the δ-oracle IVF had shorter latency (ours 0.44–0.84 ×). 34 0 50 100 Share of 1-thread time (%) SIFT YFCC agnews arxiv GIST yandex SSNPP laion DEEP1M GloVe YFCC-10M SSNPP-10M msmarco (a) 0 50 100 Share of 1-thread time (%) (b) Stage-1 judgment Later stages Exact distances Routing, initial radius Other Figure 9. 1-thread time breakdown for exact search ( δ = 0 .01). (a) k = 10. (b) k = 100. 

Because our method scans a different amount for each query depending on the test outcomes, its time varies across queries, unlike FAISS IndexFlatL2, whose query cost is nearly constant (coefficient of variation of per-query time at most 1.9%) (Fig. 8). Over the 52 settings, the p99/p50 of 1-thread latency was 1.09–4.86 for the exact-search guarantee and 1.04–3.73 for the expected-recall guarantee, with long tails for out-of-distribution queries (yandex, laion) and msmarco. Still, all 43,600 queries were faster than with FAISS IndexFlatL2, and the minimum per-query speedup was 1.06 × (GloVe, k = 100). The ranking of query times nearly matched the ranking of the number of vectors that passed the first stage (median Spearman rank correlation 0.97), and among difficulty measures, query time correlated most strongly with RC k (median −0.85). Among queries within the same dataset, the correlation with the local intrinsic dimension was weak (median 0.43), consistent with the observation of [18] that the local intrinsic dimension correlates only weakly with empirical difficulty because of estimation noise. Breaking down the 1-thread query time by stage, the first-stage test took a median of 61% (34–88%), the rotation of the query a median of 0.5% (up to 7% on agnews with 1,024 dimensions), and exact distance computation a median of 2.6%. Fig. 9 shows the 26 exact-search settings. Since the first-stage test filters out most unvisited vectors, exact distance computation takes only a few percent of the time, except in settings where the test is hard, such as SSNPP and GloVe ( k = 100), and exact search on DEEP1M (k = 100) (25%).

The results were the same for the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee with τ < 1. In 144 settings combining τ ∈ { 0.9, 0.8, 0.5},

δ ∈ { 0.01 , 0.1}, and k ∈ { 10 , 100 } on the ten 1M datasets, SSNPP-10M, and hotpotqa, our method was 5.5–72.5 ×

(1-thread), 18.4–192 × (32-thread), and 6.7–61.7 × (batch) faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 (medians 24.6 ×, 46.0 ×, and 23.4 ×). The gain over exact search with the same δ depended on the query distribution. For in-distribution queries, it was small at 1-thread, 0.99–1.08 × for k = 10 and 1.05–1.25 × for k = 100, but for real out-of-distribution queries and hard data with k = 100, it was large: 1.26–1.52 × on laion, 1.58–2.23 × on SSNPP, 1.73–2.49 × on GloVe, and 1.09–1.56 ×

on yandex. Fig. 10 shows this relation as the speedup versus the target τ , which corresponds to the recall–throughput curve of methods without guarantees. Lowering τ from 1 to 0.5 increased the geometric-mean speedup by 1.05–1.16 ×

for in-distribution queries and by 1.18–1.70 × for out-of-distribution queries and hard data. The gain is small for in-distribution queries because the error-bound parameter grows only like √ln(1 /δ ℓ) as the per-stage share δℓ set by τ and 35 0.5 0.8 0.9 1Target ¿ (± = 0.01) 310 30 100 Speedup over FAISS Flat (1 thread)                 

> (a) 0.5 0.8 0.9 1Target ¿(±= 0.01)
> (b) In-distribution queries OOD / hard queries Figure 10. 1-thread speedup over FAISS IndexFlatL2 versus the target τ(δ= 0 .01). Thin lines are datasets and thick lines geometric means. (a) k= 10. (b) k= 100.

δ shrinks, and on such data the first-stage pass rate is already low, so most of the time is spent in the first-stage scan, which does not depend on the error-bound parameter (Fig. 9). For the same reason, reducing the failure probability to δ = 0 .001 increased the cost only slightly. Our method was faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 in all 16 exact-search settings on eight 1M datasets (the smallest value being 2.4 × at 1-thread on SSNPP, k = 100), and its time was 1.08–1.14 × that for δ = 0 .01 in geometric mean. Smaller δ, which a pipeline that decides updates from search results may need (Section 5.1), costs more on some data. On six 1M datasets (SIFT, GIST, laion, SSNPP, arxiv, and yandex; 12 exact-search settings, with the configuration and techniques reselected by the rule for each δ), δ = 10 −4, 10 −6, and 10 −8 took 1.37 ×, 1.81 ×, and 2.25 × the 1-thread time of δ = 0 .01 in geometric mean (batch 1.32 ×, 1.66 ×, and 2.02 ×). From δ = 0 .01 to 10 −8 the error-bound parameter grew only 1.6–1.9 ×, but the first-stage pass rate grew 2.9–8.3 ×, so the time grew most where many vectors lie near the pruning threshold: at 1 thread, 1.2–2.3 × on SIFT, GIST, and arxiv and 2.1–5.7 × on laion, SSNPP, and yandex (the largest on SSNPP, k = 10). Even at δ = 10 −8, every setting was faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 (at least 1.46 ×, at 1 thread on SSNPP with k = 100, against the IndexFlatL2 times of the main comparison). For the copy-detection range queries on SSNPP-1M and SSNPP-10M (Section 6.6), the time at δ = 10 −8 was within 6.2% of that at δ = 0 .01 for all three configurations, because fewer than 0.07% of the vectors passed the first stage and the first-stage scan, which does not depend on δ, dominated. Audits cannot check such small 

δ; the guarantee rests on the computation of the error-bound parameter, whose tail probability matched its target to a relative error of 4 × 10 −13 in a numerical check. We compared our method with CTR, another method with guarantees, in two ways. We used SIFT and GIST, which the CTR paper [43] used in its evaluation, and arxiv, a text-embedding dataset. First, at the same τ with our δ set equal to CTR’s α, CTR’s per-query shortfall fraction exceeded α in 10 of the 27 settings on SIFT, arxiv, and GIST (up to 16.4% on GIST), whereas the failure rate of our ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee was at most 0.2% of δ in every setting (20 rotations × 1,000 queries, 20 failures in 1,620,000). Next, matching the loss target E[max(0 , τ − Recall( q))] ≤ α(1 − τ )that the CTR paper guarantees, we set our method to the Ectr guarantee, that is, the expected-recall guarantee with target 1 − α(1 − τ ) (since max(0 , τ − Recall( q)) ≤ 1 − Recall( q), this expected-recall guarantee ensures CTR’s loss target for each query). In the 27 settings, no query of ours had an expected per-query shortfall significantly exceeding the target (one of 27,000 queries exceeded it in point estimate, with a shortfall of 0.01, but it was within the target under the one-sided interval over 20 rotations), whereas 0–16.4% of CTR’s queries exceeded the target, and in five settings even the query average exceeded the target (Fig. 1(c)). The 1-thread time depended on the data. In median latency, CTR, for which a query that passes certification finishes with a single HNSW search, was 2.6–3.2 × faster on arxiv; the two were similar on SIFT (CTR at 0.89–1.31 × ours); and our method was 23.9–86.0 × faster on GIST (CTR was 1.4 ×

faster in only one setting). In mean latency, which includes the rectification time of queries that fail certification, CTR was at 0.33–4.79 × ours (arxiv), 0.90–20.2 × (SIFT), and 23.5–85.0 × (GIST). On LAION1M from the CTR paper [43] (settings with filters were excluded), our failure rate was at most δ over the entire range of τ from 0.65 to 0.9 (30 rotations × 4,000 queries, tied queries excluded, 20 failures in 12,846,600), whereas 4.8–11.4% of CTR’s queries fell below τ and the worst query’s recall was 0. The main cause was queries unreachable in the HNSW graph (about 4%), and even excluding them, CTR’s shortfall was 1.1–8.0%. CTR’s median latency was 0.38–0.44 ms in most settings, shorter than our 1-thread latency (0.64–1.44 ms), but its mean latency (0.38–95.9 ms) was 1.9–48.2 × ours at τ of 0.8 and 0.85, and 0.29–66.6 × ours over all settings. For exact search, we also compared our method on eight 1M datasets with Tribase [51], public software that gives a deterministic guarantee. For Tribase, we built an index with √n partitions that supports all three prunings, searched all partitions, and used the fastest of the seven pruning combinations on the test queries (latency is the median over 50 and 100 queries, as for the FAISS baselines). This condition favors Tribase. With ties judged strictly, Tribase failed to answer 4 of the 16,000 queries processed in batch exactly, all caused by ties in fp32 distances, the same level as FAISS IndexFlatL2. Its speed, however, was only 0.74–1.41 × that of FAISS IndexFlatL2 at 1-thread and 0.11–0.77 ×

in batch. The 1-thread gain of triangle-inequality pruning by centroid distances was 1.9–2.2 × on SIFT but 0.99–1.09 ×

36 on the other datasets, where it rarely took effect, and Tribase processes each query in a single thread and does not group distances into matrix multiplications even in batch. As a result, our method was faster than Tribase in all 16 settings: 3.8–63.6 × (1-thread, geometric mean 22.6 ×), 34.7–371 × (32-thread), and 35.9–268 × (batch). This result, that distances concentrate in high dimensions so that centroid distances alone can hardly filter vectors, is the same reason this paper adds the 1-bit test to the triangle-inequality bounds of residual norms. We also compared with PDX-BOND [54] on the eight 1M datasets with at least 128 dimensions (its Python API rejects fewer), in the same runs as our method and FAISS IndexFlatL2. We report BOND over an IVF index whose ⌈√n⌉

buckets are all visited in centroid-distance order, as in the official example, which was its fastest exact configuration in our runs, and BOND without an index. Under the strict tie rule, 19 of the 16,000 queries were not exact, almost all from fp32 rounding at the k-th distance as with IndexFlatL2 (22 queries). On our server PDX-BOND was only 1.16 × faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 at 1 thread in geometric mean (0.84–1.62 ×), and our method was faster than it in all 16 settings: 18.3 × at 1 thread (geometric mean, 4.3–35.7 ×; 22.4 × against BOND without an index), with the smallest ratios on SSNPP and laion with k = 100. Since PDX processes one query on one thread, we compared our 32-thread latency with its 1-thread latency (ours 98.6 × shorter) and our batch throughput with 32 times its sequential throughput, an ideal scaling that it does not provide (ours 14.1 × higher, 6.1–22.8 ×). PDX’s ADSampling search, which gives no guarantee, was faster than PDX-BOND but still 5.7 × slower than our method at 1 thread, and it answered 14–373 of the 1,000 queries inexactly in every setting. We also compared with the simplest configuration that gives the same guarantee. By Theorem 5, if RaBitQ’s [14] IVF searches all partitions and only its error-bound parameter is replaced by the value of the exact-search guarantee, it satisfies the exact-search guarantee even when the initial radius is infinite and the radius is reduced by the exact distances of re-ranked vectors. We built this configuration inside the implementation of our method — no visited partitions (infinite initial radius), no triangle-inequality range, slack factor f = 1, a single test stage with the full code, and all optimization techniques off (hereafter the RaBitQ-equivalent configuration) — and measured it on the 26 exact-search settings of the main comparison in the same runs as our method. For batch, the baseline was the faster of scanning all partitions in centroid-distance order per query and the partition-wise processing of our method. The RaBitQ-equivalent configuration also kept the exact-search guarantee in every setting (0 queries not exact) and was faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 by 12.1 × (1-thread), 20.5 × (32-thread), and 18.3 × (batch) in geometric mean. That is, the per-query guarantee framework alone already turns RaBitQ codes into a guaranteed method much faster than exhaustive search. Our method was again faster than this configuration by 1.64 × (1-thread, 1.01–4.42 ×), 2.33 ×

(32-thread, 1.38–5.76 ×), and 1.24 × (batch, 0.86–2.12 ×) in geometric mean. On SIFT, SSNPP, and SSNPP-10M, where both configurations use the same single stage with the full code, the 1-thread gain of 1.44–1.72 × is the effect of visited partitions, the triangle-inequality range, the slack factor, and the optimization techniques (including global centering on SSNPP); on high-dimensional embeddings (msmarco 2.86–4.42 ×, arxiv 1.91–2.91 ×, agnews, GIST) the multi-stage codes read from the front added a larger gain. In contrast, on the lower-dimensional YFCC, yandex, DEEP1M, and GloVe the 1-thread gain was small (1.01–1.31 ×), and on YFCC ( k = 100) batch our method was slower (0.86 ×). The 32-thread comparison is unfavorable to the RaBitQ-equivalent configuration, because with an infinite initial radius several threads test the first partitions together before the radius shrinks. The official implementation of RaBitQ [14] (C++, all partitions searched, with the same partitions and error-bound parameter) was 1.88 × (1.00–3.81 ×) slower than this configuration at 1 thread in geometric mean (our method was 3.07 ×, 1.74–5.61 ×, faster than it), so the comparison with the RaBitQ-equivalent configuration is the one less favorable to our method. The official implementation also had at most δ queries not exact in each setting (0–4, at most 0.4%), but its bound does not include the error of its 4-bit query quantization or of its fp32 arithmetic. To separate what our method adds to the RaBitQ-equivalent configuration, we started from it (L0) and enabled the components one at a time: the initial radius from visited partitions (L1), the triangle-inequality window (L2), the slack factor f < 1 (L3), prefix-truncated multi-stage codes (L4), and the remaining techniques chosen by the rule (2-bit last stage, global centering, early exact computation, and estimate-ordered processing; L5). Over all 26 T1 settings, L5 was faster than L0 by a geometric mean of 1.64 × at 1 thread (1.01–4.53 ×), 2.33 × at 32 threads (1.33–5.88 ×), and 1.24 ×

in batch (0.93–2.13 ×), the same as in the RaBitQ-equivalent comparison above. The largest step depended on the data: on high-dimensional data (agnews, arxiv, GIST, msmarco), multi-stage codes contributed most (1.65–3.19 × at 1 thread); on SIFT, the triangle-inequality window gave 1.20–1.43 × (0.90–1.04 × on the other datasets); and on laion, yandex, SSNPP, and SSNPP-10M, the final group of techniques gave 1.46–3.64 ×. On laion and yandex, enabling prefix stages without the 2-bit last stage (L4) made search 0.33–0.75 × as fast (on laion the first-stage pass rate jumped from 0.03 to 0.73), and the configuration chosen by the rule (L5) does not use prefix stages on these two datasets. The initial radius gave a geometric mean of 1.39 × (up to 2.37 ×) for 32-thread latency but was neutral at 1 thread (0.95–1.05 ×), and its 32-thread share includes the structural handicap of L0 starting from an infinite radius. The slack-factor step was small (0.92–1.21 ×). L0–L3 had no misses in any setting, and L4 and L5 left at most 2 of 1,000 queries inexact in a few settings, within δ.We also compared our method on the same three datasets ( k = 10) with ConANN [42], another method that gives a distribution-level guarantee. ConANN scans IVF partitions one by one, stops at a calibrated threshold, and guarantees 37 Dense 10% 25% 50% All 50% 25% Sparse 10% 012345Measured loss / target

> PQR-Search (expected, ¿= 0.9) ConANN ( ®= 0.1) CTR ( ¿= 0.9, ®= 0.1) Target Figure 11. Loss ÷target when test queries are selected by quantiles of the 10th-neighbor distance (calibration fixed, k= 10). (a) SIFT. (b) GIST. (c) arxiv.

that the average FNR (1 − recall) over the query distribution is at most α. Requiring this loss for each query yields the expected-recall guarantee with τ = 1 − α, so we set our method to this expected-recall guarantee and to the (1 − α, α )-recall guarantee with δ equal to α (α ∈ { 0.1, 0.05 , 0.01 }). Repeating with the calibration query set redrawn 20 times, ConANN’s average FNR was at the target level on SIFT and GIST, where calibration and test queries come from the same distribution (0.101 and 0.097 at α = 0 .1). Per query, however, 27–31% ( α = 0 .1, 0.05) and 6–8% ( α = 0 .01) of the queries had a mean recall over the 20 repetitions significantly below 1 − α, and the same queries fell short almost every time even when the calibration changed. Our method had no such query over 20 independent rotations, and the failure rate of its (1 − α, α )-recall guarantee was at most 0.11%. On arxiv, which has no separate query log, we used data vectors as calibration queries, as is common in practice; even a slight difference between the distributions of calibration queries (documents) and test queries (held-out documents) made ConANN’s average FNR exceed the target for all three α (0.118, 0.061, and 0.0103, exceeding it in 20, 20, and 17 of the 20 calibrations, respectively). Because a distribution-level guarantee relies on the premise that calibration queries represent test queries, even the query average is not maintained when this premise is shaken. Our guarantee does not use such a premise. At 1-thread, our method was 1.1–8.1 × faster except for two settings on arxiv; on arxiv, the two were similar at α = 0 .05, and ConANN was 1.7 ×

faster at α = 0 .1 (ConANN’s average FNR in that setting exceeded the target). Since ConANN processes each query in a single thread, our method was 3.0–46 × faster in 32-thread latency and 2.6–30 × faster in batch. We compared our method in the same way with the public implementation of PUFFINN [45], which bounds each true neighbor’s miss probability (per-query expected recall): its default setting fell short of the target even in average recall because of a candidate filter that its proof does not cover, and with the filter disabled it was 27–76 × slower than our method at 1-thread (Appendix C.3). Methods that rely on calibration can lose their guarantees when queries deviate from the distribution of calibration queries. To examine this in a controlled way, we kept the calibration queries unchanged and evaluated only the test queries whose distance to the 10th neighbor is large (the top 25% and 10%) (SIFT, GIST, arxiv, k = 10). On these subsets, ConANN’s average FNR at α = 0 .1 was 0.11–0.19 (up to 0.26 for the top 10%), exceeding the target; the mean shortfall of CTR ( τ = 0 .9, α = 0 .1) became 1.8–4.6 × the target on SIFT and GIST (2.2 × on GIST even over all queries); and the fraction of queries that IVF with the calibrated nprobe failed to answer exactly on arxiv rose from 1.7% to 8.0%. Our failure rate stayed within δ = 0 .01, at most 0.04%, on all subsets. Our method also set its parameter selection rule with the same calibration queries, but since calibration queries affect only the cost, the guarantee is unchanged even when test queries deviate from the distribution of calibration queries. Fig. 11 shows the same results as each method’s loss divided by its target; the value of our method (expected-recall guarantee at the same level) stayed at most 0.08 regardless of the query subset. Finally, to measure the price of the guarantee, we compared with indexes without guarantees, tuned so that the average recall over calibration queries reached a target, in the same run (Table 15; HNSW32 batch throughput re-timed as described in Section 6.1). These were FAISS HNSW32 (efSearch), IndexIVFFlat ( nprobe ), and IVF-RaBitQ with exact re-ranking ( nprobe and re-ranking factor), each with the cheapest setting whose calibration average met the target. On arxiv, agnews, and msmarco, which have no query log, data vectors served as calibration queries. With an average-recall target of 0.99 on the eight 1M datasets and msmarco (8.84M vectors), 18 settings, HNSW32 on laion ( k = 100) and SSNPP (3 settings) and IVF-RaBitQ on laion, yandex ( k = 10), and SSNPP (5 settings) did not reach a calibration average of 0.99 even at their most expensive settings († in Table 15). Over the settings where the baseline reached the target on calibration queries, the 1-thread latency of the exact-search guarantee (T1) was 2.5 × that of HNSW32 (geometric mean, 15 settings; 1.8 × over all 18 settings, since at its most expensive setting HNSW32 was slower where it missed the target): 1.7–2.7 × shorter on GIST and laion ( k = 10), 1.2–1.9 × longer on SIFT, YFCC, and yandex, 3.0–11.0 × longer on arxiv and agnews, and 15.5–40.4 × longer on msmarco (calibration with data vectors was easier 38 than the test queries there, so the test average recall of HNSW32 was 0.974–0.988, below the target). It was shorter than IndexIVFFlat in all 18 settings and than IVF-RaBitQ in 7 of the 13 settings where it reached the target (all but arxiv, agnews, and msmarco), and HNSW32 had 1.3 × higher batch throughput (geometric mean). In exchange, even where they reached the target, these indexes left 3.3–29.2% (HNSW32), 0.0–21.7% (IndexIVFFlat), and 3.4–25.4% (IVF-RaBitQ) of test queries below recall 0.99, with worst-query recall down to 0 for HNSW32, versus at most 0.1% for our method; where they did not reach it, 32.6–96.6% (HNSW32) and 15.4–58.7% (IVF-RaBitQ). On arxiv and agnews ( k = 10), calibrated with data vectors, the average recall of HNSW32 over test queries was only 0.97 (tuned afterwards on the test queries, HNSW32 was 5.2–5.8 × faster, with 5.9–6.4% of queries below 0.99). For a target of 0.9, HNSW32 was 1.57–94.6 × faster at 1 thread than our expected-recall guarantee (E, τ = 0 .9), most on msmarco, except on SSNPP ( k = 10) where it was 8.9 × slower, but even in the 17 settings where it reached the target, 2.9–32.1% of its queries fell below 0.9 (64.1% on SSNPP, k = 100, where it did not). The speed of indexes without guarantees is bought by meeting only the average target, and an equal average does not tell in advance which queries will fall short.

An index without guarantees can also be tuned to the tail target itself rather than to an average. We therefore repeated the comparison with each baseline set to the cheapest setting on the same grid whose fraction of the 1,000 calibration queries below recall τ was at most δ, which is the target our ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee meets for every query, and also report a post-hoc tail oracle that applies the same criterion to the test queries (Table 17; the 18 settings of Table 15, 

δ = 0 .01, timed in the same runs as our method except the re-timed HNSW32 batches). For (0 .99 , 0.01) we used T1 for 

k = 10, where τ = 0 .99 allows no miss, and the (0 .99 , 0.01)-recall guarantee for k = 100, configured for this comparison by the same parameter selection rule; for (0 .9, 0.01) we used the (0 .9, 0.01)-recall guarantee. Our method stayed within 

δ in all 36 pairs of setting and target (at most 0.1% of test queries below τ ). At τ = 0 .99, HNSW32 reached the tail target on calibration queries within efSearch 4,096 in only 10 of the 18 settings (4 of the 12 on the six datasets 39 with query logs), and the setting chosen on calibration queries still had more than δ of its test queries below 0.99 in 5 of these 10 (13 of 18 including the settings it did not reach). Where HNSW32 reached the target, our method was 1.1 × faster at 1 thread and 1.9 × faster in batch in geometric mean, and against the fastest baseline that reached the target in each setting, 2.1 × and 4.9 × faster; IndexIVFFlat reached it everywhere but was 9.0 × (1-thread) and 46 ×

(batch) slower than our method. The result depended on the calibration queries. On the six datasets with query logs our method was faster than HNSW32 in every setting where HNSW32 reached the target (2.8 × at 1 thread, 3.2 × in batch), whereas on arxiv, agnews, and msmarco, calibrated with data vectors, HNSW32 was 1.7 × faster at 1 thread (1.5 × for the post-hoc oracle) and our method remained 1.4 × faster in batch. At τ = 0 .9, HNSW32 was faster than our method: 2.7 × at 1 thread when tuned on calibration queries, whose chosen setting exceeded δ on test queries in 13 of the 18 settings, and 2.0 × for the post-hoc oracle over the 14 settings it reached. This advantage came from the three datasets calibrated with data vectors (oracle 5.4 × at 1 thread and 2.1 × in batch); on the six datasets with query logs the two were on par at 1 thread (ours 1.03 ×) and our method was 1.6 × faster in batch. The grid ended at efSearch 4,096 (all lists for IndexIVFFlat and a re-ranking factor of 200 for IVF-RaBitQ), so some settings the baselines did not reach might be reached with larger values (we widen the grid and the baselines below), and the geometric means cover only reached settings, a filter that favors the baselines. With 1,000 queries, δ = 0 .01 corresponds to 10 queries, so whether a setting exceeds δ (for example, at 1.1% or 1.2%) is within binomial variation.

We then widened the baselines and the scale with the same tail tuning and timing (Table 19; k ∈ { 10 , 100 }, δ = 0 .01). On the four datasets whose queries come from a different distribution than the data (laion, yandex, SSNPP, and imagenet-align), we added HNSW with M ∈ { 32 , 48 , 64 } (efConstruction 500 for M > 32) and efSearch up to 16,384, DiskANN [10] (Vamana, R = 64, α = 1 .2, L up to 16,384), and IVF-RaBitQ with 4- and 8-bit codes and exact re-ranking, and on laion, yandex, and SSNPP also RoarGraph [20], a graph built with training queries for cross-modal search. At 10M scale (YFCC-10M, SSNPP-10M) we compared with HNSW32 and DiskANN, and at 100M scale (DEEP-100M, SIFT100M) with HNSW32, both with efSearch up to 16,384. Our method stayed within δ in every setting and target (at most 0.1% of test queries below τ ). At τ = 0 .99 on the out-of-distribution datasets, larger graphs reached the target in more settings (HNSW64 in 7 of 8), but their settings chosen on calibration queries still left more than δ of test queries below 0.99 in 5 of 8, and against the fastest baseline that reached the target in each setting, RoarGraph included, our method was 2.9 × faster at 1 thread and 5.3 × faster in batch. RoarGraph did not reach τ = 0 .99 on SSNPP even at L = 16 ,384 and was 3.2 × (1-thread) and 6.0 × (batch) slower than our method elsewhere. At τ = 0 .9 the fastest baseline was 1.1 × faster than our method at 1 thread in geometric mean (up to 2.9 ×

in single settings; RoarGraph 1.7 ×) and 2.0 × slower in batch. At 10M scale, neither HNSW32 nor DiskANN reached 

τ = 0 .99 or τ = 0 .9 on SSNPP-10M (47–96% of test queries below τ at the largest setting), while on YFCC-10M both graphs reached τ = 0 .9 and were 4.8–6.0 × faster than our method at 1 thread (DiskANN also up to 2.3 × faster at 

τ = 0 .99). At 100M scale, where queries follow the data distribution, HNSW32 reached every target and was faster: 4.8 × (τ = 0 .99) and 19 × (τ = 0 .9) at 1 thread and 1.6 × and 7.2 × in batch, because our 1-thread latency grows to 56–71 ms; with 32 threads per query, our latency (7–9 ms) was shorter than that of HNSW32 at τ = 0 .99 in three of the four settings. The price of the guarantee is thus largest for in-distribution queries at a low target on large data, and small or negative for out-of-distribution queries and high targets, where tail-tuned indexes either do not reach the target or exceed δ on test queries. The HNSW batches of these runs were also timed without FAISS’s Python interrupt 40 callback.

When isotropic Gaussian noise was added to the test queries (with noise magnitude up to twice the 10th-neighbor distance), our failure rate also stayed within δ, and only the 1-thread latency increased, by 1.5–4.8 ×. In this case, ConANN and CTR shifted toward searching more for queries whose neighbor distances grew, so their shortfall actually decreased, but CTR’s 1-thread median latency increased more than 1,000-fold on SIFT and arxiv (from 0.37 ms to 444 ms on SIFT). Table 21 summarizes the per-query shortfall of the compared methods that rely on calibration queries. All of these methods fell short of the target on some queries, and the fraction mostly grew on the subsets that select only queries with distant neighbors.

We also measured index build time and size (Table 23). Since building is a batch job separate from query processing, all three methods were run with 64 threads; we report the median of three runs for the 1M datasets and a single run for the 10M scale and hotpotqa, and data loading is excluded from the time. Size counts all arrays read during query processing (including the fp64 centroids used for handling floating-point error) and the original IDs. Our build (Algorithm 1) took 1.6–2.6 × longer than training and adding for FAISS IndexIVFFlat on all 13 datasets, and 62–96% 41 of the time went to k-means and size-capped assignment. It was 1.4–1.9 × faster than the build of CTR, the other method with guarantees (HNSW construction and edge cache). Calibrating the parameter selection rule, including generating the candidate configurations, took an additional 3–41 s on the 1M datasets and 54–141 s at the 10M scale. In the default configuration without the 2-bit final stage, the index size for fp32 data was 1.04–1.11 × that of IVF-Flat (up to 1.21 × for configurations that use the 2-bit stage). This is because we keep the original vectors as they are for exact distances and add the first-stage data (23–39 bytes per vector) and the codes and coefficients of the later stages. On SIFT, YFCC, and SSNPP, whose original values are 8-bit integers, we keep the originals in 8 bits, so the size was 0.36–0.43 × that of FAISS, which stores them as float32.

# 6.4 Guarantees and Cost under Updates 

First, to see what happens after updates to parameters tuned at the start, we built 11 workloads that build an index on 50% of the data and then update it in five steps of 10% each. There are three update patterns: random insertion, cluster-ordered insertion (distribution shift), and a sliding window that inserts 10% in cluster order and deletes the oldest 10%; all three were applied to arxiv, SIFT, and laion, and the sliding window to GIST and yandex. Public dynamic software (IVF-Flat and HNSW of FAISS [8], hnswlib [9], and FreshDiskANN [38]; hnswlib instead of FAISS’s HNSW for the sliding window, which involves deletions) was tuned at the first step and not retuned afterward, whereas our method needs no tuning to meet recall (calibration queries are used only for the cost choices of Section 4.3). After tuning for an average recall of 0.99, we measured at each step the fraction of queries with recall below 0.99. As a result, the per-query failures of IVF-Flat increased substantially as insertions and deletions continued. On the SIFT sliding window, this fraction rose from 6.1% to 14.6% for k = 10 and from 15.8% to 31.6% for k = 100; on GIST, it rose from 9.6% to 21.3% for k = 100; and on arxiv, queries with recall 0 appeared. Graph indexes degraded gradually even under random insertion alone. On SIFT ( k = 100), the fraction rose from 21.0% to 26.9% for HNSW and from 17.5% to 24.0% for FreshDiskANN. On the yandex sliding window, which receives real out-of-distribution queries, IVF-Flat, hnswlib, FreshDiskANN, and Quake all reached the average target at first, but their failure fractions increased (Section 2.1, Fig. 1(b)), and for Quake the worst query’s recall dropped from 0.73 to 0.42. On laion, hnswlib failed to reach an average recall of 0.99 from the start (0.982 at the largest search parameter, ef = 4,096). In contrast, under cluster-ordered insertion (distribution shift), the failure fractions of the public software actually decreased. On the same 11 workloads, our T1 failures were 219 in 540,000 trials (at most 7 in 3,000 at any time point), and our time was shorter than that of FAISS IndexFlatL2 in every setting (3.4–53.8 × 1-thread, 11.3–116 × 32-thread, 3.2–67.1 × batch; the smallest value is the batch on laion). Next, we used, as is, the delete runbook of msturing-10M-clustered ( delete_runbook.yaml ) from the operation se-quences (runbooks) provided by the official repository of the NeurIPS’23 streaming track [53]. This runbook performs 32 insertions, 10 deletions, and 33 searches, and the number of live vectors reaches up to 5.3M over the whole sequence. The competition’s final evaluation scored a different runbook by the average recall over the entire sequence under limits of 8 GB of memory and one hour [53], but our experiment imposed no such limits, used 1,000 queries, and computed the ground truth directly in fp64. Searches and updates were executed serially. Without retuning, the failure rate was at most δ in all 33 search steps (Eq. (14)), and the per-step failure rate of the exact-search guarantee ( δ = 0 .01) was at most 0.17% (Fig. 12(a)). In contrast, IVF-Flat with nprobe fixed at 2612 (of 4096) so that 99% of queries were exact at the first time point failed, on the same sequence, to answer 1.0% of the queries exactly at the first search and 1.6–4.2% at each later step, exceeding its target by up to 4.2 × (Fig. 12(a)). Meanwhile, the number of live vectors at search time rose and fell between 260K and 5.07M with insertions and deletions (Fig. 12(b)). At the timed checkpoints (searches 1, 5, . . . , 33), our method was 5.9–13.2 × (1-thread), 3.0–39.3 × (32-thread), and 8.7–53.9 × (batch) faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 (Fig. 12(c)), with the smallest values at the first search with 260K live vectors (our method and FAISS were measured alternately in one run on the same update sequence). As live vectors grew from 260K to 5.07M, our 1-thread latency increased from 0.38 ms to 4.52 ms and that of FAISS IndexFlatL2 from 2.22 ms to 46.9 ms. Compared with IndexIVFFlat with the oracle nprobe , the post-hoc optimal reference, our method was also 10.0–14.3 × (1-thread), 42 0 10 20 30 Search step 0246810 Queries not exact (%)          

> (a) IVF-Flat (probe count fixed at step 1) PQR-Search (T1)
> ±= 1% 010 20 30 Search step 0246Live vectors (millions) (b) 15913 17 21 25 29 33 Search step (checkpoint) 020 40 60 Speedup over FAISS Flat (×) (c) 1 thread 32 threads Batch Figure 12. Official NeurIPS’23 streaming runbook (msturing-10M, k= 10). (a) Failure rate per search step. (b) Live vectors at search time. (c) Speedup over FAISS IndexFlatL2 at checkpoints.

5.9–18.8 × (32-thread), and 16.9–92.8 × (batch) faster. We measured two dynamic indexes without guarantees on the same runbook. FreshDiskANN [38] (R = 64, search list L = 384, the smallest giving average recall 0.99 on calibration queries at the first search) had up to 2.7 × lower 1-thread latency and up to 1.27 × higher batch throughput than ours once live vectors exceeded 2.4M, but 18.5% of its queries fell below recall 0.99 (worst recall 0; average recall per step down to 0.926), and its update throughput was 33,127 vectors/s, 1/3.1 of ours (consolidating deletions took 45% of its update time); with L re-chosen afterwards at each checkpoint for average recall 0.99, we were 1.31–3.09 × (1-thread) and 2.73–3.12 × (batch) faster. Quake [37] (APS target 0.99, default maintenance) split partitions from 1,000 into up to 29,211, so its 1-thread search became slower than FAISS IndexFlatL2 (7.3–38.0 × slower than ours), its update throughput was 1,473 vectors/s (1/69 of ours), and 2.45% of its queries fell below recall 0.99. On a 160-step arxiv 1M streaming scenario generated with the official procedure [53], the failure rate also stayed at most δ to the end (148 T1 failures in 480,000 trials, at most 4 in 3,000 per step), and our method was 6.7–34.6 × (1-thread), 6.6–52.0 × (32-thread), and 3.6–11.0 × (batch) faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 at nine checkpoints. We also measured the effect of maintenance. Without residual-based maintenance, the mean residual grew by 21–23% on msturing and by 26–34% on arxiv, and partition sizes became skewed; at the checkpoints after merges, the main-tained index was faster by 1.29–2.33 × (1-thread), 2.18–3.97 × (32-thread), and 3.69–6.66 × (batch) on msturing and by 2.42–4.84 ×, 2.16–5.62 ×, and 2.94–8.97 ×, respectively, on arxiv. Compared with an index rebuilt from scratch, the search speed of the maintained index was 0.81–1.16 × on msturing and 0.76–1.16 × on arxiv. With the same search implementation, policy K, which decides by Eq. (12), achieved 4.0 × higher update throughput than policy R on laion with 1/5.8 of the maintenance time; query latency was at the same level (0.97–1.11 × 1-thread, 0.96–1.01 × 32-thread), and batch throughput was 0.87–1.11 × depending on the configuration (step 5, a single run). We also compared, with the same search implementation and the same merge points, a LIRE-style [39] size-based policy (splitting partitions larger than twice the mean size by 2-means, dissolving those smaller than 0.25 times, and reassigning vectors of nearby partitions, in variants with and without recomputing all centroids at every merge), and found no evidence that policy K is faster. On the official runbook, at checkpoints with at least 2.79M live vectors, policy K had 1.03–1.14 × shorter 1-thread and 1.01–1.17 × shorter 32-thread latency than the size-based policy (recomputing variant) and similar batch throughput (0.93–1.04 ×), but early on it was slower (0.80–0.85 ×, 0.98M live vectors) because it splits a large partition only in two per merge. On the arxiv runbook, its 1-thread latency was 0.93–1.01 × and its batch throughput 0.78–1.01 ×,lower at 8 of 9 checkpoints, and the variant without recomputation had 1.6 × the update throughput of policy K. For all three policies the failure rate was at most δ (at most 2/1,000 exact-search failures per step), the decisions of the size-based policy were identical under two rotations at all 87 merges, and the prediction of Eq. (12) was 0.97–1.09 × the measurement on all three indexes. The maintenance gains above thus come from maintenance itself; the contribution of policy K is not speed but predicting cost without data-fitted constants while keeping maintenance separate from the guarantee. Maintenance decisions were executed separately for each rotation, and the result (centroids, assignment) was identical at every merge. This agrees with C5, which states that maintenance decisions do not depend on the rotation. In addition, searching with the four nearest live vectors of each query only marked as deleted gave the same output as the index from which those vectors were physically removed by a merge. Varying the merge threshold from 5% to 40%, the failure rate was at most δ in all 33 search steps (9–14 exact-search failures in 33,000), the search time at the checkpoints was 0.94–1.07 × that with a 10% threshold, and larger thresholds reduced merges from 35 to 13, raising update throughput by 2.2 × (Fig. 13(a)). A single update in our method includes encoding and maintenance, so it is 7–66 × more expensive than an addition or removal in FAISS IndexFlatL2, which only appends vectors to an array or removes them (66 × on the official runbook; the update throughput of the default configuration, including merges and maintenance, is 102,016 vectors/s). Since this cost is recovered by queries, it is appropriate to compare the total cost of queries and updates, Q t q + U t u. Here Q

(16) and the measured values on the 11 workloads and the official runbook were 0.0021–0.035 (batch) and 0.0002–0.0022 (1-thread) (0.134 for batch and 0.0037 for 1-thread on the arxiv runbook, whose index is small). That is, on these workloads, the total cost is lower than that of FAISS once the number of queries reaches only 0.2–3.5% (batch) of the number of updated vectors. In this calculation, tu is the time when updates are grouped and processed with 32 threads. Update throughput was highest at 16 threads (124,000 vectors/s versus 104,000 at 32 threads); beyond the number of physical cores (32), the two hardware threads of a core are used together, which slows the maintenance stage of merges, and throughput dropped to 53,000 at 64 threads (Fig. 13(b)). For updates alone, 16 threads gave 1.19 ×

higher throughput, but we used 32 threads, the same as batch search. The 27 merges took 4.74 s on average (up to 8.9 s), and the last merge (6.95 s) took 0.12 × the time to rebuild the index from scratch with the 4.6M live vectors at that point (58.8 s). In our replay of the official runbook (1,000 queries per search step), queries are only 0.22% of the number of updated vectors, a workload near the break-even point. Indeed, dividing the time of the whole runbook, 98% of our 151.0 s went to updates (91.3 s of which was maintenance), and with queries processed in batch, FAISS IndexFlatL2 (105.0 s in the same run) took 1.44 × less total time. With queries processed one at a time on 1 thread, our method took 4.1 × less. Varying the ratio of updates to queries, our total throughput relative to FAISS IndexFlatL2 was 7.7–48.6 × at an update-to-query ratio of 2:8, 5.6–45.1 × at 5:5, and 2.7–34.8 × at 8:2, and 3.0–9.6 ×, 2.0–7.1 ×,and 0.9–3.8 ×, respectively, on the arxiv runbook (queries in batch, at each checkpoint, Fig. 13(c)). On arxiv, the throughput measured by actually running alternating updates and queries in equal numbers (with the implementation before the insertion path was fixed) agreed with the value computed from unit costs to within 0.5%. In a deletion-heavy workload that halves the live vectors and then restores them (arxiv, from 540K through 270K back to 540K), the failure rate was at most δ at every step (8 exact-search failures in 27,000), and the mean residual stayed at 0.98–1.08 × its initial value. On the arxiv sliding window, inserting updates one at a time still gave an update throughput of 5,282 vectors/s (median 0.29 ms per insertion), which grew from 11,573 to 47,345 vectors/s when updates were inserted in batches of 100 to 100,000. The guarantee did not depend on the batch size, and search time varied with batch size within 22% in 1-thread latency and within 7% in batch throughput. We also measured updates at the 100M scale. After building an index with 90M vectors of DEEP-100M, we repeated 10 times a step that deletes 1M of the initial vectors and inserts 1M new vectors, replacing 10M vectors in total. At all 11 time points, there was no exact-search failure (0/11,000), and search was 10.2–13.3 × (1-thread), 29.0–48.4 ×

(32-thread), and 86–142 × (batch) faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2. At this scale, the maintenance policy did not split partitions but dissolved some of them, reducing the number of partitions from 90,000 to 81,000, and search right after each of the two merges was as fast as or faster than with the initial index (1-thread 58.3–58.7 ms versus 60.4 ms; batch 1,457–1,479 versus 1,379 QPS). Update throughput, including merges and maintenance, was 23,244 vectors/s, 18.6 ×

slower than addition and removal in IndexFlatL2, and the total time of the two merges (572 s) was 0.33 × the time to rebuild from scratch with 90M live vectors (1,711 s). Finally, we verified Theorem 6 (b) and (c): if the same rotation is kept, a query’s failures can persist, and if a new rotation is used at merges, the failures are broken. Fig. 14 shows, for three configurations with enough failure events (the exact-search guarantee with δ = 0 .5 and 0 .1, and the expected-recall guarantee with τ = 0 .9), the conditional probability that a query failing in one search step also fails in the next, split by whether a merge occurs in between and whether the rotation is changed. Keeping the rotation couples failures strongly over time. with a fixed rotation, one to two orders of magnitude larger than the unconditional failure rate (0.7–5.5%). With a new rotation at every merge, the conditional failure probability for pairs of search steps across a merge dropped from 0.41–0.52 with a fixed rotation to 0.013–0.067. For pairs without a merge, it remained high at 0.77–0.91 even with new rotations, confirming that it is the new rotation that breaks the failures. The remaining 0.067 under new rotations (the δ = 0 .5 configuration) is larger than the unconditional failure rate of 0.054 in the same configuration, but it is not a violation. Since hard queries tend to fail under any rotation, the conditional probability measured between mutually independent rotations (0.005–0.063) serves as the baseline for this query heterogeneity, and the value under new rotations is at the same level as this baseline. After a new rotation, the stored codes were bit-identical to codes computed from scratch; re-encoding cost 0.20 μs per vector and reduced update throughput by 9.7%. 

# 6.5 Cost Law and Parameter Selection 

In this section, we first verify the pass-rate prediction given by the cost law of Section 3.3, and then measure how cost is affected by the design choices of Section 4.1 that differ from standard IVF, by the techniques of Section 4.3, and by the parameter selection rule. The first-stage pass-rate prediction of Eq. (12) agreed well with measurements across partition methods, scales, and updates. On six 1M datasets, over 256 points varying the partition method (up to 10 kinds), k, and the settings of the radius and the error-bound parameter, the log correlation was 0.9995 (median of prediction ÷ measurement 0.997, with 96% within ±10%); over 38 settings at 10M scale it was 0.999; and at 118 time points of two streaming runbooks (msturing and arxiv) with continuing insertions and deletions, the ratio of prediction to measurement was 0.98–1.08 (Fig. 15). For six settings on three 100M-scale datasets, the prediction was estimated from sampled vectors for 20 queries; over 10 independent per-query samples, the mean ratio of prediction to measurement was 0.98–1.02 (0.86–1.10 for a single sample). The prediction matched the pass rate over a range of three orders of magnitude without constants fitted to the data. Next, to confirm the role of centroids explained in Section 3.3, we fixed the radius at the true k-th distance and removed two components one at a time. The residual effect is the time ratio when the centroids are replaced by a single global centroid, and the theory effect is the time ratio when the error-bound parameter is changed from that of our method (a union over the k true neighbors) to the value determined by a union over all vectors. The ratio when both are removed, divided by the product of the ratios when each is removed separately, is called the interaction. If the two effects are independent of each other, the interaction is 1; if it is greater than 1, the loss from removing both together is greater 45 than the product of the losses from removing each separately. That is, the presence of one component compensates for part of the loss from removing the other. As Table 25 shows, the interaction was 1.13–1.40 on the text data (arxiv, agnews), GIST, and SIFT, larger at 1.54–2.18 on YFCC and msmarco at 10M scale, and near 1 at 0.95–1.03 on SSNPP and laion ( k = 100). In terms of first-stage passes, the residual effect is up to 13.0 ×, much larger than in time. When the pass rate is as low as 0.002–0.009, as on YFCC-10M ( k = 10), time is bound by the first-stage scan and the time effect appears small, so the effect of residuals on cost is judged more accurately by the number of passes, i.e., by Eq. (12). On SSNPP, which has a high intrinsic dimension, the residual effect was 1.02–1.07 at both 1M and 10M. The theory effect comes from reducing the terms of the union from n vectors to k true neighbors. 

Table 25. Contributions of residuals and of the error-bound parameter (1-thread time ratios; radius fixed at the true k-th distance).

Measurements with 10 different partition methods lead to the same conclusion (per-method numbers in Appendix C.8). At the same radius, the k-means family and anisotropic quantization [13] yielded almost the same cost (0.95–1.03 ×)as the partitioning of our method (k-means with size-capped assignment), and the order of pass rates was determined by the relative residual size (rank correlation across methods 0.88–0.96). In contrast, when each method used the radius it obtained by itself, methods with poor centroids (a global centroid, random projection trees [94]) had visited partitions containing fewer neighbors, which worsened the radius as well, so they were 1.6–18.3 × slower on 1M data with in-distribution queries; the adaptive radius reduced this penalty to 1.2–2.5 ×. This agrees with the explanation in Section 3.3 that partitions play two roles: providing centroids and obtaining the radius. We first validated the parameter selection rule on imagenet-align, which was never used in design or calibration. Across 48 settings, the configuration chosen by the rule achieved on average 0.903 × (minimum 0.42) the performance of the fastest fixed configuration for each setting (hereafter the best fixed configuration). The settings that fell furthest short of the best fixed configuration were those with a small error-bound parameter, such as the expected-recall guarantee and 

δ = 0 .1, in which the rule chose the default first-stage code length rather than D. Even so, the guarantee held (( τ, δ )-recall guarantee failures 1/108,000), and our method was faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 in all settings (6.8–20.6 ×

in batch). On DEEP1M and GloVe, which were added after the rule was fixed and were likewise not used in design or calibration, the configuration chosen by the rule achieved on average 0.959 × (minimum 0.81) the performance of the best fixed configuration over 24 settings. The rule chose the default configuration in 22 settings, so no setting became slower than the default, and the shortfall came mainly from not enabling centering and 2-bit records (the minimum was 32-thread latency on DEEP1M with k = 100). On the 96 settings of the eight 1M datasets used in design and calibration, the configuration chosen by the rule achieved on average 0.957 × the performance of the best fixed configuration, and was faster, by a geometric mean of 1.18 × over the three metrics (1-thread 1.22 ×, 32-thread 1.15 ×, batch 1.17 ×), than the configuration with the default first-stage code length and all techniques other than the adaptive radius disabled, with no setting slower. The gains concentrated on laion (geometric mean 2.05 ×, up to 3.80 ×), yandex (1.32 ×, up to 2.38 ×), and SSNPP (1.31 ×, up to 1.71 ×); on the remaining data the rule mostly chose the default configuration. How the value on the design data came to be higher is explained in Appendix C.5. Reducing the calibration queries used by the rule from 200 to 100 or 50 changed the chosen configuration in only one of the 48 (setting, metric) pairs on the eight 1M datasets in each case. The time differences were within measurement noise except in one setting (1.25 × at 32 threads on yandex, k = 10), and the time to determine the rule decreased in proportion to the number of calibration queries, from 132 s to 34 s in total over 16 settings. We also varied the number of visited partitions nprobe and the partition size (the average number of vectors per partition, 

l) (SIFT, arxiv, GIST, laion, k = 10, exact search). In all 48 settings of l ∈ { 500 , 1000 , 2000 } and nprobe ∈ { 1, 2, 4, 8},46 the failure rate was at most δ (Table 11) and our method was faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 (the smallest value was 4.7 × in batch for GIST, l = 2000, nprobe = 8). nprobe = 1 was as fast as or faster than the default value 2 except at 32 threads for SIFT with l = 500 (0.92 ×) (up to 1.37 × in batch, on GIST), whereas at nprobe = 8 the batch was up to 5× slower than at nprobe = 1. This is because visiting more partitions reduces the vectors whose exact distances are computed in unvisited partitions (from 0.098% to 0.020% on GIST), but the cost of computing exact distances for all 

nprobe · l vectors of the visited partitions grows more. On arxiv and GIST, larger partitions made the 1-thread case faster and the batch slower, and for nprobe ≤ 2 the difference among the three sizes was within 1.3 × in 17 of the 24 combinations of (data, metric, nprobe ) (at most 1.61 ×, batch on GIST). All other experiments used the default values (l = 1000, nprobe = 2). Table 27 summarizes the effect of disabling or replacing each technique of Section 4.3 one at a time. Reading codes prefix-first was faster on in-distribution high-dimensional data with low first-stage pass rates, whereas reading the entire code at once was faster on data where many vectors pass the first stage because the queries are out of distribution; the parameter selection rule therefore chooses the faster of the two for each dataset. The triangle-inequality range reduced the vectors to be tested on data with low intrinsic dimension, and thanks to sorting by x the cost of finding the range is one binary search per partition. The effect of the adaptive radius was larger the more the radius determined from visited vectors exceeded the true k-th distance. Enabling techniques regardless of the data sometimes made search slower (for example, 0.89–0.94 × at 1 thread on yandex). This is why the rule decides the use of each technique separately for each dataset and metric.

# 6.6 Application, Scale, and Dimension 

As an application of range queries, we experimented with image copy detection. For the SSNPP range ground truth of the Big-ANN’21 benchmark [25] (squared L2 radius 96,237), we required the probability of missing even one copy for a query to be at most 0.01 (the error-bound parameter follows the range-query setting of Section 3.2). Over 40,000 trials at 1M scale and 10,000 trials at 10M scale (1,024 and 826 of them, 1,850 in total, on queries that have copies), our method had no query that missed a copy, and it never returned a vector outside the radius. This corresponds to 1 in mean average precision (mAP) [25], the official metric of the competition. In time, it was faster than the range search of FAISS IndexFlatL2 at 10M scale by 20 × at 1 thread, 132–133 × at 32 threads, and 54 × in batch (at 1M scale, 23 ×

at 1 thread, 222–247 × at 32 threads, and 43–44 × in batch). In contrast, methods without guarantees missed copies. At 10M scale, of the 826 queries with copies, HNSW32 missed copies for 16.7% (138) of the queries even at efSearch 4,096 (185/6,956 copies missed), and missed all copies for 68 queries. Its average recall in this case was as high as 0.97. IVF tuned to an average recall of 0.99 also missed copies for 5.7% of the queries. 47 We also measured whether missed neighbors actually change the answers of database operators (Section 1). On YFCC, which has tag metadata (1M and 10M, k ∈ { 10 , 100 }, 1,000 test queries), we defined operators that aggregate the tags of the k-NN result S. The tags are the metadata of the filtered track of the Big-ANN benchmarks, with a vocabulary of about 200,000 tags and 8.8 tags per vector on average. The operators are the most frequent tag ( GROUP BY tag ORDER BY COUNT(*) DESC LIMIT 1 ), the most frequent tag excluding common tags attached to more than 1% of the base vectors (informative), the top-5 tags with their counts, the number of neighbors that have all filter tags of the query ( COUNT(*) WHERE , the AND filter of the filtered track), and whether that number is positive. An answer counts as wrong if it differs from the answer on the exact result and the result is not exact (Recall < 1 under the tie rule of Section 6.1); a different answer from an exact result arises from choosing another exact set among ties at dk and is not counted. Hence, for any operator, wrong answers are a subset of the cases with Recall < 1. The baselines are HNSW32, IVF-Flat, and IVF-RaBitQ with re-ranking (1M only), tuned with the same definition as the price of the guarantee in Section 6.3, so that the average recall over 1,000 calibration queries disjoint from the test queries reached the target; every baseline reached it. For our method we ran the rule-selected configurations of the main comparison (the exact-search guarantee T1 with δ = 0 .01, and the expected-recall guarantee E with τ = 0 .9) with 10 independent rotations and three processing paths (batch, 32-thread, and 1-thread), 30,000 trials per dataset and k. Time was not measured.
