As Table 29 shows, indexes tuned to an average recall of 0.99 returned a different most frequent tag for 0.5–1.9% of queries, a different top-5 list with counts for 3.1–26.6%, and a different filtered count for up to 4.6% than exact search. In contrast, our method (T1) changed the answer of any operator in at most 4 of the 10,000 (rotation, query) trials of each path (0.04%), and never twice for the same query (the most frequent tag changed once, on the 1-thread path of 10M with k = 100). Because wrong answers are a subset of the cases with Recall < 1, the exact-search guarantee Pr[Recall( q) < 1] ≤ δ becomes, for any operator, a per-query guarantee that the answer is wrong with probability at most δ. Ten rotations cannot certify the per-query bound statistically; the bound comes from this argument, and the measurements confirm that they do not contradict it. Among the baselines’ queries with recall below 1, the fraction whose answer actually changed depended on how fine the operator is. For the most frequent tag it was 26–41% at 

k = 10 but 2–9% at k = 100, where the count gap between the first and second tags of the exact answer is 10–12 on average, so the answer survives a changed neighbor (at k = 10 the gap is 1.1 on average and 34% of queries have a tie). For the top-5 tags with counts it was 90–100%: missing one neighbor almost always made the answer wrong. Larger knobs did not easily remove the errors. Even at efSearch 4,096 (test average recall 0.999), HNSW32 left 4.2–4.8% top-5 48 errors at k = 100, and on 10M with k = 100, IVF-Flat scanning 15% of the partitions ( nprobe = 1 ,536) still returned inexact results for 0.2% of queries. Tuned to an average recall of 0.9, the baselines’ wrong answers grew to 2.9–17.2% for the most frequent tag and 21.6–75.4% for the top-5 tags. Our expected-recall guarantee (E, τ = 0.9), with a measured average recall of 0.9994–0.9995, gave far fewer wrong answers than the baselines at the same target (most frequent tag 0.08–0.20%; top-5 0.50–0.54% at k = 10 and 3.95–4.01% at k = 100), but since it does not bound the probability that recall falls below 1, it puts no bound on wrong answers: at k = 100, 88 (1M) and 102 (10M) queries had a Clopper–Pearson lower bound on their probability of an inexact result above δ = 0 .01. Database operators should therefore use the exact-search guarantee (T1). As limitations of this measurement, YFCC is the only dataset with tag metadata, the operators are tag aggregates, and operators on numeric columns such as averages or sums were not measured; IVF-RaBitQ at 10M was omitted because it required building a new index. We also measured filtered k-NN (Corollary 2) on YFCC with two kinds of predicates: the tag conjunctions of the 1,000 test queries of the Big-ANN filtered track (selectivity below 0.1% for 498 of them) and random subsets of 0.1%, 1%, and 10% of the vectors, chosen independently of the vectors. In exact search (T1, δ = 0 .01, with the index and parameter selection rule used without filters), each of the 22 configurations (predicate, k, and execution path) failed in at most 12 of 300,000 (rotation, query) trials on YFCC-1M (300 rotations), and 98.8–99.9% of the queries were certified individually (Clopper–Pearson upper bound below δ); on YFCC-10M (20 rotations) at most 1 of 20,000 trials failed. Filtered indexes without guarantees lost recall as selectivity fell. On the filtered-track queries with selectivity below 0.1%, ACORN [95] reached a mean recall of only 0.04–0.06, and HNSW32 searching with the predicate did not reach a mean of 0.99 even at efSearch 4,096; at the settings that did reach a mean of 0.99 (chosen on the test queries), 0.2–41% of the queries still had recall below 1. Our cost does not shrink with selectivity (0.51–0.96 ms at 1 thread on 1M, close to the unfiltered cost), so exact search over the qualifying vectors was faster below 1% selectivity, whereas above it our method was 6.4–9.7 × faster at 1 thread. Choosing between the two plans by |Sq | alone (exact search if at most 1% of the vectors qualify), which does not read the rotation, keeps every query exact or δ-guaranteed, and it reduced the mean 1-thread latency of the filtered-track queries to 0.26 ms on 1M (0.67 ms with our method alone and 1.57 ms with exact search alone; 99th percentile 0.99 ms versus 18 ms for exact search) and to 3.65 ms on 10M (8.98 and 22.7 ms). As the scale grew from 1M to 10M, no setting was slower than FAISS IndexFlatL2 (the 10M scale in Fig. 7). At k = 1, the speedups at 1 thread, 32 threads, and in batch were 16.7 ×, 81.5 ×, and 42.5 × on YFCC-10M and 90.4 ×, 319 ×, and 61.4 × on msmarco. At 10M scale, the only setting in which the oracle IVF, a post-hoc optimal reference, was faster than our method was 1-thread on YFCC-10M with k = 1 (0.48 ×). IVF with nprobe set from calibration queries failed 4 test queries in the same setting. On hotpotqa, which searches 5.23M Wikipedia paragraphs with real questions, our method was faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 by 17.2–43.9 × at 1 thread, 50.0–122 × at 32 threads, and 10.0–48.6 × in batch. On DEEP-100M, MSTuring-100M, and SIFT100M, with the scale increased to 100M, our method was again faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 in all settings (Table 31). The speedups were 12.3–20.6 × at 1 thread, 36.3–71.8 × at 32 threads, and 69.0–154 × in batch; in absolute terms, exact search took 57–77 ms at 1 thread and 7.2–12.5 ms at 32 threads and processed 583–1,272 queries/s in batch, versus 873–1,177 ms, 397–521 ms, and 8.3–11.7 queries/s for IndexFlatL2 (Table 13). The batch gain is larger than for exact search at 10M scale (15.8–55.2 ×) because the batch throughput of IndexFlatL2 drops to 8–12 queries per second at 100M. The failure rate was at most 4% of δ in all settings, with no significant violation (Table 11).

The oracle IVF, a post-hoc optimal reference, had shorter latency than our method in some latency settings of the expected-recall guarantee and of exact search ( k = 10) on DEEP-100M and SIFT100M (1-thread latency in 6 settings, namely the four expected-recall settings and exact search with k = 10 on both datasets, with ours at 0.20–0.92 ×,and 32-thread latency in the four expected-recall settings, with ours at 0.35–0.79 ×), but in batch our method was 2.6–257 × faster in all settings. This is because IVF scans only some of the partitions, whereas our method also tests the unvisited partitions for the per-query guarantee (Appendix B). With an nprobe that allows δ of the test queries to fail (the δ-oracle), the margins shrink. For exact search with k = 10 on DEEP-100M and SIFT100M, the δ-oracle 

nprobe is 1,536; our method was 3.7–5.0 × faster in batch, but IVF had shorter latency (ours 0.22–0.37 × at 1 thread). For exact search with k = 100, the δ-oracle needs 6,144 partitions instead of a full scan, so, bounding from the times of measured nprobe values, our advantage shrinks to at most 14.6–20.4 × in batch and 1.0–1.5 × at 1 thread (we did not time that nprobe ). We also measured scalability with the number of threads (SIFT, GIST, laion, k = 10, exact search, 1–64 threads). The batch throughput of our method increased up to the number of physical cores (32), reaching 12.7–22.0 × that of 1 thread at 32 threads, and did not increase further at 64 threads, where the two hardware threads of each core are used together. The batch throughput of FAISS IndexFlatL2 at 32 threads was 4.7–12.8 × that at 1 thread, so the gain in batch grew with more threads on SIFT and laion (on SIFT, 15.8 × at 1 thread and 67.4 × at 32 threads) and was similar at 1 and 32 threads on GIST (24.5 ×, 24.3 ×). The latency of a single query was 3.0–7.1 × shorter at 32 threads than at 1 thread, with the smallest gain on SIFT, where the work per query is small. Since the single-query latency of FAISS IndexFlatL2 was actually longer at 2–8 threads than at 1 thread, latency is compared only at 1 and 32 threads. We also measured cost as a function of dimension. On versions of the same data (1M subsets of GIST, arxiv, and msmarco) reduced by PCA to dimensions from 128 up to the original dimension, with the first-stage code length fixed at 128 or 256 bits, the 1-thread speedup over FAISS IndexFlatL2 grew from 19.9–24.6 × at 128 dimensions to 40.4–62.6 ×

at the original dimension (768–1024). The 1-thread time of FAISS grew 5.9–8.1 × in proportion to the dimension, whereas the time of our method grew only 1.9–3.8 ×. This is because the testing cost of unvisited vectors is determined by the number of bits, not by the dimension. This trend does not arise because the versions with more dimensions are easier data. As PCA components were added, the relative radius Rvis actually grew (arxiv from 0.80 to 0.90, GIST from 0.91 to 0.98, msmarco from 0.93 to 1.05), making the test harder. The failure rate was at most δ in all cases. In batch, this trend did not appear, because the per-dimension efficiency of FAISS matrix multiplication improves with dimension (Appendix C.4). This experiment was measured separately with the kernel prior to the introduction of the adaptive radius, to observe the relative change with dimension. 

# 7. Conclusion 

This paper presented PQR-Search, a method that guarantees a target recall τ ∈ (0 , 1] for every query in approximate nearest neighbor search in vector databases. The guarantee is defined as a probability over a random orthogonal transformation inside the index, drawn from a secret seed, so it holds for any data and for any queries and updates that do not depend on that transformation, without assumptions on the data or query distribution, and for τ < 1the same run also satisfies the expected-recall guarantee under a mild condition. If updates are independent of the rotation, this guarantee persists without retuning and without splitting the failure probability δ between segments. The dynamic index adds only a delta segment, a deletion bitmap, and merges to the static index, so an insertion consists of encoding one vector and re-laying out the delta range of its partition, and a deletion of marking one bit. To obtain this guarantee at low cost, we stored residuals with respect to IVF centroids as 1-bit binary codes after a random orthogonal transformation, and derived a formula that predicts the first-stage pass rate in our index without data-fitted constants and without reading the rotation; without changing the guarantee, we used it to choose parameters as well as the operations that maintain an efficient index in a dynamic environment and their timing. In experiments, indexes tuned for average recall incurred more per-query failures under distribution shift and updates, whereas our method was faster than standard exhaustive search with the same guarantee in all settings of static indexes up to 100M scale, and the failure rate was at most δ in all search steps of the official streaming runbook and of updates at 100M scale. For database operators that take the search results as input, the exact-search guarantee carries over to a per-query guarantee on the correctness of the answer: indexes tuned for average recall changed the answer of a tag aggregate for up to 27% of queries, whereas PQR-Search changed it in at most 0.04% of trials. Under updates, the total cost was 50 higher or lower than that of FAISS depending on the ratio of queries to updates. Future work includes guarantee forms that exploit query logs and a GPU implementation.

To state the expressions of the main text rigorously, let G be the σ-algebra generated by the data, the queries, and everything determined without looking at P (in the dynamic setting, Gt, generated by the index state and the queries at time t). P follows the Haar distribution on O(D) and is independent of G. The decisions of Section 4.3 on enabling techniques may depend on P , but their failure events are contained in a path-independent union, so the bounds below remain unchanged. In the main text, Pr[ ·] and E[·] denote Pr[ · | G ] and E[· | G ], all guarantees of the main text, being statements about these conditional probabilities, hold almost surely, and “determined independently of P ” means G-measurable. Appendix A.1 proves the static guarantees, Appendix A.2 the dynamic guarantee, and Appendix A.3 the pass-rate formula and the soundness of the implementation. 

# A.1 Proofs of the Static Guarantees 

We first show the distributional fact underlying Lemma 1. If Y is uniformly distributed on Sn−1, then Y 21 ∼

Beta( 12 , n−12 ) and its sign is independent of |Y1|. This follows directly from the facts that Y has the same distribution as a normalized standard Gaussian vector g and that g21 and ∑ 

> i≥2

g2 

> i

are independent chi-square variables. Next, for fixed unit vectors o, q, let ρ = ⟨o, q⟩, ˜ x = P −1o, and ˜ y = P −1q, and, when |ρ| < 1, define e = (˜ y − ρ˜x)/√1 − ρ2. For a fixed orthogonal matrix H, P H ⊤ is also Haar, so ( H ˜x, H ˜y) and (˜ x, ˜y) have the same distribution. This distribution lies on the set of pairs of unit vectors with inner product ρ, and O(D) acts transitively on this set, so the invariant probability measure on it is unique. On the other hand, the pair constructed by drawing ˜ x uniformly and taking as e the normalized projection of an independent Gaussian vector onto ˜ x⊥ is also invariant, so the two distributions are equal. Hence, given ˜ x, e is uniformly distributed on the unit sphere of ˜ x⊥, and if D ≥ 3, then for any unit vector u ∈ ˜x⊥

that is a measurable function of ˜ x, Z = ⟨u, e⟩ is independent of ˜ x and Z2 ∼ Beta( 12 , D−22 ). RaBitQ [14] states that 

Z is independent of ⟨¯o, o⟩ and follows the same distribution, and the proof in its technical report (arXiv:2405.12497, Appendix B.3) conditions on ˜ x; the conclusion above states this independence from all of ˜ x for any u that is a function of ˜ x, which covers prefix codes and 2-bit codes. Since the distribution of Z is symmetric about 0, the cumulative distribution function of ζ = √D − 1 Z is, for s ≥ 0, GD (−s) = Pr[ Z < −s/ √D − 1] = 12 Pr[ Z2 > s 2/(D − 1)], which is Eq. (5). Lemma 1 follows immediately from this. Decompose the code vector ¯ x in the rotated coordinates as ¯ x = A˜x + v⊥

(A = ⟨¯x, ˜x⟩, v⊥ ⊥ ˜x) and substitute ˜ y = ρ˜x + √1 − ρ2 e; then ⟨¯x, ˜y⟩ = ρA + √1 − ρ2⟨v⊥, e⟩. Applying the above fact to u = v⊥/∥v⊥∥ gives ⟨v⊥, e⟩ = ∥v⊥∥Z. Let νℓ = ∥¯x(ℓ)∥2 be the squared norm of the code vector (a 1-bit prefix-truncated code keeps only mℓ coordinates of magnitude 1 /√D, so νℓ = mℓ/D ); since the orthogonal transformation preserves inner products, A = ⟨¯x(ℓ), P −1o⟩ = ⟨¯o(ℓ), o⟩. Hence ∥v⊥∥ = √νℓ − A2, and letting ζ = √D − 1 Z and dividing both sides by A yields ˆ ρ(ℓ) − ρ = √1 − ρ2 κ(ℓ)ζ(ℓ), i.e., Eq. (8). This argument holds as long as the code is a function only of ˜ x and of randomness independent of P , so it applies equally to 2-bit codes. Part (a) of Theorem 1 is shown by cases. If the residual is 0 or the normalization constant of the code is at most 0, the rule passes the vector, so the left-hand side is the empty set. If Φ = 0, the rule prunes a vector only when 

x > r , and since the distance is then x, a pruned vector lies outside the radius. If ρ = ±1, the estimate is exact, so a pruned vector has ρ < θ (r) − f κε 0 ≤ θ(r) (since ε0 ≥ 0), i.e., lies outside the radius. In the remaining case (|ρ| < 1), if a vector is pruned and lies within the radius, then ρ ≥ θ(r) together with Eq. (9) and Eq. (8) gives √1 − ρ2 κ ζ < θ (r) − ρ − f κε 0 ≤ − f κε 0. If κ = 0 or θ(r) ≥ 1, this is a contradiction; otherwise ζ < 0 (the right-hand side is at most 0 because ε0 ≥ 0) and √1 − ρ2 ≤ f (if θ ≤ 0, then √1 − ρ2 ≤ 1 ≤ f ; if θ > 0, then √1 − ρ2 ≤ √1 − θ2 ≤ f ). Therefore f ζ ≤ √1 − ρ2 ζ < −f ε 0, and ζ < −ε0. Equivalently, if ζ ≥ − ε0, then 

ρ = ˆ ρ − √1 − ρ2 κζ ≤ ˆρ + √1 − ρ2 κε 0 ≤ ˆρ + f κε 0, where the last step is ( f − √1 − ρ2) κε 0 ≥ 0 and uses ε0 ≥ 0. This assumption ( δℓ ≤ 1/2) is needed: if ε0 < 0, a vector on the boundary ( ρ = θ(r), κ > 0) with f > √1 − ρ2 is pruned whenever ζ < f |ε0|/√1 − ρ2, a threshold above −ε0 = |ε0|, so (a) and (b) fail. Part (b) follows from the facts that the conditional distribution of ζ is fixed and that ε0 is G-measurable, and part (c) from the union bound obtained by applying (a) at each stage. We first fix the treatment of ties. Therefore a failure is contained in the event that some unvisited true neighbor is pruned at some stage, and Theorem 1 and the union bound give Eq. (10). 56 Theorem 5 is proved as follows. Regarding re-testing, when the same vector is tested again with the same code at a smaller radius r′ ≤ r, θ(r) − f κε 0 is decreasing in r (θ decreases in r, the f of the implementation increases in r, and ε0 ≥ 0), so a vector pruned at r is also pruned at r′. The word “distinct” in condition (i) is necessary, because if the same vector enters the candidates twice, the k-th element of the multiset of distances can be smaller than dk.Theorem 3 follows from E[M ] ≤ | V |β ≤ kβ = δ(m + 1) and Markov’s inequality. When all that is known is that each neighbor fails with probability p, the coupling that selects one subset of size m + 1 with probability kp/ (m + 1) and makes only its elements fail attains this bound, so the bound cannot be improved using the marginal probabilities alone. Proposition 1 below shows that an actual data configuration also comes close to this bound. The tightness is meant only in this sense: an analysis that uses only the per-neighbor probabilities cannot give a smaller bound, and actual data attain the bound exactly only with k = m + 1 ( G = 1, m + 1 duplicates in one unvisited partition) and otherwise within a second-order term. It does not exclude a smaller failure probability for the algorithm on other data or a sharper analysis that uses the dependence among neighbors or stages, and it is separate from the cost lower bounds of Appendix B. Theorem 4 applies linearity of expectation to Recall ≥ 1 − M/k . Corollary 1 follows from the fact that the proof of Theorem 3 bounds E[M ] ≤ | V |β ≤ δ(m + 1) before applying Markov’s inequality. By Lemma 2, 

E[Recall] ≥ 1 − δ(m + 1) /k , so if this value is at least τ , i.e., if δ(m + 1) ≤ (1 − τ )k, then E[Recall] ≥ τ . The proofs of Theorems 5 and 6 reproduce this expectation-bound step as it is, so the corollary also holds under the adaptive radius and under updates. 

Proposition 1 (Tightness of Theorem 3): Suppose there is a single test stage ( L = 1), the per-vector value √1 − max( θ(r), 0) 2 is used as the slack factor, ρ > 0, k = G(m + 1) , and D ≥ max(2 G, 8) . In each of G unvisited partitions, place m + 1 identical vectors at distance r − η from the query, such that in partition g the unit vectors of the data and the query normalized with respect to the centroid are og and qg = ρ og + √1 − ρ2 eg , with the 2G

vectors og , eg orthonormal, and place k vectors at distance r in the visited partitions. Let p = GD (−ε0) (equal to the failure probability budget β, since there is a single stage). Then, as η → 0, the limit of Pr[Recall( q) < τ ] is at least 

Gp − (G

> 2

)λ∗p2, where λ∗ is a constant determined only by D and ε0. In particular, if G = 1 (k = m + 1 ), this limit equals the bound kp/ (m + 1) = p of Theorem 3. 

Such a configuration can be constructed, for example, by placing the query at the origin and the centroid of partition 

g at −Φ qg . The proof is as follows. Identical vectors in the same partition have the same o and q, and hence the same error ζ and the same test outcome; so if the event Dg that group g is pruned occurs, m + 1 true neighbors are missed at once and recall falls below τ . Hence the failure event contains ⋃ 

> g

Dg . As η → 0, the margin tends to 0, θ(r) → ρ,and the slack factor tends to √1 − ρ2, so Dg converges monotonically to {ζg < −ε0} and Pr[ Dg ] → p. If G = 1, this completes the proof. If G ≥ 2, the probability of the union is at least the sum of the probabilities of the individual events minus the sum of the probabilities of the pairwise intersections (Pr[ ⋃

Since og , eg , oh, eh are orthonormal, given P −1o of the two groups, the two error directions form an orthonormal pair drawn uniformly in the remaining D − 2 dimensions, and ζg and ζh are functions of the 2 × 2 block of this pair. Applying det( I − B⊤B) ≤ e−∥ B∥2 

> F

to the formula of Diaconis, Eaton, and Lauritzen for the block density of a uniform orthogonal matrix (summarized in Jiang [96]), the density of this block is at most κJ times the density of a Gaussian block whose entries are independent with variance 1 /(D − 7). In the Gaussian block the two errors are independent and each has tail probability at most ¯Φ( ε0

√(D − 7) /(D − 1)) (where ¯Φ is the tail probability of the standard normal distribution), so one may take λ∗ = κJ [ ¯Φ( ε0

√(D − 7) /(D − 1)) /p ]2. κJ is determined by the normalizing constant of that density formula and equals 1.042, 1.020, and 1.007 for D = 128 , 256 , 768. In a Monte Carlo experiment ( k = 4, m = 1, G = 2), the failure rate ÷ the bound of Theorem 3 was 1.006 at D = 128 and 0.990 at D = 768, i.e., 1 within sampling error. 57 A.2 Proof of the Dynamic Guarantee 

In part (a) of Theorem 6, the update sequence is given, and by C5 the state at time t (live vectors, centroids, assignments, segments) is independent of the rotation; since the rotation was drawn independently of this state, it is Haar conditionally on Gt. The visited set and the error-bound parameters are functions of exact distances and residual norms and are therefore Gt-measurable, and by C7 the radius is the k-th of the exact distances of distinct live vectors, so it satisfies condition (i) of Theorem 5 on Lt. Unvisited true neighbors are live, so by C7 they are not caught by the bitmap and are processed by the test path of their own segment, and by C6 the premise of Lemma 1 holds. The pruning event of each vector is defined by the code of its own segment and the current centroid, so the proof of Theorem 5 is repeated verbatim on Lt. The terms of the union and of Markov’s inequality are only the k vectors of N ∗

> k,t

(q), so the failure probability δ need not be split. In (b), ζ is a function only of ( o, q, P ), so if the centroid and the deterministic slack are unchanged between two times and rt ≤ rs, then θ(r) − f κε 0 at time t is greater than or equal to that at time 

s (ε0 ≥ 0 is constant, θ decreases in r, and f increases in r). Hence a neighbor pruned at time s is also pruned at time 

t. In (c), the rotation schedule is part of the update sequence and a fresh rotation is independent of the past, so it is Haar conditionally on the past, and events at two times that use different rotations are conditionally independent. 

# A.3 Pass-Rate Formula and Soundness of the Implementation 

Substituting Eq. (13) into Eq. (12), when the numerator is nonnegative, √1 − ρ2 ≤ 1 gives, for fixed f and σdet , a decreasing upper bound that depends only on the margin. The soundness of the implementation reduces to the argument of Section 4.2. If the accumulated value used in the test is at least the exact value, and the approximation of the right-hand side together with the deterministic slack is at most the exact right-hand side for every rotation, then every vector pruned by the implementation is also pruned by the exact rule. The slack may depend on the rotation. This argument applies to the 8-bit quantization of nibble LUTs, the LUTs and accumulation of later stages, the reconstruction of 8-bit metadata (minimum rounded down, step rounded up), the fp32 error of per-vector slack factors, the fp32 addition in the 2-bit final stage, passing all vectors when the radius is infinite, and passing vectors with zero residual without testing. The implementation used in the experiments also covers, in the same way, the errors of the query rotation, of the distance Φ to the centroid, of the exact distances entering the radius, and of the floating-point computation of the right-hand side. For the rotation and data-side values, the values computed in fp64 at index construction serve as the reference. The query rotation is computed with an fp32 matrix and fp64 accumulation, and per-coordinate bounds determined from the fp32 rounding of the matrix and the accumulation error are subtracted from the query term of the test. For Φ, an upper bound on its relative error is added to the slack, and partitions for which this bound exceeds 4 × 10 −6 are recomputed with fp64 centroids. Distances entering the radius are enlarged by an upper bound on their fp32 computation error. The remaining premises are these fp64 reference computations and the given fp32 query coordinates. The time added by this treatment was within 2% in geometric mean on six 1M datasets for 1-thread and 32-thread latency and for batch throughput alike (up to 17% at 32 threads). 

# Appendix B. Lower Bounds for Per-Query Guarantees 

This appendix proves three lower bounds on the cost of per-query guarantees. Theorems 7 and 8 are lower bounds within the probe model below, and Theorem 9 is a lower bound in the word-RAM model under SETH. Throughout, 

τ ∈ (0 , 1], δ ∈ [0 , 1), and m = ⌊(1 − τ )k⌋. Since τ > 0, we have m + 1 ≤ k, and, as in Section 2.2, missing m + 1 or more true neighbors implies Recall( q) ≤ (k − m − 1) /k < τ .

Probe model. Consider a probe model in which the algorithm knows the public information (the query, the candidates, and the public structure of the index) at no cost, and learns the content of a hidden cell only by probing that cell. The cost is the number of probed cells. Precisely, an instance consists of public information J and the contents y1, . . . , y n′

of n′ hidden cells, and J contains the query and the vectors and ids of the candidate set C. The algorithm uses internal randomness ω that is independent of the instance (the orthogonal transformation P for the index of the main text); the record of cell i is a function of yi and ω only, and reading this record is a probe of cell i. The next cell to probe may be chosen adaptively from J, ω, and the records read so far. The output consists of k items chosen from C and the probed cells; a cell that is output without having been probed is counted as probed. Let Q be the set of probed hidden cells and T = |Q|. The IVF index of the main text belongs to this model as long as its public structure (centroids, assignments, residual norms) is fixed independently of the contents of the hidden cells; the hidden content is then the direction of the residual, and the record is its code. Structures whose access paths change with the contents, such as graphs, LSH, and trees, lie outside this model. 

Hard family of instances. Fix the public information J, and let C consist of k vectors whose distances from the 58 query are distinct and lie in [1 , 2]. For each cell, fix a near content at distance less than 1 /2 from the query and a far content at distance greater than 3, with distinct distances for the near contents of different cells. For a set of cells 

B ⊆ { 1, . . . , n ′}, let IB be the instance in which the cells in B have their near contents and the other cells their far contents. All IB share the same J, and in I∅ the candidates are the correct answer. If |B| ≤ k, all distances are distinct, so the true k-NN set is uniquely B together with the k − | B| nearest vectors of C. Since the output lies in 

C ∪ Q, we have k(1 − Recall( q)) ≥ | B \ Q|. This family can be realized within an IVF index. For example, for a vector with residual norm 2 in a partition whose centroid is at distance 2 from the query, changing only the direction of the residual, with the centroid and the residual norm unchanged, makes its distance to the query any value between 0 and 4. 

Coupling. Fix ω, and let Qω be the set of cells probed by the run on I∅ and T∅ = |Qω |. If Qω ∩ B = ∅, the run on IB is identical to the run on I∅. Indeed, the two instances differ only in the cells of B, so two runs that start from the same J and ω read the same records and choose the same cells until a cell of B is probed for the first time (by induction on the number of probes); since the run on I∅ probes no cell of B, the two runs coincide to the end and have the same output. Hence, in this case, the output on IB contains no cell of B, and k(1 − Recall( q)) ≥ | B| if |B| ≤ k.

Lemma 3 (Hypergeometric Avoidance Probability): For integers N ≥ 1 and 1 ≤ u ≤ N , the probability that u cells chosen uniformly at random among N cells all avoid a fixed set of t cells is, for t = 0 , 1, . . . , N ,

Theorem 7 (Cost Lower Bound in the Probe Model): In the probe model, let n′ ≥ m+1 , and suppose that an algorithm gives the per-query (τ, δ )-recall guarantee Pr ω [Recall( q) < τ ] ≤ δ on IB for every B of size m + 1 . Then, even on the instance I∅ in which the candidates are the correct answer, the expected number of probes satisfies Hg n′ (m+1 , E T∅) ≤ δ,and hence E T∅ ≥ (n′ − m)(1 − δ1/(m+1) ).Proof. We apply Yao’s principle [97] to an adversarial distribution that chooses the defect configuration at random: draw B uniformly from the sets of m + 1 cells, independently of ω. Since an average is at most the maximum, 

δ ≥ EB Pr ω [Recall( q) < τ on IB ]. By the coupling, the failure event on IB contains {Qω ∩ B = ∅}. Given ω, Qω is a fixed set of size T∅, so Pr B [Qω ∩ B = ∅] = Hg n′ (m + 1 , T ∅). Exchanging the order of averaging and applying Jensen’s inequality with the convexity in Lemma 3, 

δ ≥ Eω

[Hg n′ (m + 1 , T ∅)] ≥ Hg n′ (m + 1 , E T∅).

If E T∅ ≥ n′−m, the final inequality is trivial. Otherwise, the lower bound of Lemma 3 gives (1−E T∅/(n′−m))m+1 ≤ δ,which yields the claim. ■

For example, for k = 100, τ = 0 .9, and δ = 0 .01, we have m = 10 and 1 − 0.01 1/11 = 0 .3421, so even on the instance in which the candidates are correct, E T∅ ≥ 0.3421( n′ − 10). That is, when there are many hidden cells, about 34.2% of them must be probed (the fraction is at least 0.342 once n′ ≥ 51 ,227). Thus, in this model, τ , δ, and k enter the cost as the probed fraction 1 − δ1/(m+1) = 1 − e− ln(1 /δ )/(m+1) , not through the logarithm in the error-bound parameter. On this family, the condition of Theorem 7 cannot be improved: an algorithm that probes t cells chosen uniformly at random, with Hg n′ (m + 1 , t ) ≤ δ, and outputs the k nearest among C and the probed cells fails on IB with |B| = m + 1 with probability exactly Hg n′ (m + 1 , t ), because once it probes any cell of B, all probed cells of B enter the output and at most m neighbors are missed. 

Theorem 8 (Deterministic Skipping): Under the assumptions of Theorem 7, for every set Z of m+1 cells, Pr ω [Qω ∩Z̸ =

∅] ≥ 1 − δ on I∅. In particular, a deterministic algorithm can skip at most m hidden cells, even on the instance I∅ in which the candidates are correct. Proof. Let B = Z. By the coupling, if Qω ∩ Z = ∅, then m + 1 or more neighbors are missed on IZ and Recall( q) < τ ,59 so Pr ω [Qω ∩ Z = ∅] ≤ δ. For a deterministic algorithm, Qω is a single fixed set; if m + 1 or more hidden cells were left unprobed, taking m + 1 of them as Z would give Pr ω [Qω ∩ Z = ∅] = 1 > δ , a contradiction. ■

If m = 0 (e.g., τ = 1), a deterministic rule must probe all hidden cells. Hence the statement “certification without assumptions must read all vectors” is true only for deterministic rules. A randomized rule only needs to hit every set of m + 1 cells with probability at least 1 − δ, and the cost of doing so is the lower bound of Theorem 7. 

Theorem 9 (Lower Bound Conditional on SETH): Fix k ≥ 1, τ ∈ (0 , 1] , and δ ∈ [0 , 1) , and assume randomized SETH [77], [79]. Then for every c ≥ 1 and ε ∈ (0 , 1] there is a constant γ, determined by c and ε alone, such that for any dimension D(n) (a function computable in time polynomial in n) with γ log 2 n ≤ D(n) ≤ nc for all sufficiently large n, there is no word-RAM data structure that preprocesses n vectors with distinct ids (duplicates of the same vector allowed) in time O(nc), returns k ids per query in time O(n1−ε), and, without assumptions on the data or queries, gives for every query (i) the (τ, δ )-recall guarantee Pr[Recall( q) < τ ] ≤ δ or (ii) the expected-recall guarantee 

E[Recall( q)] ≥ τ . Here the probabilities are over the internal randomness of the data structure, which is independent of the data and queries. Consequently, when D = ω(log n) and D is at most polynomial in n, no data structure with polynomial-time preprocessing that gives such guarantees can achieve query time O(n1−ε).

Randomized SETH is the following hypothesis: for every ε′ > 0 there is an integer w such that no randomized algorithm (with error probability at most 1/3) decides the satisfiability of w-CNF formulas with nv variables in time 

O(2 (1 −ε′)nv ) [77], [79]. The proof also uses the sparsification lemma [78]: for every w and ε′′ > 0 there is a constant K

such that a w-CNF φ with nv variables can be written, in time 2 ε′′ nv poly( nv ), as a disjunction of w-CNFs ψ1, . . . , ψ s

(s ≤ 2ε′′ nv ) over the same variables, each with at most Kn v clauses. The proof is the split-and-list reduction to the orthogonal vectors problem, whose data-structure version is given in [81]. 

Proof of Theorem 9. Let c′ = c + 1, α = 1 /(2 c′), and ε3 = εα . Apply SETH with ε′ = ε3/4 to obtain w, apply the sparsification lemma with ( w, ε 3/2) to obtain K, and set γ = 6 c′K. Suppose such a data structure A exists. We decide the satisfiability of a w-CNF φ as follows. 1. Using the sparsification lemma, rewrite φ as ψ1, . . . , ψ s. The formula φ is satisfiable if and only if some ψi is. If some ψi has no clauses, answer “satisfiable” immediately. 2. For ψ = ψi with μ clauses (1 ≤ μ ≤ Kn v ), split its variables into a set V1 of n1 = ⌈αn v ⌉ variables and the remaining set V2. For each assignment a of V1, define a ∈ { 0, 1}μ by aj = 1 if no V1-literal of clause j is true under a, and aj = 0 otherwise. Define b in the same way for each assignment b of V2. Clause j is satisfied by (a, b ) if and only if aj bj = 0, so ψ is satisfiable if and only if there is a pair with ⟨a, b⟩ = 0. 3. Per coordinate, let G(0) = (1 , 0, 0), G(1) = (0 , 1, 0), H(0) = (1 , 1, 0), and H(1) = (1 , 0, 1). Then ∥G(aj ) −

H(bj )∥2 = 1 + 2 aj bj , so the coordinatewise concatenations G(a), H (b) ∈ { 0, 1}3μ satisfy ∥G(a) − H(b)∥2 =

μ + 2 ⟨a, b⟩. That is, an orthogonal pair has the smallest possible distance √μ, and every other pair has distance at least √μ + 2. Padding with zero coordinates to dimension D(n) does not change distances. 4. The data consist of n = k2n1 vectors, k copies of each G(a) with distinct ids, and the queries are the 2 nv −n1

vectors H(b). Since n ≥ 2αn v , we have 3 μ ≤ 3Kn v ≤ (3 K/α ) log 2 n = γ log 2 n ≤ D(n). If some a is orthogonal to 

b, there are at least k vectors at distance √μ, so dk = √μ and every valid true k-NN set, obtained by exchanging vectors at equal distance, consists only of vectors at distance √μ. Hence, if Recall( q) > 0, the output contains a vector of an orthogonal pair. We check the k outputs directly in O(kμ ) time and answer “satisfiable” only when an orthogonal pair is found, so there are no false positives. 5. Build r copies of A with mutually independent internal randomness and issue all queries to each. For a query that has an orthogonal pair, the probability that one data structure misses the pair is at most δ in case (i), and at most 1 − τ in case (ii), because Recall( q) ≤ 1 gives Pr[Recall( q) > 0] ≥ E[Recall( q)] ≥ τ . The queries are determined by φ in advance and are independent of the internal randomness, so the per-query guarantee applies as is, and the r outcomes are independent. With ρ = max( δ, 1 − τ ) < 1 and r = ⌈ln 3 / ln(1 /ρ )⌉ (r = 1 if ρ = 0), the algorithm answers “satisfiable” with probability at least 1 −ρr ≥ 2/3 when φ is satisfiable, and always answers “unsatisfiable” otherwise. Here r is a constant depending only on τ and δ.6. We count the time. For one ψ, building the vectors takes (2 n1 + 2 nv −n1 )poly( nv ), and writing the zero-padded input and building the r data structures takes O(r(nD (n) + nc)) = O(nc′

) = O(kc′

2c′(αn v +1) ) = O(2 nv /2). Each query vector is formed in O(μ) time by overwriting the first 3 μ coordinates of a zero-filled buffer, and the query phase takes r2nv −n1

(O(n1−ε) + O(kμ )) ≤ 2nv −εn 1 poly( nv ) ≤ 2(1 −ε3)nv poly( nv ) (using n1−ε ≤ k2(1 −ε)n1 ). Since 

ε3 ≤ 1/4, one ψ takes 2 (1 −ε3)nv poly( nv ); adding up the s ≤ 2ε3nv /2 formulas and the sparsification time, the total is 2 (1 −ε3/2) nv poly( nv ) = O(2 (1 −ε′)nv ). This is a randomized algorithm with one-sided error that solves w-CNF satisfiability in time O(2 (1 −ε′)nv ), contradicting randomized SETH. The last sentence of the theorem follows by taking c larger than both the exponent of the prepro-cessing time and the degree of D(n), so that the preprocessing is O(nc) and D(n) ≤ nc, and noting that D(n) = ω(log n)implies D(n) ≥ γ log 2 n for all sufficiently large n. ■

60 Because the distances of the k outputs can be checked in O(kD ) time, there are no false positives, so Theorem 9 holds even when δ is close to 1. This conclusion is in the word-RAM model, so unlike Theorems 7 and 8 it covers graphs, LSH, and trees alike. It is, however, an asymptotic statement about worst-case instances of binary vectors with 

D = Ω(log n), and it says nothing about whether sublinear cost is possible on real data. Lower bounds of the same form are known for approximate nearest neighbor search [80]; what we add is only the k-fold replication (step 4) and the one-sided amplification (step 5) that carry them over to recall guarantees. 

# Appendix C. Supplementary Experiments and Discussion 

# C.1 Comparison with Other Baselines 

Our own GEMM baseline, an exhaustive search based on matrix multiplication that we optimized ourselves, was 1.5–8.4 × faster than FAISS IndexFlatL2 (default setting) in batch. Against this baseline, our method was 1.03–37 ×

faster in batch in all settings except laion ( k = 100, T1) (the smallest margin was 1.03 × on SSNPP ( k = 100, T1)), and on laion ( k = 100, T1) this baseline was 1.2 × faster. In the main text we excluded this baseline, following the principle of using well-known public software with standard settings. For the comparison with GPUs we used GpuIndexFlatL2 of FAISS [8] (RTX 4090) (GPU times were measured in early October 2026). In single-query latency, our method with 32 threads was faster than the GPU in all 44 settings in which the GPU was measured (eight 1M datasets and three 10M datasets) (3.5–31.7 × at 1M scale and at least 7.4 × at 10M scale). With 1 thread, our latency was longer than the GPU’s in four exact-search settings: SSNPP and SSNPP-10M ( k = 100) and laion ( k = 10 , 100). Batch throughput was higher on the GPU when the data fit in GPU memory. The GPU led in 31 of the 32 settings at 1M scale, and the throughput ratio of our method to the GPU had a geometric mean of 0.44 and a minimum of 0.11. In contrast, on msmarco (fp32, 36 GB), which exceeds GPU memory, our method was 5.4–10.3 × faster in batch. Since the 1-bit codes take only about 1.6 GB even for 100M vectors, we leave a GPU implementation of our method to future work.

# C.3 Per-Neighbor Guarantees and PUFFINN 

PUFFINN [45] solves with an LSH forest the ( k, δ )-NN problem, which asks to find each true k-nearest neighbor with probability at least 1 − δ. It scans multiple LSH tries starting from the longest hash matches and stops after examining ln(1 /δ )/p i tries ( i is the length of the matching hash), a number determined by the collision probability p

between the query and the current k-th candidate; by the monotonicity that the collision probability of the true k-th neighbor is at least p, it shows that the probability of missing each neighbor is at most δ. This is a guarantee that each query’s expected recall is at least 1 − δ, of the same form as our expected-recall guarantee, but since it is a per-neighbor probability, the probability of finding all k neighbors is only at least 1 −kδ by the union bound. Still, since the expected number M of missed neighbors is at most kδ , Markov’s inequality gives the tail bound Pr[ M ≥ m + 1] ≤ kδ/ (m + 1), which equals the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee for a per-neighbor probability of δ(m+1) /k . The implementation supports only cosine and Jaccard similarity, filters candidates before exact distance computation with a sketch filter whose threshold is placed at the expected value, selects hash functions from a shared pool, and approximates collision probabilities by a Monte Carlo table. The proof does not cover these steps. Insertions require a rebuild, deletions are not supported, and the original paper verified the guarantee by average recall. Our guarantee subsumes this problem as follows. 

Corollary 3 (Per-Neighbor Guarantee): In each mode of Theorems 2–4 (and likewise with the adaptive radius of Theorem 5), for every query and every true k-nearest neighbor v of it, the probability that v is missed is at most β.Hence the expected-recall guarantee mode ( β = 1 − τ ) solves the (k, δ P)-NN problem of PUFFINN [45], which asks to find each true neighbor with probability at least 1 − δP, with δP = 1 − τ (δP is PUFFINN’s per-neighbor failure probability). The exact-search guarantee ( β = δ/k ) solves the same problem with δ/k and in addition ensures that all 

k neighbors are found with probability at least 1 − δ, and the (τ, δ )-recall guarantee ( β = δ(m + 1) /k ) solves the same problem with δ(m + 1) /k while further bounding the tail of the number of missed neighbors. 

Corollary 3 follows directly from Theorem 1. True neighbors in visited partitions are tested with exact distances and are therefore never missed. An unvisited true neighbor, under the condition that the test radius is at least the true k-th distance (C2 or Theorem 5), is pruned at each test stage ℓ with probability at most GD (−ε0,ℓ ), and the error-bound parameters are set so that the sum of these probabilities is at most β (Theorem 1(c)); hence, by the union bound, it is missed with probability at most β. What the exact-search guarantee and the ( τ, δ )-recall guarantee add is given by Theorem 2 and Theorem 3, respectively. The per-neighbor guarantee alone only ensures, by the union bound, that all 

k neighbors are found with probability at least 1 − kβ .We also verified the per-neighbor bound experimentally. On the eight 1M datasets and imagenet-align with k ∈{10 , 100 }, we measured the miss frequency of each (query, true neighbor) pair over 20 independent rotations not shared 62 with the other audits and three processing paths (batch, 32-thread, and 1-thread). Under the expected-recall guarantee (τ = 0 .9, β = 0 .1), the miss rate pooled over all pairs was 0.0002–0.017, and 52–90 pairs per path had a Clopper–Pearson 95% lower bound exceeding β. Because the pairs are very numerous, such pairs also arise by chance, and their number falls far short of the number expected by chance if the true probability of every pair were exactly β (37,994), consistent with the per-neighbor bound. The minimum per-query mean recall was 0.935. For the (0 .9, 0.01)-recall guarantee and the exact-search guarantee, whose β is small, pair-level tests lack resolution, so we examined the pooled rate instead, which was at most 9% and 7% of β, respectively. We also compared with the public implementation of PUFFINN (commit fd86b0d) (Table 35). The datasets are yandex, laion, and arxiv, which are normalized to unit length so that L2 and cosine rankings coincide; k = 10, and the target recalls are 0.9 and 0.95 ( δ = 0 .1, 0.05). Following the GloVe 1M 4 GB setting of the original paper, we set the index size to 381 hash tables, and we rebuilt the index 10 times with different hash seeds (5 times for the version with the sketch filter disabled). For hash functions we used independent hashing, the default of the Python interface, a choice closer to the assumptions of the proof than the shared pool used in the experiments of the original paper. The officially recommended default setting (with the sketch filter) fell short of the target even in average recall; the per-neighbor miss rate was 1.2–4.1 × δ, and 15–93% of queries had a repetition-averaged recall significantly below 1 − δ. Disabling the sketch filter brought the per-neighbor miss rate within δ but made queries 4–9 × slower, and at δ = 0 .05, 4–11% of queries were still significantly below the target. The shortfalls of the filter-disabled version occurred almost entirely at the k-th neighbor, in cases where the relative distance gap to the ( k + 1)-th neighbor was 10 −4–10 −3, i.e., the order was swapped in 16-bit fixed-point distances. Our method, configured for the expected-recall guarantee at the same level (τ = 1 − δ), had no query significantly below the target and was 3.1–17.5 × faster in 1-thread latency than the default setting and 27–76 × faster than the filter-disabled version. PUFFINN processes one query with one thread and has no interface for processing multiple queries together. Results were measured with the same tie handling as in the other experiments (Section 6.1). The build time of PUFFINN was 31, 60, and 149 s on yandex, laion, and arxiv, respectively (ours: 11.3 s on laion and 27.3 s on arxiv — Table 23).

Comparison of PUFFINN with our method configured for the expected-recall guarantee at the same level ( τ = 1 − δ) ( k = 10 ; PUFFINN indexes were built 10 times for the default and 5 times with the filter disabled). Queries below target are those whose repetition-averaged recall is significantly lower than the target.

# C.4 Dimension and Relative Cost 

The dimension experiment of Section 6.6 was performed on several versions of the same data reduced by PCA to dimensions ranging from 128 up to the original dimension (GIST 960, arxiv 768, and a 1M subset of msmarco 1024; 

k = 10, one rotation; a run separate from Table 33). In batch, the speedup over FAISS IndexFlatL2 decreased from 37.9–56.1 × at 128 dimensions to 29.2–42.4 × at the original dimension, because the per-dimension efficiency of FAISS’s matrix multiplication improves with dimension. With the first-stage code length set equal to the dimension, the test error alone reduced the pass rate by 9–80 ×, but the first-stage cost also grew in proportion to dimension, so the 1-thread speedup was 21.5–24.4 ×, independent of dimension. Hence the growth of the gain with dimension is a property that holds when the first-stage code length is fixed. 

# C.5 Unsuccessful Attempts, Design History, and Settings 

Several attempts to reduce the cost of testing unvisited vectors were ineffective. Examples include methods that shrink the radius or build partitions with uniform radii, deterministic certification using PQ [12] centroids, enlarging the set of visited partitions, pruning with partition summaries (spheres, boxes, PCA, and cones), the pigeonhole principle based on subspace partitioning [98], [99], prediction of the maximum cosine in the query direction, and residual whitening. Subspace partitioning produced few candidates, but finding those candidates cost as much as exhaustive search, and 63 even when the maximum cosine in the query direction was known by an oracle, the surviving vectors decreased by only 27–58%. The common cause is that the maximum cosine in the query direction, the information that separates the answers, exists only at the level of individual vectors, so partition summaries cannot provide a deterministic upper bound. Methods that introduce a data model and prune at the partition level could filter 65–89% of the vectors on text data without reading them, but the surviving vectors that dominate the cost are concentrated in a few partitions near the query, so these decreased by only 1–11%. The throughput gain was 0–20%, and guarantee violations of up to 1.7% occurred. These negative results are the grounds for our choice of an assumption-free per-query guarantee. We disclose the following regarding the measurements. Early exact computation and estimate-ordered processing, which assist the adaptive radius, their enabling rule, global centering, m1 = D, and the cost model of the parameter selection rule were designed or revised after seeing the first measurements on laion, SSNPP, and yandex. Early exact computation selects the 2 k + 20 vectors with the smallest distances in the first-stage estimates of the first 64 or 192 partitions in Φ order for 1-thread, and of the first 64 or 128 partitions in partition order for batch. The 1-thread scan visits the first 132 partitions in Φ order first and the rest in their stored order. The enabling rule times the candidate paths over 3 rounds on calibration queries (200 for latency and 1,000 for batch) and chooses the simpler option (off) if its median is within 1.05 × of the fastest candidate; this threshold was initially set to 1.02 × and raised after we observed settings in which the calibration and test results disagreed. Estimate-ordered processing is a candidate only for 32-thread latency, and a variant that removes synchronization barriers in batch was not used because it gave no gain in 32-thread batch. The batch thread count of 32 was chosen by measuring the batch throughput of our method and FAISS IndexFlatL2 with 24–64 threads on laion ( k = 100), SIFT ( k = 10), and SSNPP ( k = 100) and taking the value that maximizes the minimum, over all (dataset, method) pairs, of the ratio to each pair’s own peak throughput (0.94; FAISS was 6% faster with 63–64 threads on SIFT and SSNPP, our method peaked at 32 threads or was within 2% of its peak there, and slow runs increased sharply at 60 or more threads). In addition, the calibration data used to set the machine constants of the rule overlap with the evaluation data. The evidence for generalization that does not overlap with calibration comes from arxiv, the three 10M datasets, imagenet-align, and DEEP1M and GloVe, which were added after the rule was fixed. The oracle nprobe of IVF and the oracles of some configurations were chosen with the test queries. Batch measurements varied by ±5–10% between runs, and FAISS at 10M scale was measured in the first round only. Multi-bit codes for prefix stages, a dual first-stage index with 128 and 256 bits, block skipping, block sharing among multiple queries in batch, and a prefilter on a 4-bit copy were tested at the implementation level but gave no gain (0.88–1.03 ×), because the first stage is compute-bound and different queries make few visits per partition, leaving nothing to share. To the gains of split and dissolution in policy K, we add a term equal to the change in the number of partitions multiplied by the fixed cost of testing one partition (a machine constant set to 73 ns if the first-stage data fit in the L3 cache and 397 ns otherwise) and by the probability that the partition becomes a test target at the initial radius of the calibration queries. Unlike the official evaluation, the streaming measurements used 1,000 queries, fp64 ground truth, and zero-padding to 112 dimensions, with no time limit, and IVF was trained on the first batch and never retrained. In one instance, the first attempt at the streaming measurement violated C5 by measuring the constants of the maintenance policy with the rotation used in the measurement. Those results were discarded and the measurement was repeated, and the code now rejects such use. 

# C.6 An Empirical Formula for Relative Radius and Pass Rate 

Summarizing the inputs of Eq. (12) in a single variable yields the relative radius Rvis = rvis /√xΦ, the initial radius (C2; hereafter rvis ) divided by the residual scale. For each query, x and Φ are taken as medians over the unvisited vectors within the triangle-inequality range, and the value for a dataset is the median over queries. This variable shares the idea of relative contrast [92] but differs in that its denominator is the residual scale and its numerator is the test radius actually used in search. Regressing the first-stage pass rate p1 on Rvis over the 26 combinations of 13 datasets and k ∈ { 10 , 100 } gives logit p1 ≈ − 17 .3 + 14 .4 Rvis (R2 = 0 .908) (17) On the 12 settings measured at the initial radius with the adaptive radius disabled, R2 = 0 .95. In contrast, the residual norm x divided by the mean query–data distance alone gave R2 of at most 0.08. Hence what determines the cost difference between datasets is not the residual norm itself but the relative distance of the neighbors with respect to the residual scale. This formula is a summary across datasets, and its direction can be reversed between partitions within the same dataset or between time points before and after updates. For example, the dynamic index without maintenance (Section 5) had a smaller Rvis than the maintained index (0.88 vs. 1.01) but a higher pass rate (0.018 vs. 0.011). When centroids drift away, x and Φ grow together so that Rvis decreases, but the margin in Eq. (13) also shrinks and more vectors outside the radius pass. Therefore, when comparing time points or deciding on maintenance, we use Eq. (12) directly (Section 5.3). 64 C.7 Details of the Optimization Techniques 

The operation and implementation of the optimization techniques of Section 4.3 are as follows. Early exact computation scans some partitions at the front in Φ order using only first-stage codes and computes in advance the exact distances of a few vectors with the smallest estimated distances. This first-stage reading serves to select vectors and is not a test; after the radius is reduced with the selected vectors, all unvisited partitions, including those scanned, are tested as usual with the reduced radius. The selected vectors enter the candidates with their exact distances, and if they pass the test again during the scan, they are filtered by id so that they are not added to the candidates twice (Section 4.2). Since batch queries are processed grouped by partition, early exact computation is also done in partition order. Estimate-ordered processing is used when one query is processed by multiple threads: among the vectors that passed the first stage in a partition, a few with small estimated distances are first processed through the later stages and their exact distances to reduce the radius, and the remaining passing vectors are then processed with the reduced radius. If too few vectors are computed first, the radius does not come down enough; if too many, exact distance computations increase. The first-stage code length m1 is chosen from m1 ∈ { 128 , 256 , D }. On data with a low first-stage pass rate, a short code discards most vectors, whereas on data with a high pass rate, reading the entire code at once reduces the cost of the later stages. The 2-bit final stage adds one more test stage after the last 1-bit stage with mℓ = D. The 2-bit code quantizes each coordinate of P −1o on a 4-level uniform grid symmetric about 0, and, as in Extended RaBitQ [72], the grid scale is determined from that vector alone so as to maximize the cosine between the code vector and P −1o. The error scale is the value of Eq. (7) with mℓ/D replaced by the squared norm of the code vector, typically about half that of the 1-bit code (0 .365 /√D vs. 0 .755 /√D). This stage reduces the number of vectors whose exact distances are computed from the original vectors, but it uses an additional 16 + D/ 4 bytes per vector, so it pays off only when the fraction of vectors passing the final 1-bit stage is large. Global centering reduces the deterministic slack. The slack of 8-bit LUT quantization is proportional to the norm of the query vector, so on data whose vector mean lies far from the origin, such as data with integer coordinates in 0–255 and no mean subtraction, ∥qr ∥/Φ is much larger than 1 and the deterministic slack σdet grows large enough to reach the error scale κ(1) . Building the LUT from P −1(qr − c0), with the mean c0 of the whole data subtracted, and subtracting the contribution of P −1(c − c0) from the per-vector coefficients leaves the test values unchanged and reduces only the slack. The number of front partitions, the number of vectors computed first, the pass-rate threshold, and the number of queries used for calibration change only the cost; their values are given in Appendix C.5. 

# C.8 Comparison of Partitioning Methods 

The comparison of the ten partitioning methods summarized in Section 6.5 is as follows. At the same radius, k-means [83] and its variants k-means++ [100], hierarchical k-means [101], spherical k-means [102], and anisotropic quantization [13] differed little, at 0.95–1.03 × of balanced k-means (except spherical k-means on GIST, at 1.12–1.21 ×), and within the same dataset the order of pass rates was determined by the relative residual size (rank correlation 0.88–0.96 among the ten methods). The picture changes, however, when each method uses the radius it obtains itself. On 1M datasets with in-distribution queries (text, GIST, and SIFT), with the radius fixed by the visited vectors, a global centroid was 4.8–18.3 × slower and the random projection tree [94] 1.6–5.5 × slower, because poor centroids make the visited partitions contain fewer neighbors and thus worsen the radius as well. Shrinking the radius with the adaptive radius while scanning the unvisited region reduced this loss to 1.3–2.5 × for the global centroid and 1.2–1.6 × for the random projection tree, and the same trend appeared at 10M scale (from 2.8–25.5 × to 1.18–1.95 ×). These results support the explanation in Section 3.3 that partitions play two roles, providing centroids and acquiring the radius. On laion and SSNPP, however, where the visited partitions contain few true neighbors, the difference between the two partitionings was small (1.0–1.7 × with a fixed radius and 1.1–1.6 × with the adaptive radius). Graph partitioning, which splits the k-NN graph with METIS [103] to gather neighbors into the same partition, and subdividing only partitions with large residuals gave no gain. 

# C.9 Effects of the Techniques and Data Characteristics 

On which datasets the techniques of Section 4.3 are enabled is determined by which term of Eq. (12) is large on each dataset. In terms of cost, our datasets fall into three groups. Data with in-distribution queries and low intrinsic dimension (SIFT, YFCC, arxiv, agnews, GIST, and msmarco) have a relative radius Rvis of 0.81–0.97 and an initial radius within 1.03 × of the true k-th distance, so the first stage already filters out almost everything, and little remains to be gained by adjusting the radius, bits, or slack. Data with OOD queries (laion, yandex, hotpotqa, and imagenet-align) have an Rvis of 1.16–1.54; the partitions visited in Φ order contain few true neighbors, so the visited radius 65 exceeds the true k-th distance (1.07 × on laion), and many vectors pass the first stage. Integer data with high intrinsic dimension and coordinates far from the origin (SSNPP) have an Rvis of 1.22–1.24 and a large LUT quantization slack. Setting k = 100 raises Rvis on all datasets, shifting them toward the second group. Table 37 summarizes, for each technique, the term it targets, the condition for enabling it, and its effect in the settings where the rule enabled it in our measurements. Because the defaults are set without regard to the guarantee level, m1 = 256, which benefits exact search on text and GIST at k = 100, increased the 1-thread time by 1.07–1.34 × under the expected-recall guarantee. This is because the error-bound parameters of the expected-recall guarantee are small, so the pass rate is already low with 128 bits; choosing m1 per mode resolves this.
